## One · A Hundred and Eleven Miles

The second letter came on the eleventh of October, and there was money in it.

I knew before I opened it. A letter that has been through the post twice goes soft at one corner, where the first direction was scraped away. This one had been scraped. The scrape had taken the top of the paper with it. Under my thumb there was the corner of a note folded twice. Two pounds. Nobody puts two pounds inside a letter unless they have already decided you will not take it as a fee.

The first letter had come in May.

I answered it the same day, with my coat still on, standing at the table, which is how I do anything I have decided to do before I have thought of a reason not to. I wrote three words. *Bring it.*

There was no direction on the letter. There was a postmark and no direction, and I got the book of post towns down from the chest and found the place in it, and I wrote to the postmaster there and asked him to put my letter into the way of a woman who could not bring herself to put her own county on the front of a letter.

That is how you answer a letter with no address on it. You do not send it to a person. You send it to a room where a person might come in.

Not because I am short with strangers. Because a question is a thing that has to be carried to the table by the person it belongs to, and a woman who answers a question nobody asked her is a woman answering her own. I have that written down somewhere. I have most things written down somewhere.

Then nothing. May, and June, and a summer of nothing, and the office open at seven and shut at four, and Mrs. Trewin putting the cloth on the table at four and taking it off again at seven the next morning for eleven months without once asking me why I do it.

Nine people in September. Eleven in October. A woman from Wendon who wanted to know whether her sister had been frightened, and had not been. A man who wanted to know where his brother had put a will, which is a question about paper and not about a person, and I sent him to the lawyer at Carrow. A boy of nineteen who came about his father and could not say what he had come about, and stood in the door for a quarter of an hour and then went away, and came back the next day and asked it properly.

That is the trade. Half of it is standing in a door not saying it.

***

The woman came at three in the afternoon, which is the worst hour, because the light has gone off the yard and there is not enough of it left indoors to see a face by. She had a basket and no reason to be carrying one, and she stood on the step with both hands around the handle and said, "Mrs. Rooke."

"Mrs. Pye."

She was surprised I knew her. I knew her because I had written her husband into the book in March. I write everybody twice. Once in the register and once in my own book at the back, and the second writing is the one I remember.

"I have not come about a dead man," she said.

"All right."

"I have come to ask you to be a witness."

I put the lamp on the table and got the wick up and sat down, and did not ask her to sit, because a person who is going to say a hard thing will say it faster standing up.

She said, "They have decided I made him out worse than he was."

"Who has?"

"His brother. And the vestry. And Mrs. Craddock, who is no friend of mine, and I do not care about Mrs. Craddock." She turned the basket a quarter turn. "He has been dead since March and I have not said one word against him in the lane and I am not going to begin now. But his brother said at the funeral that I had a hard tongue and a soft head, and last week he said it again in the shop, and now when I go in for the meal they look at me the way they look at a woman who has been telling a story."

"What is it you want witnessed?"

She put the basket on the table and took out a belt.

It was a man's belt, black, worn through on the second hole, and it had been rolled and tied with a piece of string, and the string was new. She had done that. She had rolled up her husband's belt and tied it with new string and carried it a mile and a half in a basket.

"Anything you like," she said. "One line. I am not asking for a sermon."

I put my hand on it.

Three seconds.

It is not a picture. It is a room going about its business, and then it is over, and the room is a room again. This one was a kitchen with a fire low and a plate on the table with something on it that had been eaten and not cleared, and a man standing at the end of the table with his back half to me, unhurried, taking his belt off.

He folded it once, in the middle, the way a man folds a strap he means to use.

The buckle was brass and it touched the table and it did not ring, because he set it down.

Then the belt went out of the frame, and the room held still, and there was no anger in it anywhere. That is the part I have not been able to put down since. He was not in a rage. He was doing a thing he had done before, and he did it the way a man does a chore, and the fire went on being low.

I came back with my hand on the belt and my ears ringing.

Mrs. Pye was watching my face. She had the look of a woman watching a doctor.

"Well," she said.

"He did it," I said. "More than once. He was not angry."

I did not put my hand on it again. I looked at it instead, which is the other half of the trade and the half nobody pays for. The strap had a soft crease at the fold, the crease a belt takes when it has been folded the same way a great many times, and it had not taken that crease from a nail. And the second hole was worn through and the first was not, and a man does not wear out the second hole putting his trousers on.

She sat down then, without being asked, and put her hands in her lap.

"That is the whole of it," she said. "He was never angry."

I have used her sentence ever since.

I got the day book and wrote it out on a half sheet, because a thing said across a table the parish can say never happened, and a thing on paper with my name under it the parish has to walk around. I wrote it in eight lines. I did not put in the kitchen or the plate or the brass buckle, because those are mine and they are not evidence, and because a woman who gives away everything she sees has nothing left to swear to.

I signed it and blotted it and gave it to her.

She read it twice. Then she folded it small and put it inside her dress, and picked up the basket with the belt in it, and stood.

"I am sorry about your nose," she said.

I put my hand up. It had started while I was writing and I had not attended to it, which is what happens when the reading is a hard one and the body gets on with it without asking.

"It is the price," I said. "It is not you."

"It is a high price for a belt."

"It is a high price for anything." I got the cloth off the shelf and held it to my face. "Mrs. Pye. You knew before you came."

"Yes."

"Then what did you come for?"

She stood in the door with her basket and thought about it, honestly, the way a person does when the question is a fair one and they have not been asked it before.

"To have it said in a room with somebody else in it," she said.

***

Mr. Rooke came in at half past four with the smell of the church on him, which is cold stone and lamp oil and, in October, the mould from the chest.

He saw the cloth and did not say anything about it. He put the water on and got the small basin and set it down in front of me and went out to the yard and came back with the pump water, which is colder than the house water and stops it faster.

"You have had a hard one," he said.

"A belt."

He sat down on the other side of the table and turned his hat over once and put it down.

"Tell me when you want to."

That is the whole of him. Six months married and he has never once asked me what I have been doing, and I have never once been able to decide whether that is the best thing about him or the thing I will not be able to bear for thirty years.

I took the cloth away and looked at it, and then I took the letter out of my pocket and put it on the table between us.

He looked at it and did not touch it.

"Today?"

"This morning."

"And you have carried it about all day."

"I have."

He picked it up then, and turned it over, and read the front, and then read it again, and put it down.

"There is money in it," he said.

"Two pounds."

"Then it is a fee, and you do not take fees."

"It is a coach fare," I said. "A fee is for the answer. Nobody has bought an answer off me with two pounds. Two pounds is what it costs to go and be asked."

He was quiet for a while. The fire took a piece of coal and the piece of coal fell in and the sound of it went round the room.

"Say the rest of it," he said.

So I said the rest of it, which was one line near the bottom of the letter, under the name, as though she had written the letter and then gone out of the house and come back in and added the thing she had not been able to say the first time.

*There is a line in our book that is in no hand in this house.*

He read it. He read it three times, which is what he does with a document, and then he put it down and pushed it an inch towards me and took his hand back.

"That is the part that decides it," he said.

"Yes."

"You could send it to the bishop's court. You could write to the man at Ely and let him write to the man at Ely."

"I could."

"It would take nine months and it would be the right thing and it would be done by post." He looked at the fire. "You are going to go."

"I am going to go."

"Then let us have the argument first," he said, "because I would like to have it while I am still able to be reasonable about it."

***

We had it in the passage, because the passage is where we have the ones that matter, and it went the way I knew it would go.

He said the office had been open eleven months and had never been shut. He said the parish had got used to the door being open, and a parish that gets used to a thing and then loses it does not go back to how it was, it goes looking for somebody to blame. He said that a woman who was given an office by a court in October and shuts it in October twelve months later has handed the other side a piece of paper.

He said, "It is not your four parishes."

I said, "No."

He said, "It is a hundred and eleven miles and you have never been further than Ely."

I said, "No."

He said, "And you will be in a house in a town where nobody knows what you are, and the first thing they will do is decide what you are, and they will decide it inside a day, and you will not be able to correct them because correcting them would mean explaining the office to a room full of people who have already made up their minds."

I said, "That is what happened here."

"That is what happened here," he said, "and it took a board and a court and a year and it nearly took your father's place in the world, and you are proposing to do it again in a town where you have no father and no court and no vestry and no me."

There it was.

"You could come," I said.

He stood with his hand on the door frame and did not answer at once, and I understood the whole shape of it while he was not answering.

"I have the audit at Christmas," he said. "There is nobody else in this parish who can do it. Your father is sixty-two and he has a catalogue and he is happy, and I am not going to put the book in front of him in December and ask him to be careful."

"No."

"And the vestry meets on the fourteenth."

"I know."

"So you would go alone."

"I would go alone."

He turned round then. "And come back when?"

"When the work is done."

"You do not know when that will be."

"No."

He looked at me for long enough that I had time to count the panels on the door, which is six, and the crack in the third one from the bottom, which is old and does not go through.

"I am not afraid you will be hurt," he said. "I am afraid you will go into that town and be the only person in it who can tell them what they want to know, and they will want it, and you will give it, and you will come back and I will have to live with whoever comes back."

I had nothing to say to that which was not a lie.

So I said the true thing, which was smaller.

"I have been in this trade eleven months," I said, "and in eleven months nobody has written me a sentence like that one. A book with a hand in it that does not belong to the house. That is the only thing in the world I am for. If I do not go to it, then what I have is a licence to sit in a warm room and be interesting to the parish."

He laughed at that. It was not a good laugh but it was a real one.

"All right," he said.

"All right?"

"All right." He came back and put his hand flat on the table, which is as near as he comes to putting it on mine in the daytime. "But you will write to me every week, and you will put down what you spend, every penny, and you will not take one farthing from anybody in that town for anything, and when it is done you will come home and we will have the rest of this argument in the spring when I am better at it."

***

I went up the lane to my father's house after that, because he is sixty-two and he does not like to hear a thing third hand, and because I wanted to see whether he would ask me a question.

He was at the table with the catalogue.

He has been at it since Michaelmas of last year. It is a list of every paper in the church chest, in his own hand, with a note against each one of what it is and whether it can be found, and it will never be finished, and that is the point of it. He works at it four hours a day and he has a system of letters at the top of each page and he has never once explained the letters to anybody.

I told him. He listened with the pen down and his head a little to one side, which is how he listened for thirty-one years.

"And who is to sit in the office?" he said.

"Nobody. Hettie will keep the door and the book. Questions that can wait, wait."

"And them that cannot?"

"Mr. Tice."

He wrote something in the catalogue. I could not see what, and I did not lean to see, because a man's catalogue is his own the way the office book is mine.

"Take the small case," he said. "The leather one with the strap. Your mother's things are in it and they are not doing anything, and a woman going a hundred miles ought to have a case that shuts."

I stood there a moment.

"You are not going to ask me what it is about," I said.

"No."

"Why not?"

He put the pen down and looked at me, and for a moment he was the man in the church porch on a Tuesday, which is the man I have never been able to argue with.

"Because I asked your mother one question in nineteen years," he said, "and I asked it wrong, and I have had a long time since to think about it. Take the case. And Mercy. When you come back, do not tell me all of it and then tell me the rest in six months. Tell me the whole of it once."

That is the longest speech my father has made to me since my mother died in January, and I have written it down here whole because I do not expect another.

***

I wrote the notice that night at the table, with the lamp, and Hettie Fenn holding the candle because she had come at six to sit with me and would not go home.

It said that the office would be shut from the fifteenth of October and would open again when I came back, and that anybody with a question that could wait should wait, and that anybody with a question that could not wait should go to Mr. Tice, who would do what could be done, which was nothing, but would do it kindly.

"How long do I put?" Hettie said.

"You do not put. You leave it."

"People will want to know how long."

"People will want a great deal," I said, "and I have two pounds and a hundred and eleven miles and I am not going to put a date on the door that I have made up."

She held the candle while I pinned it up. Then she read it, moving her lips, and said, "You have not said why."

"No."

"Shall I tell them why?"

"You shall not, because you do not know, and neither do I." I got my hand flat on the door and pushed the pin in with my thumb. "It is a book with a wrong hand in it, Hettie. That is all I know."

She was quiet for a bit.

"That will do for me," she said. "It will not do for Mrs. Craddock."

Then I sat down and wrote to the postmaster at Barrowfield, and told him the fifteenth of October and the King's Head on the eighteenth, and asked him to put that in the way of the same woman, and I paid the postage on it myself, which made the two pounds in the letter into one pound eighteen and tenpence, and I wrote that down in the day book because a sum you have written down is a sum nobody can say you invented.

***

I packed that night in my mother's small case, the leather one with the strap that shuts. Two shifts, a comb, her thimble, the day book, the office book in its cloth, and the belt buckle of a man I never met, which is not a thing you pack, it is a thing you find in your pocket three days later and cannot account for.

The ring I did not take off. It has been on my hand a year and a half and it has never once been off it, and it says, on the inside where nobody sees it, *ASK, AND BE ANSWERED. AND THEN BE QUIET.*

I can do the first two.

I read the letter one more time before I put it in the case, and I read the last line of it the way I read a page of the register when I am looking for a thing that has been scraped.

*There is a line in our book that is in no hand in this house.*

I have been in this trade eleven months. That is the first sentence anybody has written to me that I would have written myself.

I did not say that to Mr. Rooke. He would have understood it, because he is the only person I have met who reads a thing three times the way I do, and he is the one I will need beside me before this is done. I mean to bring him to it, if not in the flesh then in every letter I write from Barrowfield.

## Two · The Road

The carrier's cart left the Green at six on the fifteenth of October with two barrels, a crate of plates, a dog, and me, and it went to Wendon, which is six miles, and took an hour and a quarter because of the barrels.

Mr. Rooke came as far as Thorneley.

He had said, the night before, that he would walk beside it to the second gate, and he had said it in the voice a man uses when he has decided a thing and does not want to be argued with. At the second gate he did not get down. He got up behind with the barrels and the crate of plates, because there was no room on the box, and at every stop he came out and stood in the road until he had seen that I was still there, which he did four times, and at noon he handed me half a pie he had bought at the last house and did not eat any of it himself.

"You said the second gate," I said.

"I said a great many things in October."

He rode the rest of the day in his good coat with the dust on it and did not say anything about the office, or the parish, or what he thought I was going to find. At about the twentieth mile he said, "You will write."

"On Sunday."

"Every Sunday. Not when there is something to say. Every Sunday. And Mercy. If it is bad, write it bad. Do not write me a fair copy."

***

The country changes at the thirtieth mile. That is the thing nobody tells you.

Up to Wendon and past it, it is chalk. The roads are white and the ruts hold the light and there is dust in October that gets into your collar, and the water in the brooks is clear because it has come out of the ground a mile ago and has not had time to learn anything.

After the thirtieth mile it is clay, and the clay is yellow and it holds the water, and the hedges are taller because a hedge in clay has something to stand in. The road goes brown. The wheels make a different sound, which is a wet sound and not a dry one, and by four in the afternoon everything I had was the colour of the road.

At the thirty-eighth mile I smelled it, before I saw anything.

It is not a bad smell. That is what I had not expected. I had been told about the smoke and I had made up my mind to a thing like a house on fire, and it is not that. It is a low, warm, mineral smell, like a kettle left on a hob in an empty room, and it comes and goes with the wind, and when it is there you can taste it at the back of your mouth.

The man beside me on the box said, "That is Barrowfield, that is."

"We are forty miles off."

"You can smell it at forty miles when the wind is nor'-east." He spat over the side. "You will smell it at ten and you will not smell it at all when you are in it. That is the way with a town. You stop being able to smell the thing you are standing in."

***

We put up at Thorneley at six, and the inn was full, and there was one room, and he carried the case up the stair himself.

The girl behind the bar looked at the ring on my hand and then at Mr. Rooke, and then at the ring again, and gave us the key, and said the water would be up at seven and the coach at five.

The room had a bed and a chair and a window that looked at the yard, and a jug and basin, and a nail on the back of the door where somebody had hung a coat for forty years and the paint had grown round it.

He stood in the middle of the room with his hat in his hand and said, "I will take the chair."

"You will not take the chair."

"Mercy."

"I have been in a strange house for one night in my life," I said, "and that was in June, and I lay awake in it being polite. I am not going to spend tonight being polite as well. Tomorrow I will be in a town where nobody knows what I am, and the day after that I will be in a house where a girl is waiting to ask me a thing she has been carrying eleven years, and I will have to sit in a chair and be looked at and be careful, and I am not going to be careful tonight."

He put the hat down.

The candle was on the chair because there was no table, and I put it on the floor in the corner where it would not light the bed, and then I could not see him properly, and that was better.

It was not the way it is at home.

At home it is dark and it is ours and it is the one hour of the day when neither of us has to account for anything. Here there was a bed that had been slept in by a hundred people and a wall with the next room's voice coming through it and a door that did not lock, and I did not care about any of it. I got his coat off him and unbuttoned the front of his waistcoat with my hands going too fast and I had to do the third button twice, and he said my name once, quietly, in the way he does when he means stop, and I said, "No," and he stopped saying it.

He is careful. That is the thing about him that took me six months to understand. He is not careful because he is afraid of me. He is careful because he has been forty-five years in a world where a man takes what he wants and calls it affection, and he has decided to be slow about everything, and it makes him slow in a way that would be funny in a lesser man.

I did not want slow. I wanted the thing that happens after, when there is no more accounting.

So I put my hand flat on his chest and pushed, and he went back onto the bed, and I got over him, and I did the rest myself.

There is a thing that happens when I am the one doing it. He goes quiet. Not the quiet of a man who has given up but the quiet of a man who is paying attention, and his hands stay where they are put, and he watches my face in the dark, and when it comes he does not say anything at all, he puts his hand on the back of my neck and holds it there, which is the only thing he ever does without being asked.

Afterwards I lay with my ear on his chest and counted his heart down from where it was, which took a hundred and twelve beats, and he said, into the dark, "That was not a wife going on a journey."

"No."

"That was a woman getting something over with."

I did not answer, because he was right, and because being right is not the same as being kind.

Then he said, "I am not offended. I would rather be the thing you do before a hard day than the thing you do after."

That is the nearest he has come to saying something about himself in six months, and I have thought about it a great deal since, and I have decided it is either the most generous thing anybody has ever said to me or the most frightening, and I cannot tell which, and I am not sure it matters.

***

He was gone before six.

I heard the door and did not get up, because getting up would have made it into a thing, and it was not a thing, it was a man going back to a parish he had taken on for my sake and a house with a cloth on the table at four o'clock.

He had left the rest of the pie on the chair with a note under the tin, and the note said, *The office is open at seven. Write on Sunday. E. R.*

That is the last I saw of him for ten weeks.

***

The second day was the long one.

I had the inside because of the rain, and the inside had a woman with a baby and a clergyman and a man who sold soda, and by eleven o'clock I knew the clergyman's opinion of the bishop and the soda man's opinion of the clergy, and I had said nine words.

At the change at Marthwaite a man got in with a leather roll and a good coat and a way of taking up more of the seat than the seat had, and he was a rider for a coal factor, and he was going to Barrowfield.

I have never in my life been so glad of a talkative man.

He told me the state of the trade for forty miles. He told me the price of a ton at the wharf and the price at the canal basin and the difference, which is called the carriage. He told me that the town had been having a hard winter for three winters and that the coal was wet, which everybody said, and that a wet ton is a heavy ton and a heavy ton is a good ton if you are the one selling and a bad one if you are the one burning.

Then he said, "You will be going to Barrowfield on business?"

"My husband is a clerk," I said. "I am going to a family he knows."

That is not a lie. It is a smaller thing than the truth, which is not the same as a lie. I have had that sentence in my mouth since I was fourteen.

"Then you will hear about the Kells," he said, and settled himself, and told me.

The Kells had the wharf at the basin end since before the canal. There was a fire in 'twenty-one that took the warehouse and the office and six men, and Amos Kell was one of the six, and after that the widow did not keep it up, and the girl is the last of it. The girl has the offices. That is the thing people cannot get their heads round.

"The offices," I said.

"The market and the crane and the bridge and the rest. Fourteen of them, and thirteen is hers to sell and one is not, and the one that is not is the beam." He tapped the leather roll. "You cannot sell the beam. You cannot let the beam. You cannot put a man in to do the beam. The beam is a thing a person does with their own hands, and the courts have always held it, and there is a paper about it somewhere from the old queen's time."

"And the thirteenth can be sold."

"The thirteen go with her when she marries. That is what a husband is for." He laughed at his own joke, and then, because I did not, he stopped. "Beg pardon. It is how it is. There is a man in this town who has made a trade of it. He has a book. Every woman in this county with an office to her name is in that book with a figure against her, and he will tell you the figure to the shilling, and he is not often wrong."

"What is his name?"

"Bedloe." He said it the way a man says the name of a dog that has bitten him. "He is a lawyer, or he says he is. He does the marriages. A man wants a wife with a decent set of offices, he goes to Bedloe, and Bedloe reads him the book, and it costs him a guinea to hear it read."

I looked out of the window at the clay going by.

"And the girl is in the book," I said.

"The girl has been in the book since she was nine," he said.

***

The third day the country went to pieces and came back together as something else.

There was no hedge for a mile at a time. There were gates across the road and a man at each gate and a board with a rate on it, and the rate went up at every gate. There were mounds of grey spoil with grass trying to take them and not managing, and there were shafts with a shed over them and a wheel going round, and at one of them the wheel was stopped and the shed was empty and the spoil had been there long enough to have a colour of its own.

At eleven we came to a place where the road ran along the top of a cut, and down in the cut there was a wharf and a crane and three boats, and a woman standing on the edge with a board and a piece of chalk, putting down what came out of the boats.

I watched her for the four minutes the horses were being changed.

She did not look at the beam. There was a beam on the wharf, a proper one, iron, with a pan, and a man in a leather apron working it, and she did not look at it once. She looked at the sacks. She counted the sacks going over and she wrote the number on the board and she never once looked at the weight.

"Does she not trust the beam?" I said.

The rider looked down at her and then at me, and something in his face changed and went careful.

"She does not need it," he said. "She has been at that wharf nineteen years. She can put her hand under a sack and tell you what it is to a pound." He sat back. "That is what they do here. The beam is the law and the hand is the truth, and when the two disagree the hand wins and nobody says anything, because the beam belongs to somebody."

I got out the day book and wrote that down.

The rider watched me write.

"You are a one for the book," he said.

"I am."

"What do you put in it?"

"The price of things," I said, and shut it, and he laughed, and I put it away, and neither of us said anything else about it for forty miles.

***

The last night we put up at a place eleven miles short of the town, and it was a public house and not an inn, and the bed was over the kitchen and the kitchen never went out.

I could not sleep. There was a woman down there working and a man coming in and going out and a dog that had decided about me, and at one in the morning I gave it up and sat on the edge of the bed with the candle and copied the letter into the day book.

I copy all of them. I have copied every letter that has come to the office since the second of November, and there are nineteen of them, and it is not for the answer, because the answer goes in the office book. It is for the hand. A person's hand in May and the same person's hand in October will tell you whether they have been ill, or frightened, or writing on a table that was too high, and no witness in the world will tell you that.

This one had been written at a table that was too high. The letters got smaller towards the bottom of the page and the last line ran uphill, which is what happens when a person is writing with their elbow in the air.

Under it, after the name, was the line about the book.

I copied that too, and then I sat with the pen in my hand and did the thing I have done since I was fourteen, which is to read it as though it were an entry in a register and not a thing written by a woman at one in the morning.

*There is a line in our book that is in no hand in this house.*

A book is a thing with a house round it. A line in a book that is not in any hand in the house is a line that somebody came in to write, or a line that was written by somebody who lives there and is not admitting to it, and those are two different crimes and they want two different answers.

I put the candle out and lay down and did not sleep, and at half past four the woman downstairs began on the bread.

***

We came into Barrowfield at a quarter past six on the eighteenth, in the dark, in the rain.

You do not see a town like that arrive. You see the lights first, and the lights are not in a row like a village, they are in a smear, and there is one tall light at the basin end that goes up and down as the boats come through the lock. Then you smell it properly, which is the smell of a kettle and a stable and a wet brickfield all at once, and then the road goes down and you are in it.

The wharf is at the bottom. There is a stone edge and black water with a skin on it and coal stacked in long ridges under tarpaulins, and the ridges go on further than the lamps do. Men were still working. There is no hour in a coal town when men are not working, and the sound of it is not shouting, it is a kind of low continuous grinding, like a very large animal turning over in its sleep.

The coach set me down at the King's Head and the guard put my case on the step and the coach went on.

I stood there with the rain on my face and the case in my hand and the letter in my pocket, and I had got as far as thinking that I did not know which way to walk.

A woman came across the yard with a lamp held low, and she came straight at me, and she stopped three feet off and lifted the lamp and looked at my face.

"You are the letter," she said.

It was not a question. There was nothing rude in it either. It was said the way a person says the name of a medicine they have been waiting for.

"Yes," I said.

"I am Sowerby. I keep the shop at the end of the lane." She let the lamp down. "I have been at this yard every night since the twelfth, and the girl was at it every night from the fourth, and tonight I made her stop at home because she has not slept. Come in out of this. There is a thing I have to tell you before you see her, and I would rather tell it to you sitting down."

I picked up my case and followed her.

I had been in Barrowfield four minutes and I had already been recognised, and I did not like it at all.

## Three · The Beam House

Mrs. Sowerby's shop was one room with a back room behind it, and the back room had a fire and two chairs and a table with a cloth on it, and she put the lamp on the table and a pot on the fire and sat down opposite me with her hands on her knees.

"First," she said, "you are not staying at Tarrant's."

"I have not been asked."

"You will be. He will ask you tomorrow before dinner and he will make it sound like the decent thing, and it will be the decent thing, and if you sleep under his roof you will be his guest, and a guest does not ask questions about the price of coal." She nodded at the wall. "You will have the room above this. It is not much and it is mine and nobody in this town can turn you out of it."

"Thank you."

"Second." She looked at the fire and not at me. "He has told everybody you are here to value the offices."

I put my cup down.

"Say that again."

"Mr. Tarrant. He was in the market on Tuesday and again on Thursday, and he said the letter-writer from Collow was coming to put a figure on the thirteen, for the settlement, and that it was a kindness in him to have it done by somebody who knows books and not by a lawyer who would charge for it." She turned her cup a quarter turn. "Half the town thinks you are here to help him sell her."

"And the other half?"

"The other half thinks you are here to buy." She looked up. "There is no third half, Mrs. Rooke. Not in this town."

I sat and thought about that for a while, and about how long it takes to undo a thing that has been said twice in a market.

"Who told him I was coming?"

"The postmaster. You wrote to him, and he is a good man and he reads everything, and he told the parson, and the parson told Tarrant, because the parson tells Tarrant everything, and that is not the parson's fault, it is the way the town is made."

I had written to a room where a person might come in. I had not thought about the man in the room.

"Then we will begin behind," I said. "It is not the first time."

"It is the first time in this town." She turned her cup a quarter turn. "You should know a thing about me before you sleep in my house."

"Go on."

"I buy by the ton and I sell by the pound." She said it to the cloth. "Two years ago I weighed out a hundredweight for a woman and it came to ninety-eight. I thought I had made a mistake. I weighed the next one and it came to ninety-eight. Then I went home and sat down for an hour."

"And then?"

"And then I made the difference up out of the till," she said. "Two years, Mrs. Rooke. Every sack. There is a book at the back with the difference in it in my own hand. If anybody finds that book they will say I have been cheating the poor. And the licence for this shop is the office of the market, and the office of the market is in Mr. Tarrant's gift. I would be out of this door in a week."

I looked at her for a while.

"You have been paying for it yourself," I said.

"Somebody had to."

"How much?"

"Four pounds eleven since March last year, and I have not added up the year before because I did not want to know."

I got out the day book and wrote it down, and she watched me do it and did not stop me, which is the largest thing anybody had done for me since I came into that town.

***

I went out at seven the next morning, before the shop opened, because I wanted to see the wharf before anybody had decided what to say to me.

A coal town in the morning is not like a village in the morning. There is no hour when it is quiet and then an hour when it is not. There is a low fire going all night and men coming and going all night and the water in the basin moving all night because the boats work the lock at any hour the level is right, and by seven the light was up and the whole place was the colour of a wet slate.

The wharf ran along the basin in a long ridge. Coal in heaps under tarpaulin, coal in sacks stacked in fours, coal in a cart with the tail down and a boy asleep on it. Beyond the coal the canal went out flat and grey between two brick walls, and there was a bridge, and beyond the bridge the town went up in roofs.

At the far end of the wharf, where the basin narrowed, there was a small stone building with a slate roof and one window and a door, and the door had a chain on it and a padlock the size of a man's fist, and beside the door there was a stool, and on the stool there was an old man with a blanket round him.

I went and stood in front of him.

He looked up. He had one eye gone milky and the other one sharp.

"Shut," he said.

"I can see that."

"Then you can see it and go away, which is more than most."

"What is it shut for?"

He looked at me for a while with the sharp eye.

"It is shut because it is not to be opened," he said. "That is the whole of the reason and I have been sitting here eleven years on account of it, and I am paid nine shillings a week to sit here and say what I have just said, and I say it very well now, and I do not require an audience."

"Nine shillings a week to sit on a stool."

"And a coal allowance." He pulled the blanket up. "Do not look at me like that. It is a living."

I looked at the padlock. It was a good one. The chain was new, or newer than the door, and the hasp had been put on with four screws and the screws were bright.

"Who holds the key?"

"Mr. Tarrant."

"And who put the chain on?"

"Mr. Tarrant."

"And what is inside?"

The old man shifted on the stool and looked up and down the wharf, the way a person does when they are deciding how much of a thing is safe.

"A beam," he said. "It is a weighing beam. It is a great iron thing with a pivot and two arms and a pan, and it is the finest beam in this county, and it is shut in a shed with a man on a stool in front of it, and every ton of coal that goes over this wharf is weighed on another one in the yard, and everybody knows it, and nobody says it."

"Why does nobody say it?"

"Because the other one gives a good weight," he said, "and a good weight is a good price, and there is not a man on this wharf who does not want a good price." He spat. "You are the letter, then."

"I am."

"I thought you would be older."

***

Kell's house was at the basin end, and it was a mistake to call it a house. It was a warehouse with a house grown onto the side of it, and the house part had a good door with a fanlight and a brass knocker and two steps, and the warehouse part had a cart door and a crane over it and a smell of coal and rope.

A girl opened the door. She had a cap on and her sleeves pinned and she looked at me the way a servant looks at a person who has come to the wrong door.

"Mrs. Rooke," I said. "For Miss Kell."

She went away and came back and took me into a room at the front with a fire and a clock and a good carpet and a table with a blue cloth, and she left me there for four minutes, which I counted.

Then Judith Kell came in.

I had made a picture of her on the road and the picture was wrong. I had made a girl who had been run over by a town. What came into the room was a woman of twenty who had been running a wharf since she was ten, and it showed in the way she came in and did not hurry and did not apologise for the four minutes.

She was not tall. She had her hair up in a way that took time. Her hands were a wharfinger's hands and she had made them look like a lady's hands and had not quite managed it at the knuckles.

"Mrs. Rooke," she said. "You came."

"You asked me to."

"I asked you in May. It is October."

"You did not put a direction on the letter."

She stood with her hand on the back of a chair.

"No," she said. "I did not."

"Then it took five months, and a postmaster, and a book of post towns, and two pounds, and it has taken me three days on the road, and I am here." I sat down without being asked, because I had decided on the road that I would sit down first in every room in this town. "Sit down, Miss Kell. You have had eleven years. You can have ten minutes."

She sat.

***

She gave me the offices first.

I had expected to have to ask. She went to the press in the corner and brought out a bundle of books tied with tape, and put them on the blue cloth, and untied them, and set them out in a row, and turned each one so that the spine was towards me.

Fourteen. They were not all the same size. Some were tall and some were small and two were no more than pamphlets in a paper cover, and every one of them had a name written on the spine in a different hand, because the offices had been held by different people and the books had come down with the offices and nobody had ever thought to write them all out again.

"Read them," she said. "You will want to."

"I would rather you told me."

"There are fourteen." She put her finger on the first. "The market. The crane. The bridge. The pump. The shambles. The coal back. The measure. The ferry. The lamps. The scavenger. The bell. The almshouse. And the toll at the turn. That is thirteen. And the beam."

"That is the one that cannot be sold."

"That is the one that cannot be sold, let, deputed, or devised." She said it flatly, the way a person says a thing they have said to themselves a great many times. "The others are mine to hold and my husband's to hold after me, and they bring in three hundred and twelve pounds a year between them, and every one of them is a fee and not a wage, and every one of them can be taken off me by the town if I am shown to have neglected it."

"And the beam."

"The beam is nine pounds twelve a year." She looked at the books and not at me. "It is the smallest of the fourteen and it is the only one that matters, and it has not been used since the nineteenth of December, 1821."

"Why not?"

"Because my father promised it would not be."

There it was, put down on the table between us like a coin.

"Say the rest of it," I said.

"I will not say the rest of it in this room," she said. "I have had eleven years of saying the rest of it in this room and it has got me nowhere."

She stood up and went to the window and stood with her back to me.

"Come and see the crane," she said.

***

The crane was over the cart door of the warehouse, and it was a chain crane with a jib, and there was a boat under it with six ton of coal in the hold and two men shovelling into a basket.

They were not weighing. That was the first thing I understood. The basket went up, the jib swung, the basket went over the cart, the basket came down, and the man at the cart tipped it and chalked a mark on a board. One mark, two marks, three.

"There is no beam in that," I said.

"There is a beam in the yard and I am not to use it." Judith stood with her hands on the rail of the cart door. "That is the twenty-eighth basket."

"How do you know?"

"Because I have been counting since we came out and I count without knowing I am doing it. It is six ton and a quarter in the hold and it will go out at six ton and three quarters, and the difference is water and dust and it is a fair difference, and I could tell you the weight of that basket to four pounds with my eyes shut."

The basket went up and swung and came down.

"Twelve," she said.

The man at the cart tipped it and chalked a mark and called out something to the man in the boat, and the man in the boat said something back, and the boy with the board wrote it down.

Judith did not look at the board.

"Eleven," she said. "He has written eleven. He has been writing eleven since I was ten and nobody has ever asked him why."

"Why has nobody asked him?"

"Because he is a good man and his wife is ill and it is one basket in twenty-eight." She turned round. "That is this town, Mrs. Rooke. It is not one great wickedness. It is one basket in twenty-eight, twenty-eight times a day, for eleven years, and at the end of it there is a wharf that does not belong to me any more and a book in Mr. Bedloe's office with my name in it at a figure."

***

She took me up herself after that, without asking whether I wanted to go. There was a good stair with a turn in it and a window on the half landing with a view of the basin and the crane, and she went up past the first floor and did not stop, and on the second floor there was a passage with three doors and she stopped at the middle one.

"This is where he died," she said.

The door was shut and had a brass handle worn bright, which is what happens to a handle that is used, and it had not been used in eleven years.

"Not in this room," she said. "In the warehouse. The fire was in the warehouse and the floor went and he was under it, and they brought him up here because this was the nearest room with a bed in it, and he was alive for four hours." She had her hand on the door and had not turned it. "I was ten. I sat in that chair."

I did not say anything.

"Everybody in this town has been very kind to me about this room," she said. "They have not made me open it. Mrs. Sowerby has cleaned it twice a year since I was fourteen and she has never once asked me to go in with her." She turned the handle and pushed the door and stood back. "You will want to be in it. That is what you are for."

The room was cold and clean and had been aired. There was a bed with the counterpane on it and a chair by the window and a washstand with a jug on it, and there was no dust anywhere, and the window was shut.

I stood in the door.

"I am not going to read this room today," I said.

"Why not?"

"Because I have been in this town eleven hours and I do not yet know what the question is, and a woman who reads a room without a question gets the room's own answer, which is usually about furniture." I looked at her. "And because you have not asked me anything yet, and I have come a hundred and eleven miles, and I am not going to guess."

She stood in the passage with her hands at her sides.

"Tomorrow," she said.

"Tomorrow."

"At the beam house." She looked at the shut door and then at me. "Not here. If you tell me a thing in this room I will have to live in it. The beam house is a shed and I have never liked it and I do not care what happens in it."

Then she went down the stair ahead of me, and at the turn she said, without stopping and without turning round, "Mrs. Rooke. Do you take the money?"

"No."

"Then what do you take?"

"Nothing," I said. "That is the difficulty with it. It makes people want to give me something else."

## Four · Fourteen Offices

Mr. Tarrant's clerk came for me at seven in the morning, which Mrs. Sowerby said was the first lie of the day.

"He does not see anybody at eight," she said. "He does not get up at eight. He has a man to light his fire at eight."

"Then he is up early for me."

"He is up early for the wharf, and you are the wharf's business, and I would take my book." She put a cup down in front of me with a hand that was not quite steady. "When he says a figure, write it down before you answer. A figure you have written down cannot be talked out of the air afterwards."

I took the day book. I ate half a piece of bread because I had learned on the road that a morning with a strange man in it goes better on bread.

***

He had a house on the high side of the market cross with a green door and two windows and a brass plate that said only his name, and the clerk took me through a passage into a room with a fire and a long table and a wall of pigeonholes that went from the floor to the ceiling.

Tarrant was standing at the window with his hands behind him.

He was a big man gone soft, sixty or near it, in a dark coat that had been made for him when he was thinner and had been let out at the sides, and he had the face of a man who has been told he is kind. He came across the room with his hand out and took mine in both of his, which is a thing men do to women they intend to talk over.

"Mrs. Rooke. You are good to come at an hour I would not ask of my own clerk." He put me in a chair with its back to the fire. "You had a bad journey."

"Three days."

"And rain from Thorneley on. My brother lives at Thorneley. It rains there as a matter of principle." He sat down across from me and folded his hands on the table. "I will not keep you all morning. I want a valuation."

"So I was told."

"By whom?"

"By everybody," I said. "Two men in the market on Friday. The parson's wife at the pump. And a boy who brought a note from your clerk at seven this morning, in a town where a note at seven is a piece of news."

He did not smile. He looked at the fire for a moment, deciding something, and then he nodded once, which was the first honest thing he had done.

"Then say what you have heard, and I will tell you what is true."

"I have heard that I am here to put a figure on thirteen offices, for the settlement, as a kindness, because a lawyer would charge you for it."

"That is three-quarters true," he said. "The kindness is a word I used in the market. The figure is what I want. The lawyer is one I cannot afford to have in this room." He reached to the shelf behind him and put a book on the table. "Here."

It was a folio, calf, worn at the corners, with a paper label pasted on the front, and on the label, in a good clerk's hand and in capitals, one word: OFFICES.

He opened it and turned it round for me.

Fourteen leaves. Each leaf ruled into columns and each column filled, and one leaf for each of the fourteen offices, and the first leaf had been written in 1809 and the last in 1831, and each leaf carried the same columns: the name of the office, the fee, the day of election or appointment, the name of the holder, and at the bottom, in a different column, the number of pounds for which the office had last changed hands.

"The market," he said, and put his finger on the first leaf. "The market is forty-one pounds a year and the tolls are farmed to Mr. Whitworth's agent at present for thirty-eight and a half. The crane is fifty-two. The bridge is nineteen and the bridge wants two hundred pounds spending on it and nobody will spend it. The pump is twenty-four. The shambles is sixteen and fourteen. The coal back is sixteen. The measure is eight. The ferry is ten. The lamps are five and the lamps are the worst of them because the oil is bought by the town and the town buys the cheapest. The scavenger is four. The bell is three. The almshouse is one pound six and it is a charity and I do not count it. And the toll at the turn is seventy-five, which is more than the market, and you will want to know why, and the reason is the road."

I wrote them down as he said them. Some of them I had heard from Judith on the stairs. The totals I had not.

I added the column twice, because a column added once is a wish. The fees came to two hundred and seventy-three pounds fourteen. The tolls of the market were farmed at thirty-eight and ten and were not in the column and had to be in it, because a farmed toll is money in the hand and not in the book, and money in the hand is what a settlement is made of. Two hundred and seventy-three and fourteen, and thirty-eight and ten, is three hundred and twelve pounds four.

"Three hundred and twelve," I said, and left the four shillings where they were, to see what he would do with them.

"Three hundred and twelve pounds four shillings, and the shillings have not changed since 1813, and I know them the way I know the Ten Commandments."

"And the beam is nine pounds twelve, and it is not in this book."

"No." He closed the folio. "It is not in this book."

"Why not?"

"Because a thing that cannot be sold cannot be valued, and a thing that cannot be valued does not go in a valuation book." He said it patiently, the way a man says a thing he has explained before to people who did not want to hear it. "There are fourteen offices. Thirteen are in that book and one is not, and the one that is not is the only one in Barrowfield that anybody outside Barrowfield has ever heard of."

I looked at the closed folio.

"Mr. Tarrant," I said, "I am not going to value your asset for you."

He did not move.

"You are not valuing my asset," he said. "You are valuing hers. She is twenty years old and she has fourteen offices and no more idea of what they are worth than a girl with fourteen spoons. If she marries Whitworth she will be rich and she will not know it. If she marries Ellerbeck she will be comfortable and she will not know it. If she marries my nephew she will be at my table every Sunday for the rest of her life and she will never know it either." He put his hand flat on the folio. "Somebody in this room should know what she has. I had rather it was a woman with a book than a lawyer with a bill."

"Then let her have the book."

"I have offered her the book." He said it without heat. "She will not open it. She says it is her father's hand and she cannot abide the sight of it, and that is eleven years of grief talking and grief is a thing you cannot argue with, so I have stopped arguing with it and got on with the business."

That was true, and the trouble with it was that it was true in the wrong place, like a nail driven in straight through the middle of a window.

"Twenty years," I said. "You said twenty."

"I did not."

"You did not say it out loud." I turned the day book round so that he could see what I had written. "Three hundred and twelve pounds a year, and in this county an office is bought at twenty years' purchase, because that is what the crane went for in 1809 and what the ferry went for in 1817. Six thousand two hundred and forty pounds. You had that figure in your head before I came in the door."

He looked at the day book for a while. Then he did something I did not expect, which was to laugh, one short breath of it, without any pleasure in it at all.

"Six thousand two hundred and forty," he said. "I have had that figure in my head since 1826, Mrs. Rooke, and in six years nobody in this town has said it out loud, and the reason nobody has said it out loud is that saying it out loud makes people ask what happened to the wharf."

"What happened to the wharf?"

"You have been here two days."

"I have been here two days and I have been up the stair and seen the room with the bed in it, and I have watched twenty-eight baskets go over that crane and I have counted the chalks, and I have sat with a woman in the shop who has been paying four pounds eleven out of her own till because the hundredweight comes to ninety-eight." I put the pencil down. "You have brought me here to put a figure on a thing, Mr. Tarrant. I am telling you what the figure is and what it is not. Six thousand two hundred and forty for thirteen offices is a price in a market where the thing sold is a right to take a fee. The fee comes off the coal. The coal goes over a beam. If the beam is short, the fee is still paid and the buyer is still short, and the six thousand two hundred and forty is a price for the right to go on doing that."

The fire moved. He had a good fire and a good grate and a guard in front of it, and there was no ash on the hearth.

"That is the speech you came with," he said.

"It is the speech I have made twice before in other rooms."

"And how did it go in the other rooms?"

"Badly," I said. "The men in the other rooms were smaller than you."

He got up and went to the window and stood with his hands behind him, and I looked at his back and understood that I had given him a compliment without meaning to, and that he had taken it, and that this was going to make the rest of it harder.

"Her birthday is the twentieth of November," he said. "She will be twenty-one."

"I know."

"Then you know what it means. On the twentieth of November she can sign, sue, sell, marry without my consent, and take that wharf out of my hands and into whatever hands she likes, and every man in this town who has been waiting eleven years for a decision will get one in the same week." He turned round. "I have been her guardian since she was ten. Do you know what that has cost me?"

"Tell me."

"Nine hundred pounds," he said, "of my own money, in 1821, to clear the mortgage on that wharf so that the fire could be paid for and the town could go on taking coal through it. Not lent. Paid. There is no paper on it because a guardian cannot lend to his ward, and if I had taken one there is not a man in this county who would not have said I had taken advantage of a girl of ten." He came back to the table. "I have had eleven years of being that man. I would like, before I am too old to enjoy it, to be the man who arranged something."

"Arranged what?"

"A marriage," he said. "Before the twentieth. Her mother was married at nineteen to a man she had known six weeks and it was the best thing that ever happened to this family, and the certificate is in that box behind you if you want to see the date on it, and I will tell you what nobody says at the wedding. A woman with fourteen offices is not a woman. She is a settlement with a face on it. The man who marries her marries the settlement. The settlement must be managed by somebody who can sign his name in a court. That is not a thing I invented. That is the law of England and it has been the law of England since before either of us was born."

"Then it is a bad law."

"It is a bad law," he agreed, "and it is the law. I am not asking you to like it. I am asking you to put a figure on thirteen offices so that when the settlement is drawn, the girl's side has a number in it that a lawyer did not make up."

I sat with my hands in my lap and hated him in an orderly way, which is the only kind of hating that is any use in a room like that.

"Three hundred and twelve pounds four," I said. "At twenty years, six thousand two hundred and forty-four. At fifteen years, which is what the ferry went for when it was in want of repair, four thousand six hundred and eighty-three. The crane wants a new chain and the bridge wants two hundred spending, and I would take four hundred off the total for the two of them. The toll at the turn is the best of them and the lamps are the worst and the almshouse should not be sold at any price because it is a charity and a charity sold is a scandal in the county paper."

He had his pencil out before I finished.

"Say the bridge again."

"Four hundred off for the bridge and the crane chain, and get it in writing that the buyer does the work, because otherwise it will come out of the fees and the fees are what the town lives on."

He wrote that down. He wrote it down slowly and he underlined it twice, and I sat there and watched a man write down the one sentence in the whole morning that was on my side, and I understood that he had wanted it, that he had been waiting six years for somebody to say it, and that he would use it, and that using it would be the most useful thing anybody did in Barrowfield that week.

"Who are the three?" I said.

"Whitworth is not a candidate. Whitworth is a price. He is fifty-one and he has a wife in Hull who is not well and he wants the toll at the turn and he will pay for it in a marriage he does not intend to consummate, and if you think that is a slander you have not been in a coal town long." He turned a leaf. "Ellerbeck is a candidate. He is a miller, he is fifty-six, he buried his wife in March, he has four children and a good name and he would be kind to her in the way a man is kind to a horse he means to keep a long time."

"And the third?"

"My nephew." He said it without any particular warmth. "He is twenty-four and he has been in this office since he was seventeen and he knows the fourteen offices better than she does. He is not a fool. He is not a good man either, but he is not a fool, and in this county that is a distinction."

"Does he want her?"

"He wants the wharf."

"Does he want her?"

Tarrant looked at me and let a moment go by.

"He wants the wharf," he said again. "Whether he wants her is a question that has not been put to him, and I am not going to put it, because a young man asked that question in this town gives an answer that costs him nothing." He closed the folio. "You will meet him. He has been at Thorneley since Tuesday and he comes back on the twenty-second, and he will call on you, because he calls on everybody, and he will be civil, and I would take it as a kindness if you did not decide what he is until you have heard him talk about the pump."

"Why the pump?"

"Because the pump is the office nobody wants," he said, "and the pump is where you find out what a young man is. Ask him what he did about it, and listen to the answer."

***

He walked me to the door himself, which his clerk did not like.

"Mrs. Rooke." He had his hand on the latch. "You are married."

"I am."

"To a man who has no offices."

"To a man who has no offices."

"Then you have been standing in this town for two days on ground you do not know," he said, "and I will tell you the one thing I know that you do not. You are married. In this county that is a licence. A married woman may ask about her husband's interest and the town will answer her. The same question from an unmarried woman is a scandal, and the town will talk about her instead of the question. Use it while you have it. When Judith Kell is twenty-one she will have nobody to ask for."

I stood in his doorway with the market cross behind me and the smell of his good fire coming out past him.

"And when the question is about my own interest?"

He looked at me for a moment with something that was nearly pity, and pity from a man like that is worse than contempt.

"Then you will have to be quiet about it," he said, "and you are a woman with a book, so you will write it down instead."

***

I went to the beam house at eleven and Judith was not there.

The old man on the stool said she had come at nine and stood at the door and waited and gone away at half past ten, and that she had left no message because she was not a woman who left messages.

I stood on the wharf and looked at the padlock.

There was a smell off the basin that I had not noticed on Friday, which was the smell of wet coal and rope and something under it, and a boat was working the lock with a man leaning on the beam of it and the water going down in the chamber with a noise like a held breath let out.

I had come a hundred and eleven miles to be told a thing by a girl who had waited two hours in the wrong place because I had been in a room with a man who had paid nine hundred pounds in 1821 and wanted his money's worth in a nephew.

I got the day book out and wrote, under the figures:

*He wants to be the man who arranged something. Ask him in three weeks what he arranged, and watch his hands.*

Then I walked back up the hill to Kell's and knocked on the good door with the fanlight, and the servant with the pinned sleeves opened it and said Miss Kell was not at home, and I said I would wait in the passage, and she said Miss Kell was not at home to anybody, and I said I would wait in the passage.

She went away and came back and took me into the room with the blue cloth.

Judith came in four minutes later with her cap off and her hair not redone, and she stood in the door and looked at me, and I understood that I had done something to her by not being on the wharf at nine.

"I was at Mr. Tarrant's," I said. "He sent for me at seven. I did not send word because I did not know at seven that I would be kept till eleven, and I should have sent word at nine, and I did not, and that is the whole of it."

She came in and sat down.

"He told you the figure," she said.

"He told me the fees. I did the figure."

"What was it?"

"Six thousand two hundred and forty-four, and four hundred off for the bridge and the crane chain."

She sat with her hands in her lap, and then she did a thing I did not expect. She laughed. It was a short, ugly, astonished laugh, and it went out of her like something she had been holding since she was ten.

"Six thousand," she said. "Six thousand pounds. And I have been sitting in this house for eleven years thinking I was poor."

"You are not poor. You are illiquid."

"I am what?"

I had used a word out of a book and I saw it go past her, and I changed it, because there is no use in a word a person cannot carry.

"You own a great deal and you cannot lay your hand on any of it," I said. "A poor woman has a shilling in her pocket. You have six thousand pounds in a book and a locked shed on a wharf and you cannot spend either of them."

"Yes," she said. "That is it. That is exactly it." She looked at the fire. "Say the rest of it."

"The rest of it is that your uncle paid nine hundred pounds of his own money in 1821 and there is no paper on it, and he has been eleven years being the man who took advantage, and what he wants now is to be the man who arranged something."

"Before the twentieth," she said. "Before I am twenty-one."

"Before the twentieth."

She got up and went to the press in the corner and came back with the bundle of books and put it on the blue cloth between us, and she did not untie it.

"On the twentieth of November I can sign," she said. "Do you know what I would sign, Mrs. Rooke, if I were a man and it were the twentieth of November tomorrow?"

"Tell me."

"I would sign a lease of the beam house to myself for one shilling a year for ninety-nine years, and I would walk down there with a hammer and knock the padlock off with my own hands in front of the whole wharf, and I would hang that beam and weigh the first ton of coal that came over it, and let Mr. Tarrant take me to Chancery if he liked it." She had her hand flat on the bundle. "That is what I would sign. And I cannot, and I will tell you the reason I cannot, and the reason is not the law, the reason is that on the twenty-first of November I would be a woman who had broken her father's word in a public place, and every man on that wharf would know it, and the coal would go to Hull instead, because a wharfinger who cannot keep a promise is a wharfinger whose weights nobody will trust, and there is no trade in the world that dies faster than the weighing trade when the weights are doubted."

The fire went down. Neither of us put anything on it.

"Then the promise is the whole of it," I said.

"Whose promise?"

She looked up.

"My father's."

"Then it is not yours," I said, "and you have been keeping another person's promise for eleven years, and you have been keeping it so well that you have forgotten there was ever a person in it."

She sat very still.

"Say that again," she said, and her voice had gone careful, the way a person's voice goes when the floor has moved.

"You were ten," I said. "You have kept it since you were ten. A promise kept by a child on behalf of a dead man is not a promise, Miss Kell. It is a house you were left standing in, and you have not moved out of it, and I have come a hundred and eleven miles to stand in the door of it and tell you the roof is not yours."

She got up and walked to the window and stood with her back to me, and her shoulders were not shaking, and I have seen enough grief in my life to know that the shoulders that do not shake are the ones to be careful with.

"To-morrow," she said. "At the beam house. Nine o'clock."

"Nine o'clock."

"And Mrs. Rooke." She did not turn round. "If you are going to be at the beam house at nine, do not be at Mr. Tarrant's at seven."

"I will not."

"Then we will begin behind," she said, and it was my own words coming back at me out of a girl's mouth, and it did not sound like a joke any more.

## Five · The Assembly

Mrs. Sowerby put a shawl on the table and said I was to go.

"There is an assembly at the rooms on Wednesday and you will be in it."

"I have not been asked."

"Everybody is asked. It is a subscription and Mr. Tarrant has paid your shilling and if you do not go, the town will say you are here for the offices and ashamed to be seen with the people who own them." She spread the shawl out. "If you go, they will say you are here for the offices and not ashamed. The second is better."

"It is the same thing."

"It is the same thing said in a better voice," she said, "and in this town that is all the difference there is."

The shawl was black and good and had been her mother's, and she put it round my shoulders and stood back and looked at me with her head on one side, and then she took two pins out of her own collar and pinned it at the back so that it hung right.

"You have a good gown under it?"

"I have two."

"Wear the worse one," she said. "The better one will be counted against you. There is a Mrs. Bedloe in this town who can tell you the price of a woman's silk across a room and put it in her husband's book that night."

***

The assembly rooms were over the corn merchant's, up a wide stair with a red carpet that had been bought for the election of 1826 and never replaced, and there were two chandeliers with twenty candles in each and a platform for three musicians and a card table at the far end with four men at it before the first dance.

Forty people, perhaps. It is always forty. In a town of six thousand there are forty people who will pay a shilling to be seen in a room, and the other five thousand nine hundred and sixty are the reason the forty have anything to talk about.

I stood at the top of the stair with my shawl and my day book, which I had brought because I do not go anywhere without it, and I understood within a minute that I had made a mistake.

Not the book. The book was the whole of my standing. A woman may go into a room with nothing in her hand and she is a woman; a woman who goes in with a book and a pencil is a woman with a trade, and a trade excuses everything except being poor.

The mistake was the shawl.

There were three women by the door in silks that had come from Leeds, and they looked at Mrs. Sowerby's mother's black shawl the way a person looks at a thing they have decided not to mention, and one of them turned her shoulder a quarter turn, which I have seen done in Collow and in Thorneley and now here, and it is the same quarter turn in every town in England.

***

Judith came at half past eight.

She came up the stair with her hand on the rail and a woman in grey behind her who was the aunt or the chaperone or the woman who was paid to be one, and she had a white gown with a green sash and her hair done high, and she stopped at the top of the stair and looked down the room before she came into it.

That is a wharfinger's habit. You look at the water before you put the boat in.

Then she came in, and it was not four minutes before she was dancing.

She danced well. Not the way a girl dances who has been taught by a master, though she had been, because there was a master at Thorneley and she had gone twice a week for four years. She danced the way a person dances who has spent ten years standing on a wharf with men who are waiting for something, and who knows exactly how long a man will wait before he says a thing he will be sorry for.

She was dancing with a young man in a blue coat, and two older men stood at the side of the floor with their hands behind their backs and watched her the way men watch a horse being trotted out.

I went and stood near enough to hear them, because that is what I am for.

"Six thousand two hundred," one of them said.

"That is Tarrant's figure."

"That is Tarrant's figure and Tarrant's figure is Tarrant's figure. The crane wants a chain. The bridge wants two hundred."

"The bridge has wanted two hundred since 1819."

"And it will want it in 1849," the first man said. "You are not buying the bridge. You are buying the turn. Sixty-eight a year off the turn and it does not want a chain and it does not want two hundred and the road is the road."

"The turn is not hers to sell."

"The turn is hers to hold, and a husband holds what his wife holds, and you may put that in your pipe."

They both laughed. It was not a cruel laugh. That was the thing that made it worse. It was the laugh of two men discussing a field.

***

Bedloe came at nine.

I knew him before I knew his name, because he came in with the book.

He was a man of about fifty in a black coat that was better than any other coat in the room and worse than the coat of a man who did not need to be careful, and he had a clean white stock and a face that had been arranged for comfort. He came up the stair slowly, and he stopped and spoke to the woman at the door, and she laughed at something, and then he came on into the room carrying a quarto book under his left arm the way a man carries a hat.

Nobody in a ballroom carries a book. That is the whole of him. He carried it so that the room would see him carrying it and would understand that the room was not what he had come for.

He put it on the card table, and the four men at the card table made room for it, which told me more about Barrowfield than anything that had happened all evening.

Then he came and stood beside me.

"Mrs. Rooke," he said. "You will forgive me. I have wanted to meet you since Friday."

"Then you have had four days and a short walk."

"I have," he agreed. "I have spent them deciding what to say." He looked out at the floor. "You are the woman who answers for the dead."

"I am a woman who writes letters for the living."

"That is better said than what I had." He had a low voice that did not carry, which is a thing a man learns when he has spent thirty years in rooms where other men's money is decided. "You will not remember me, but I remember you. Collow, in the spring. There was a case in the consistory court and there was a woman who read a coat."

I did not say anything.

"I was not in the court," he said. "I read the report. It was in the ---" and he named a newspaper that I will not put down here, because I have never in my life bought it and I do not intend to be the reason anybody else does. "The report was three lines and one of them was that a woman had answered a question by putting her hand on a coat. I have thought about those three lines a great deal, Mrs. Rooke. Not the coat. The three lines. A thing can be done in a room and be in a newspaper in three lines, and the three lines are what the county gets, and the county is what a thing has to live with."

"And what did the three lines leave out?"

"Everything," he said, "which is the usual arrangement."

***

He gave me his arm for the next dance, and I took it, because a woman who refuses an arm in a room like that has made a speech, and I had not come to make speeches.

He danced correctly. He did not talk while he danced, which is a courtesy I had not expected, and when the dance was over he walked me to the side of the floor and stood with me and looked at Judith.

"Miss Kell is dancing with my clerk," he said.

"I did not know he was your clerk."

"He is not my clerk this evening. He is a young man in a blue coat, and he is a very good dancer, and he has been in my office since he was fourteen, and he will be an attorney himself in two years." He watched them turn. "I have three clerks and eight clerks' children and one of them is at Cambridge, and none of that is in the three lines either."

"Mr. Bedloe."

"Mrs. Rooke."

"What is in the book?"

He turned his head and looked at me for the first time without the arrangement on his face.

"That is not a question a person asks in a ballroom."

"It is the only question I have," I said, "and I have been in this town four days, and nobody has answered one yet."

He looked back at the floor.

"The book is a register of marriages," he said. "Not the parish register. My own. In this county a marriage that carries property is a marriage that has to be drawn, and I have drawn them for thirty-one years, and I keep a book of the ones I have drawn, with the property in it, because a man who draws a settlement and cannot remember what he put in it is a man who will one day be shown a paper he does not remember signing."

"And Miss Kell is in it."

"Miss Kell has been in it since 1821."

I made myself keep my eyes on the dancers.

"She was ten."

"She was ten," he said, "and her father was four days dead, and her guardian came to me on the twenty-third of December with a mortgage, a fire, six bodies, and a girl, and asked me what was to be done. I told him. He did it. That is the whole of the wickedness, Mrs. Rooke, and you will find it very dull, because there is no villain in it. There is a man with a bad position and a lawyer with a good one."

"You were the witness to the covenant."

The music stopped for the interval and the room made the noise a room makes when forty people all speak at once.

"Yes," he said. "I was the witness."

"Did you write it?"

"I drew it. There is a difference and the difference is three guineas." He said it without any shame at all, which I have come to respect in a man, though I do not recommend it. "Her father came to my office with the terms in his own hand and I put them into form, and he read the form twice, and he signed it, and I witnessed it, and I was paid for it, and the money came out of the nine hundred, which is a thing I have never liked and have never been asked about."

"Nine hundred pounds that cleared the mortgage."

"Nine hundred pounds that cleared the mortgage, and it did not come from Tarrant, whatever Tarrant has told you, or rather it came from Tarrant through me and I will not say more about it in a room with a card table in it." He turned and looked at me. "You want a thing from me. Say what it is."

"I want to know what the covenant says."

"You have not read it?"

"Not yet."

"Then read it," he said, "and when you have read it, come to my office, and I will tell you what it means, and I will not charge you, and you will not like the reason I am not charging you."

"What is the reason?"

"Because in thirty-one years I have drawn four hundred settlements and I have never once been asked what a thing means by somebody who had read it first."

***

I got away from him at the interval and went to the card table.

The book was there, closed, with a brass clasp and no lock. I did not open it. I put my hand on it, which is a thing you can do in a room with a book, and I stood there with my hand flat on the leather and my eyes on the dancers, and I let it come.

Three seconds.

Ink. Not the ink of a letter. Iron-gall ink that has been in a bottle a long time, and under it the smell of a coat that has been in a room with a fire and a wet dog, and the cold of a table under a hand that has been writing since eight in the morning.
