And then a page, one page, and a column of numbers down the left side, and the numbers were three figures and they were not in the same hand as the rest of the page. They were smaller and they had been put in later, and one of them had been written over another one, so that you could see the old figure under the new one like a stain.

Then it was gone, and my hand was on a book on a card table, and the second violin was tuning, and a woman behind me said something about the weather to another woman and neither of them had seen me do it.

I took my hand off.

I have seen those numbers before. Not these numbers. That shape of number, in that position, in a column at the side of a page whose business is not numbering. I have seen it in a room in Collow in a book that was not supposed to exist, and I have seen it since in a copy of a copy, and I have never once found a person who could tell me what they were for.

I went and stood by the window where the air was better.

***

Bedloe came to find me at the end, when the shawls were being got out and the musicians were packing.

"Mrs. Rooke." He had his book under his arm again. "I will say one thing to you and then I will go home."

"Say it."

"You will read the covenant, and you will find that it is a good piece of drafting, and you will find that it does exactly what it says, and then you will go and look for the man who made her father sign it." He put his hat on. "There is no such man. There is a wharf that could not pay, and a fire, and a town that wanted its coal weighed cheap, and a guardian with nine hundred pounds and no paper. That is all there is. You may spend the winter looking for a villain and you will find a bad year."

"And the short weight?"

He stopped with his hat in his hand.

"Say that again."

"The hundredweight that comes to ninety-eight. The eleventh basket written as ten. The difference paid out of a shopkeeper's till for two years because the licence is in Mr. Tarrant's gift." I had my back to the window and the cold coming through it. "That is not a bad year, Mr. Bedloe. That is a decision somebody makes every morning at eight o'clock."

He looked at me for a long moment. The arrangement came back onto his face, and behind it he was doing arithmetic, and I could not see the numbers.

"You have been four days in this town," he said, "and you have found the one thing in it that everybody knows."

"Everybody knows?"

"Every man on that wharf. Every woman who buys a sack." He put his hat on and settled it. "Do you know what happens to a town when everybody knows a thing and nobody says it? I will tell you, because I have watched it twice. Nothing happens. It goes on for eleven years. The weights are short and the coal is dear and the fees are paid and the bridge does not get its two hundred, and the men who could say it are the men who are buying and selling, and they would each of them lose by saying it, and so they say it to their wives."

"And you?"

"I draw the papers," he said. "I have drawn the papers for eleven years, and I have never once been asked to draw a paper about the weight, and if I were asked I would draw it, because that is what I am." He went to the door and turned. "You are wondering why I have told you all this."

"I am."

"Because you will read that covenant, and you will find the hand in it, and you will want to say a thing out loud in a public place, and when you do, this town will close like a fist, and the girl will be the one who is squeezed." He put his hand on the door. "I have seen that too. Twice."

"Mr. Bedloe."

"Mrs. Rooke."

"Your book has numbers in the margin that are not in your hand."

He did not move for about the length of a breath.

"Every office in this county has a number," he said. "It is a convenience. Ask at the Shire Hall and they will show you a book with eleven hundred of them in it and they will charge you a shilling to look."

"And the ones written over other ones?"

"Then somebody made a mistake and corrected it," he said, "which is what numbers are for."

He opened the door and the cold came in and the candles leaned.

"Mrs. Rooke. One thing more, and then I am done." He had his hat on and his hand on the latch. "You answer for the dead. What do you take for it?"

"Nothing."

"Nothing." He said the word as if he were weighing it. "Then you are the only person in Barrowfield who is not being paid. We have dead here too, Mrs. Rooke. They are not buried. They are still paying."

Then he went down the stair, and I heard him say something to the man at the bottom that made the man laugh, and the door shut, and the room was forty people getting into their shawls.

***

Judith came past me on her way out with the woman in grey.

She stopped.

"You did not dance," she said.

"I do not dance."

"You stood by the window with Mr. Bedloe for a quarter of an hour and then you stood by the book." She had her shawl up over her shoulders and her chin was up. "I watched you. Everybody watched you. Half of them think you are his woman now."

"Let them."

"Mrs. Rooke." She came a step closer. "What is in the book?"

I looked at her, and I thought about a thing I had decided in Collow, which was that I would not tell a person a thing they had not asked for, because a truth given away is a truth nobody has to carry.

"Your name," I said. "Since 1821."

She stood very still with her shawl in her hands.

"And the rest of it?"

"I do not know yet."

"Then find out," she said, "and do not tell me in a room with forty people in it."

She went down the stair. I stood at the top and watched her go, and beside me the corn merchant's man was putting out the candles two at a time with a brass snuffer on a pole, and the room went from gold to grey in the order of the ceiling.

## Six · What the Town Weighs

The shop opened at six and by half past six there were three women in it and a boy, and by seven there was a queue on the step, because in a coal town the first cold morning of October empties every purse in the street.

I stood behind the counter with Mrs. Sowerby because she had said, on the Tuesday, that a woman who sits in the back room all day is a woman the street learns to walk past.

The coal was in a bin at the end of the counter, under a sloping lid, and it came out of the bin in a scoop and went into a sack that was hung on a hook on a beam over the counter, and the beam had a sliding weight on it and a brass face with the marks on it, and when the sack was full she slid the weight along until the beam came up level and then she read the mark.

The mark she read was always one hundred.

"Sevenpence," she said, and took the money, and the woman took the sack and went out.

That happened eleven times between six and eight. I counted because I count. Eleven sacks, eleven times sevenpence, six and fivepence, and the beam coming up level, and the brass face with the marks on it, and the sliding weight stopping at the same place every time.

At eight there was a gap and I said, "May I weigh one?"

She looked at me over the counter.

"You may weigh the sugar," she said. "You may weigh the flour. You may not weigh the coal."

"Why not?"

"Because if you weigh the coal and it comes to ninety-eight, I have to look at you, and I have been not looking at people for two years and I am very good at it now." She wiped the counter. "Weigh the sugar."

I weighed the sugar.

The sugar came to a pound and a quarter ounce over, which is a fair pound, and the flour came to fourteen pounds on the nose, and the tea was short by three quarters of an ounce and she took a pinch out of the tin and put it in the paper without my saying anything, because she had known.

"Your beam is true," I said.

"My beam is true. I bought it in 1829 from a man in Wakefield and I paid eleven shillings for it and I have never once wished I had paid ten." She put the tea on the shelf. "It is not my beam that is wrong, Mrs. Rooke. It is what goes into the sack."

***

At nine a woman came in with a child on her hip and asked for a hundredweight and put down sevenpence.

She was maybe thirty and she had the look of a woman who has been up since four and will be up at four tomorrow, and the child was asleep against her shoulder with its mouth open.

Mrs. Sowerby filled the sack and hung it and slid the weight and the beam came up level and she read one hundred and took the money.

"Mrs. Sowerby," I said, when the woman had gone.

"Not in front of the street."

She waited until the queue was done and the boy had gone to the yard for more coal, and then she turned the card round in the window and shut the door and bolted it and stood with her back against it.

"Out with it."

"I want to weigh a sack."

"You will not weigh one of mine."

"I want to weigh one of mine." I had my purse out. "I am going to buy a hundredweight off the wharf this morning and I am going to carry it up this hill and I am going to put it on your beam, and you are going to be standing here at the end of it, whatever it comes to, and after that nobody in this town can say I said a thing I did not weigh."

She looked at me for a long moment.

"You will want the boy to carry it."

"I will carry it."

"It is a hundredweight."

"I have carried worse."

***

The wharf sold retail at the crane, out of a cart, sevenpence for a hundredweight and a halfpenny back if you brought your own sack, and the man who sold it was the man with the board who had written eleven on Friday.

He did not know me. That is to say, he knew me as a woman who had stood on the wharf, and he did not know me as anything that could do him harm, which is the condition of most people in most towns and the reason most towns go on as they do.

"A hundredweight," I said.

He filled the sack from the cart and hung it on the yard beam. The yard beam was a big one, iron, on a frame, with a pan and a sliding poise, and the poise was bright where a hand had worn it.

The beam went up. He read the mark.

"A hundred and twelve," he said. "Sevenpence, and give me the halfpenny if you want the sack."

I paid him sevenpence and kept the sack, which cost me the halfpenny, and I put the sack on my shoulder and walked out of the yard.

At the gate I stopped and put it down and looked back.

He was already filling the next one.

I carried it up the hill. A hundredweight on that beam is not a hundredweight, and I knew it before I got to the market cross, because I have carried a hundredweight of coal before and a hundredweight sits in the shoulder in a particular way, and this one did not sit in it.

***

Mrs. Sowerby's beam said ninety-eight.

We did it twice. The second time she took the sack off the hook and put it on again herself and moved the poise herself, and the beam came up level at ninety-eight and a half, and she stood with her hand on the poise and did not say anything for a while.

"Fourteen," she said at last. "I have been saying ninety-eight for two years and I have never once said the number fourteen."

"What is the price of a ton at the crane?"

"Eleven shillings. It was nine in 1821 and it is eleven now and it will be twelve by January."

"And a hundredweight at retail?"

"Sevenpence. It should be sixpence farthing and it is sevenpence, and I have been putting the farthing in the till and calling it my own wickedness."

"Then let us do the sum." I got out the day book. "A hundredweight sold at sevenpence is eleven shillings and eightpence the ton. A hundredweight bought at the crane costs eleven shillings the ton, and that is if a ton is a ton."

"It is not a ton."

"It is not a ton. That beam is a seventh part heavy, and a seventh part of ninety-eight is fourteen, and fourteen and ninety-eight is a hundred and twelve, and a hundred and twelve is what he read out and a hundredweight is what he wrote." I put the pencil down. "So a ton at the crane is nineteen hundred and sixty pounds of coal written up as a ton, and you pay eleven shillings for nineteen hundred and sixty pounds, and at sevenpence the hundredweight you can put seventeen and a half hundredweight over your counter for ten and twopence halfpenny, and that is ninepence halfpenny gone on every ton you buy."

She sat down on the stool behind the counter.

"Say it in money," she said.

"In money, every ton you buy costs you eleven shillings and puts seventeen and a half hundredweight on your floor, and seventeen and a half at sevenpence is ten and twopence halfpenny, and the ninepence halfpenny comes out of your own till, and you have been doing it since March last year, and it comes to four pounds eleven." I looked at the page. "Which means you have been selling at a loss on every sack and calling the loss your own cheating."

"I have."

"You have not. You have been paying a tax to a beam."

She put her hands over her face and sat like that, and the shop was quiet, and outside a cart went past on the cobbles with a noise like a shutter being shaken.

"I want to tell you a thing," she said, from behind her hands, "and then I want you to tell me what to do, and if you tell me to go on doing it I will go on doing it, because I have been doing it for two years and I am tired."

"Tell me."

"In November the coal comes dear," she said. "It always comes dear. The boats are slow and the roads are bad and every woman in this street buys a hundredweight in October and another in November and another in January, and in January they buy the most, because January is when the money is shortest and the cold is longest, and they buy a hundredweight and they carry it home and they burn it and they are still cold." She took her hands down. "Now go and weigh the first sack of November and weigh the first sack of January and tell me if they are the same."

I looked at her.

"They will not be the same."

"No," she said. "They will not be the same. I do not know why and I have never wanted to know why, because the man who sells me the coal is a good man and he has a wife and six children and he does not set the beam, he only reads it." She got up. "But there is a thing I have wondered for eleven years and I have never said it out loud, and I will say it to you because you have carried a hundredweight up my hill."

"Say it."

"Who sets the beam?"

***

I went to Kell's at eleven with the sack still on my shoulder.

Judith was in the room with the blue cloth with the fourteen books out on it and a pot of ink and a pen, and she looked up when I came in and then she looked at the sack.

"What is that?"

"A hundredweight of your coal."

"It is not my coal. I have not sold coal since my father died." She put the pen down. "I let the crane and I take the fee and the coal is other men's coal."

"Then it is a hundredweight of other men's coal that went over a beam on your wharf this morning reading a hundred and twelve and came to ninety-eight on Mrs. Sowerby's beam at the top of this hill." I put it down on the floor between us. "I want to see the measure."

She did not move.

"What measure?"

"The office of the measure. It is the eighth of the fourteen. It is eight pounds a year and it is yours and it is in that bundle on the table, and an office of the measure is not a right to charge eight pounds, it is a right and a duty to keep the standard, and the standard is a set of weights with the county stamp on them, and they are in this house."

She sat looking at me.

"How do you know they are in this house?"

"Because every office of the measure in England has them in the holder's house," I said. "Because they cost eleven pounds in 1711 and no man leaves eleven pounds of brass in a shed on a wharf. Because you have not opened that cupboard in eleven years and you have not sold it either, and a thing a person has neither used nor sold is a thing they are afraid of."

She got up and went out of the room, and I heard her go up the stair, and she was gone four minutes, and when she came back she had a wooden case under her arm with a rope handle, and she put it on the blue cloth and did not open it.

"It has a lock," she said. "The key is in the kitchen drawer. I have known where the key is since I was twelve."

"Open it."

"I would rather you did."

"No," I said. "It is yours. If I open it, it is a thing that was done to you. Open it."

She stood there with her hand on the lid of the case for about the length of a breath, and then she went out and came back with the key and turned it and lifted the lid.

Brass. Nine pieces, in sockets cut into the wood, each one with a handle and a ring, and every one stamped on the top with a crown and a date and three letters, and the brass was dull and had a green bloom on it in the hollows and there was no dust in the sockets at all, because the lid had been shut.

"Fifty-six," she said. "That is the biggest. Fifty-six pounds, and then twenty-eight, and fourteen, and seven, and four, and two, and one, and eight ounces, and four ounces. My father had them out once a year and he weighed the shop weights in the market against them, every September, and he used to make me hold the fifty-six with both hands because he said a child who has held the standard does not grow up cheating."

"And since 1821?"

"Since 1821 they have been in that case." She touched the biggest one with one finger. "The measure is eight pounds a year and I have taken the eight pounds every Michaelmas and I have never once done the thing the eight pounds is for."

I looked at the brass and the brass looked like brass, which is what a standard looks like when nobody has consulted it.

"Then we will do it," I said. "Not today. Not in the market with a crowd. But when the first sack of November comes over that wharf, I am going to buy it and I am going to bring it here and we are going to weigh it against that fifty-six, and then we are going to write the number down."

She shut the lid.

"And in January?"

"And in January we will weigh the first sack of January, and there will be two numbers, and one of them will be worse than the other, and when a thing gets worse it stops being weather and starts being a decision."

She turned the key in the lock.

"Mrs. Rooke," she said. "You have been in this town six days."

"Six days."

"In six days you have found the beam, the case, and the difference. I have lived in this house for eleven years." She put the key on the table. "Why could I not find them?"

I thought about how to say it, and then I said it the plain way, because she was a woman who had been handled gently for eleven years and it had done her no good at all.

"Because you were the thing being weighed," I said. "A person on the scale cannot read the scale. That is not a failing, it is a position."

She stood with her hand on the case.

"And you are not on the scale."

"I am on it," I said. "I am on it in Collow and I am on it here. But I am not the thing this town came to price, and there is a difference between standing on a scale and being the goods."

***

I went back down the hill with the sack on my shoulder because I had paid for it and I was not going to leave sevenpence on a wharf.

At the bottom of the street the woman with the child on her hip was standing at her door with her hands wrapped in her apron, and the door was open, and behind her there was a room with a grate in it and no fire in the grate.

She looked at the sack on my shoulder and then at my face.

"You bought one," she said.

"I bought one."

"From the crane?"

"From the crane." I put it down. "It is yours if you want it. I have weighed it and I have written the weight down and I do not need it any more."

She did not answer at once. The child on her hip turned its head and coughed, and it was not a bad cough, it was the cough of a child who has been in a cold room for a fortnight and will be in it for a fortnight more.

"I cannot take it," she said.

"Why not?"

"Because Mrs. Craddock at the end will see me take it, and by Friday it will be a thing I was given, and by Sunday it will be a thing I asked for." She hitched the child up. "You are the letter-writer."

"I am."

"Then write me a paper that says I bought it." She said it without any shame and without any hope. "Write me a paper and I will give you a penny for it, and I have a penny."

I got out the day book, and I wrote on a clean page, in my best hand, that on the twenty-fourth of October 1832 one hundredweight of coal had been sold at the crane for sevenpence and delivered at the door of Mrs. Anne Craddock's yard, number nine, and that the buyer was Mrs. Ellen Sunderland, and I signed it and dated it and tore it out and gave it her.

She read it. It took her a long time, moving her lips.

"That is not what I asked for," she said.

"It is better than what you asked for," I said. "A paper that says you were given it is a paper that says you could not buy it. A paper that says you bought it is a receipt, and a receipt is a thing a woman can put in a drawer."

She folded it and put it inside her dress and took the sack by the neck and dragged it in through her own door, and she did not say thank you, and I have been given a great many thanks in my life and I would rather have had that.

***

That night I sat at the table in the room over the shop with a candle and a sheet of paper and the rain coming on the window.

I had written to Rooke three times since I came and every letter had been four lines and every one of them had been about the weather and the road and the price of a bed, and I had told myself it was because the post was slow and there was no use in writing what would be stale before it arrived.

That is not the reason. The reason is that I have never in my life written down a thing that mattered without putting my hand over the paper at the end of it, and a letter does not allow that.

So I wrote him the beam.

I wrote that a hundred and twelve pounds goes over a scale on a wharf and is written as a hundredweight, and that a hundredweight comes to ninety-eight at the top of the hill, and that a woman in a shop has been making the difference up out of her own till for two years and calling it her own cheating, and that eleven years of it comes to a number I had not yet worked out but I was going to.

Then I wrote:

*I am well. The room is small and the woman who keeps the shop is honest and I have not been asked to read anything yet. Tell me what the office is doing and whether the roof held in the rain.*

And then, because it was late and the rain was on the window and I had carried a hundredweight up a hill that day:

*I have thought about the inn at Thorneley more than I have thought about the beam, which is not a thing I ought to put in a letter that goes through a postmaster's hands in a county where they read everything, and so I have not put it in.*

I looked at that for a while. Then I took the sheet by the corner and held it to the candle until it caught, and I dropped it in the empty grate and watched it go, and the flame ran up the paper in a line the way flame does, taking the writing faster than it took the blank part.

Then I wrote a clean sheet with nothing on it but the beam and the room and the roof, and I sealed it, and I sat with my hands flat on the table and did not write the other thing again.

## Seven · The Covenant

The room her father had used was on the first floor at the back, over the counting house, and it had a window that looked down on the basin and a desk with a slope to it and a chair with arms, and it had not been swept since the summer.

I know it had not been swept because there was a line of dust along the edge of the desk where a hand had not been.

"He would not have it swept," Judith said. "He used to say a swept office is an office where a man has nothing to do. And after, nobody went in, and then it got to be a thing, and now Mrs. Sowerby does it twice a year with the door open and does not touch the desk."

She stood in the door with a lamp in her hand.

"You wanted to see the covenant," she said. "It is in the desk."

"Then get it out."

"I have not opened that desk in eleven years."

"Then open it now, and I will stand here, and I will not help you."

She looked at me.

"You are a hard woman, Mrs. Rooke."

"I am a woman who has been in this town eight days," I said, "and I have learned that everything here is done gently and nothing here is done, and I have no time for gentle."

She went in and put the lamp on the desk and stood in front of it.

Then she opened the top drawer and took out a key, and unlocked the second drawer, and took out a tin box about the size of a family Bible, painted black, with a paper label on it in a hand that was not hers.

She put it on the desk and lifted the lid.

There were four papers in it. She took out the top one and put it on the slope of the desk and stood back.

"Read it," she said.

"I will read it when I have looked at it." I sat down in the chair with arms, which was a thing I had not been invited to do, and I put my hands flat on the desk either side of the paper and I looked.

It was parchment, not paper. Two skins, folded once, with the fold gone soft and dirty along the crease. It had been folded in four for the box and it did not want to lie flat, and the corners curled up like a thing breathing. There was a stamp at the top left in the corner, blue, a shilling and sixpence, and the ink of the stamp had gone brown at the edges.

The writing was a clerk's hand, small and upright and even, and it filled the first skin and half the second, and there was a schedule at the foot of the second skin in a different hand, and below the schedule there were the signatures.

I did not read a word of it.

I want that understood, because it matters. I sat in a dead man's chair with my hands on either side of a covenant worth six thousand pounds and I did not read it. I looked at it the way a person looks at a face before they speak to it.

The ink. Iron-gall, and there was a place in the middle of the first skin where the ink had gone grey, and the grey began at the top of a line and went to the bottom of the page and then stopped, and after it the ink was black again. That is a pen that was dipped and wrote four lines and ran dry and was dipped again. That is nothing. That is a clerk with a bad bottle.

The edges. Somebody had ruled the margin lines with a straight edge and a dry point, and you could feel them with a thumbnail, and the point had gone through the parchment in one place, at the top of the second skin, so that there was a little tear the size of a flea.

The fold. It had been folded before the ink was dry. There was a transfer, faint, a mirror of four words, on the inside of the fold. I did not turn it into the light to read them. I noted that they were there.

The signatures. Three, and a seal. The seal was red and it had been pressed with a ring, not a matrix, because a matrix makes a clean edge and a ring makes a clean edge only if the hand is still. The wax had run a little at the bottom and somebody had put a thumb in it while it was warm.

"The other papers," I said.

"The mortgage. The discharge of the mortgage. And a copy of the burial entry, which I do not know why he kept."

"Put them out."

She put them out in a row, and I looked at them the way I had looked at the first, and the mortgage was on paper and had been folded small, and the discharge was on paper and had a stamp on it and a receipt for nine hundred pounds written across the face of it in a hand I did not know, and the burial entry was a copy in a clerk's hand and it said, in the plainest language in the world, that Amos Kell, wharfinger, of Barrowfield, aged forty-six, had been buried on the twenty-second of December 1821.

I sat with that for a moment.

"Your father was forty-six."

"Yes."

"Then he was born in 1775."

"I suppose he was."

"You suppose." I looked up. "You have had eleven years and a box of papers and you have never once written out your father's dates."

She stood with her hands at her sides.

"Why would I write them out?"

"Because a person who does not know the dates of the dead is a person who cannot tell when a thing that happened to them happened." I turned the burial copy round. "I am not scolding you. I am telling you what I need. I need the dates, and I need them in your hand, and I need them before I read that parchment, because when I read a thing I bring a question to it and the question has to be sharp."

She went to the desk and took a sheet and dipped the pen and wrote, and her hand was steady, and when she had done she turned it round and pushed it across.

Amos Kell, born the third of May 1775, at Selby.
Married the eleventh of September 1808, at Barrowfield, to Anne Fenwick.
Judith Kell, born the twentieth of November 1811.
Anne Kell, died the fourth of March 1819.
Amos Kell, died the nineteenth of December 1821.

I read it twice.

"Your mother died in 1819."

"In the March. She was thirty-one." Judith put the pen down. "There was a fever in the town and it took nine people and she was one of them, and my father did not speak for a month, and then he stopped the clock in the hall and he never wound it again, and it is still stopped, and I have not wound it either."

"The clock is at four."

"Twenty past four. She died at twenty past four in the morning and he stopped it at twenty past four in the morning and he made the man wait until it was the same minute."

I looked at the paper with the dates on it.

"Thank you," I said. "Now I will tell you what I am going to do, and I want you to listen to the whole of it before you answer, because it is not a thing you will like."

"Go on."

"I am going to read this parchment, and I am going to read it tomorrow, in the beam house, with the door open, and not in this room." I put my hand flat on the desk. "Today I have touched it and I know what it is made of and I know how many hands have been on it. Tomorrow I will bring a question and I will take the answer. That is how it is done. A thing read twice in two days is a thing read badly, and a thing read in a dead man's chair is a thing read in a dead man's chair."

She had gone white about the mouth.

"Read it now."

"No."

"Mrs. Rooke." She came round the desk. "You do not understand what you are asking me to do. You are asking me to put that parchment back in the box and shut the lid and go down those stairs and eat my dinner and go to bed, and I have not done that for eleven years without knowing what is in it, and I will not do it now that there is somebody in the house who can tell me."

"You have read it."

"I have read it four hundred times."

"Then you know what it says."

"I know what it says!" It came out of her loud, and the lamp moved, and she put her hand over her mouth, and then she took it away. "I know every word of it. I could stand in a court and recite it. It says my father bound himself, his heirs, his executors, and his assigns, that the beam in the beam house on the wharf at Barrowfield should be for ever shut and should not be used for the weighing of any coal, grain, or merchandise whatsoever, and that in consideration of the sum of nine hundred pounds paid to him by Whitworth and Company of Hull he did covenant and agree that neither he nor any person claiming under him should open the said beam or permit the same to be opened, and it says that if he did, the said Whitworth and Company should be entitled to recover such damages as should be assessed, and it is dated the fourteenth of October 1821, and it is signed by my father in a good round hand, and it is witnessed by Mr. Bedloe, and I could tell you the width of the margin."

The fourteenth of October in 1821 fell on a Sunday. I did not say it to her. A deed dated on the Lord's day is a deed dated for a day when every office in England is shut and every man who might ask a question is in church, and I have done enough accounts to know that lawyers do not choose a Sunday by mistake.

The room was quiet. Below the window a boat worked the lock and the water went down in the chamber.

"Then you know what it says," I said, "and you still do not know what it means, and that is the difference between us and that is why you are going to put it back in the box."

She sat down in the chair by the window.

"I am sorry I shouted."

"You are not. You are sorry that I did not move." I got up. "Miss Kell, I have a thing to tell you and it is not a kindness. In eleven years you have not once read that parchment with a question. You have read it with a wound. A person reading with a wound reads the same three words over and over and the rest of the page goes dark. That is why you can recite it and cannot say what is in it."

"What is in it?"

"I do not know yet. Tomorrow, in the beam house, with the door open." I stood over her. "And there is one thing in it you have told me already without knowing it."

"What?"

"You said your father signed it in a good round hand." I put my finger on the parchment, on the signatures. "Look at it. That is not a good round hand. That is a hand that has been writing since six in the morning and is tired, and there is a place in the middle of the name where the pen stopped and went on, which is a thing that happens when a man lifts the pen to think."

She looked at it.

"He signed it at the end of a day."

"He signed it at the end of a day, after a clerk had read him four skins of drafting, and he signed the counterpart first and the deed second, because the deed is the one that goes to the other party and a man always signs the one that leaves the room last." I straightened up. "Now put it in the box."

She put it in the box.

***

We went down and ate dinner, which was a piece of mutton and a dish of potatoes, and neither of us said anything for the whole of it, and the servant with the pinned sleeves came and went and looked at us both and asked nothing, which is the mark of a good servant and was in this case also the mark of a town that had decided to wait.

At the end she said, "I will walk down with you."

The wharf at night is a different animal. The heaps are the same heaps and the crane is the same crane, and there are lanterns at the lock and a man with a pole, and the water makes the only noise there is, and there is a smell that comes up off the wet coal at night that is not a bad smell, it is the smell of a thing that has been rained on for a hundred years.

We stood by the beam house with the padlock and the chain.

"I have to tell you a thing," Judith said, "and I have been saying it to myself since the assembly, and I would rather say it here than in the house."

"Say it."

"If you read that covenant and you find that he signed it himself, in his own hand, knowing what it said, I am going to burn it."

I turned and looked at her.

"You will not."

"I will." She had her hands in her shawl and her chin up and she was not a girl saying a thing she had not thought about. "I have thought about this for eleven years, Mrs. Rooke, and I have thought about it properly, which is more than most people do with a thing they are afraid of. If he signed it, then my father sold this town's weights for nine hundred pounds and a mortgage, and the six men who died in that warehouse died because there was no beam and the coal was going over a short one, and their wives are still in this parish, and I have been giving them coal out of the yard at Christmas for eleven years and calling it charity." She said it without any trembling at all. "I will not live in a house with that paper in the desk. I will burn it, and I will go on keeping the promise, and no one will ever know there was a question."

"And if he did not sign it?"

"Then I will know that too."

"And if he signed it with another man's hand on his?"

She was quiet.

"That is not one of the two," she said.

"No," I said. "It is the third, and it is the one nobody ever thinks of, and it is the one I am going to look for, because I have seen it before."

She stood looking at the padlock.

"Then do not tell me which of the three it is," she said. "Not at once. Tell me the thing and let me sit with it, and do not put your hand on my shoulder while I do."

"You will want to know at once."

"I will want to know at once," she agreed, "and I will hate you for telling me, and I will hate you more for not telling me, and there is no arrangement of it that is comfortable, and I am not asking for comfortable. I am asking for the room to be quiet when you say it."

I looked at the padlock and the bright screws.

"Miss Kell," I said. "What is your father's name in that covenant?"

"Amos Kell."

"His whole name?"

"Amos Kell, of Barrowfield, in the county of, wharfinger."

"No middle name."

"He had no middle name."

"Then that is the first thing to look at," I said, "because a man signing a thing he does not want to sign signs it the way he signs a docket, and a man signing a thing he has decided to sign signs it the way he signs his will."

She looked at me.

"You are telling me that the hand will tell me which."

"I am telling you that the hand is the only witness in this town who cannot be got at," I said. "Everybody else in Barrowfield has a reason. That parchment does not."

***

I went up to the room over the shop and lit the candle and sat down with the day book and wrote out the whole of it in the order I had seen it, because a thing you have seen once and not written down is a thing you have half seen.

*Parchment, two skins, folded in four, folded before the ink was dry. Transfer on the inner fold, four words, not read.*
*Stamp, 1s 6d, top left, blue, gone brown.*
*Ink grey from the top of a line to the foot of a page and black again after. One dip.*
*Ruled margins, dry point, gone through at the head of the second skin. A tear the size of a flea.*
*Seal, pressed with a ring and not a matrix. Wax run at the bottom. A thumb in it while warm.*
*Signatures three. Amos Kell, in his own hand, at the end of a day, pen lifted once in the middle of the name.*
*Counterpart here. Deed with Whitworth. Bedloe witnessed both.*

Then I turned the page and wrote the question, and I wrote it the way I write a question I mean to carry into a thing, which is short and in the plainest words and with nothing in it that I want to be true.

*Did Amos Kell put his own hand to that parchment, and did he know what he was doing when he did it?*

It is a short question. It took me a long time to get it that short.

Then I wrote underneath it, smaller:

*And if he did, was there a man in the room who could have stopped him and did not?*

I blew the candle out and lay in the dark and listened to the rain, and I thought about a thing that had been at the back of my head since the desk, which was that there had been four papers in that box and no letter. A man signs away a town's weights for nine hundred pounds and there is no letter. A man is pressed for money in October and dead in December and there is no letter.

Either he was a man who did not write letters, or somebody had been through that box before the lid was shut.

## Eight · The Fire

The parish church at Barrowfield is a low church with a square tower and a clock that works, and it stands on the rise above the market cross with the churchyard going down the slope in rows, and the oldest stones at the top and the newest at the bottom, which is the way of every churchyard in England and means that a person walking down is walking forward in time.

I went on the Monday morning and asked the clerk for the register, and he asked me what I wanted it for, and I said I was a widow looking for a burial, which is a lie I have used before and which works because it is the one lie nobody asks a woman to prove.

He brought the book to the vestry and put it on the table and stood there.

"You may go," I said.

"I am the clerk."

"And I am a woman with a book, and we will get on better apart."

He went, and I opened the register at the winter of 1821, and the ink on that page was the ink of a man who had written a great deal in a short time.

Six of them, in five days.

*Dec 20. Thomas Hogg, of this parish, labourer, aged 31.*
*Dec 20. William Rennard, of this parish, labourer, aged 24.*
*Dec 20. Joseph Rennard, of this parish, labourer, aged 19.*
*Dec 21. Sarah Rennard, of this parish, widow, aged 51.*
*Dec 21. Peter Thwaite, of this parish, keelman, aged 38.*
*Dec 22. Amos Kell, of this parish, wharfinger, aged 46.*

I sat and looked at it for a while.

Two Rennards on the same day and a third the next, and the third was the mother, and a woman does not die of a fire two days after her sons unless she was in it too or went into it after them. The clerk had written her down as widow, which meant that in the three days between the fire and the burial he had had to decide what she was, and had decided she was a widow, and had not written that she had died of anything at all.

Five days, six burials, and a man's hand getting worse as it went down the page, and at the bottom of the page nothing. No note. No inquiry. No coroner's name.

I turned back a page and forward a page. In the whole of that winter there was no entry for an inquest.

***

The graves were at the bottom of the slope, in a row, because six at once is a row.

They were good stones. That was the first thing. Good stone costs money and a labourer's family does not have it, and these were matched, all six, with the same cutting and the same lettering, and there was no verse on any of them and no "beloved" and no "in the midst of life."

I stood at the end of the row and read them.

*Also of the above, Sarah Rennard, who died of the same.*

That was on the third stone, under her sons.

I looked down the slope at the town, at the basin and the wharf and the beam house with its padlock, and I thought about a man who pays for six stones and has no verse cut on any of them.

A man who does that is not grieving. A man who does that is closing a file.

***

The almshouse is the last of the fourteen offices and it is worth one pound six a year and it is a row of four rooms at the back of the churchyard with a pump in the middle of the yard and a garden that grows onions and nothing else.

The office is a duty, not a fee. I had read that in the folio at Tarrant's, in the column at the bottom that said *almshouse, 1 6s, in the gift of the holder, no fine*, and I had thought at the time that one pound six a year is a strange price for a duty, and now I understood that the pound six was not the price of the duty. It was the price of the coal.

There were two people in the almshouse. One was a woman of about forty who did not know what year it was, and the other was a man of about forty-five sitting on a stool in the doorway of the second room with his hands in his lap.

His hands were the reason he was sitting there.

They were not hands. They were a shape that had been a hand and had healed the way a thing heals when nobody has anything better to put on it, and the fingers on the right one were gone to the middle joint, and the left one had a thumb and two fingers and the rest was smooth.

"Mr. Hogg," I said.

He looked up.

"There is only one Hogg in here," he said. "The other Hogg is in the churchyard and he has been there eleven years and he is better off."

"I am Mrs. Rooke."

"I know who you are. You are the woman from the shop who carried a hundredweight up the hill." He shifted on the stool. "Sit down. There is a bench and it is out of the wind and if you sit on the end of it you will not have to look at my hands."

I sat on the end of the bench.

"I want to ask you about the night of the nineteenth of December."

"I know you do." He said it without surprise and without any particular interest, the way a man says a thing he has said to himself for a long time. "Eleven years and no one has asked me. The parson has not asked me. Mr. Tarrant has not asked me, and Mr. Tarrant pays for this room, and he has been very good about it, and he has never once asked me. Now a woman comes with a book and asks me in the first minute."

"Will you tell me?"

"What will you give me?"

"Nothing."

He looked up at that, and the corners of his mouth moved, and it was not a smile, it was the beginning of one that he had decided not to finish.

"Good," he said. "Then I will tell you, because everybody who has ever listened to me has wanted something and I have got tired of working out what."

***

He told it in the order a man tells a thing he has been over a thousand times, which is not the order it happened.

"There was a boat in at six. The *Providence*, out of Selby, six ton and a half. It went into the warehouse because the yard was full and the crane was wanted for the morning, and the coal went in loose, not sacked, and it went into the north end, and it was still going in at nine when I came off."

"You were on the wharf until nine."

"I was on the wharf until twenty past eleven." He held up the left hand, the one with the thumb. "I will tell you why I know the time. There is a clock at the lock house and it strikes the quarters and it is loud, and I heard the three quarters and I heard the hour, and I was still there at the hour, because I had a thing to say to the night man and he had not come."

"What thing?"

"A thing about the boat. The boat's man had left his tarpaulin over the north end and the coal was wet and wet coal in a heap in a closed place is a thing that will heat itself, and I had told the night man about it on the Tuesday and he had said he would see to it and he had not seen to it."

He stopped.

"Go on."

"At the hour I went round to the north end to look at it myself, and the tarpaulin was off, and there was a smell." He looked at the pump in the yard. "You will want to know what it smelled like. Not smoke. Smoke comes after. This was a fire that had been told to wait. It was in the coal and not in the air, and if you have been on a wharf ten years you know it in the dark."

"And then?"

"And then I went and got a bucket, and while I was getting the bucket the north end went up."

"All at once?"

"Not all at once. That is what everybody says and it is not what happened. It went up at the bottom, at the floor of the heap, and the flame came out under the coal the way water comes out under a door, and then it went up the whole side of the heap in about the time it takes to say a prayer, and then it was in the roof."

"Was there a lamp?"

"There was a lamp on a hook at the north end and it was not lit, and I will say that to anybody, and I have said it to the parson and to Mr. Tarrant and to the man from the insurance office in Leeds, and nobody has ever written it down."

"Why not?"

"Because a lamp that is not lit is nobody's fault. A wet heap is somebody's, and a heap that somebody was paid to turn is a sum in a book in Leeds." He put his hands back in his lap. "The insurance paid and there was no inquest and nobody was hanged and six people are in that row at the bottom of the churchyard, and if you ask me what killed them I will tell you it was a tarpaulin and a night man who did not see to it."

I sat with that.

"Who was the night man?"

"Now that," Daniel Hogg said, "is a question I have been waiting eleven years for somebody to ask, and I will tell you the answer and then I will tell you why nobody has asked it."

"Tell me the answer first."

"The night man was not there. He was not on the wharf at all that night. He had gone to his sister's at Thorneley and he had told nobody and he came back on the Thursday and he was not the night man any more." Hogg looked at me. "So there was no night man. There was a wharf with nobody on it but me and the fire."

"And Mr. Kell?"

"Ah," he said.

The pump dripped. Somewhere behind the wall the old woman said something to somebody who was not there.

"Mr. Kell was not on the wharf," Hogg said. "Everybody says so. It is the thing that is said, and it has been said for eleven years, and I have said it myself, and I will say it now because I am an old man in a room that Mr. Tarrant pays for."

"Did you see him?"

"I saw a man at the north end door."

"Was it Mr. Kell?"

"I saw a man at the north end door in a good coat, and the light was on him for about the time it takes to say a word, and then the roof came down at that corner and I did not see anything at all for a while because I was on fire."

"You were on fire."

"My hands were on fire and I put them in the water at the lock and I have thought since that if I had put them in the water sooner I would have a right hand." He said it flatly. "The man in the good coat was not a labourer. Labourers do not wear a good coat on a wharf at eleven at night. There were three men in this town with a coat like that and one of them was Mr. Kell and one was Mr. Bedloe and one was Mr. Tarrant."

"Could it have been Mr. Bedloe?"

"It could have been the Archbishop of Canterbury," Hogg said. "I saw a coat and a shoulder and the side of a face. I am not going to swear a man into a fire on the side of a face. I am telling you what I saw and I am telling you what everybody says, and they are not the same, and I have had eleven years to think about why they are not the same."

"Why are they not the same?"

"Because the daughter was ten," he said. "Because a man came the next morning and stood in the yard with the ashes still hot and said, in front of nine of us, that Mr. Kell had been at home in his bed and had come out when the alarm was given and had gone into the warehouse to bring out the boat's man, and that is the story, and it is a good story, and it makes him a hero instead of a fool who left a tarpaulin on wet coal."

"Who said that?"

"Mr. Tarrant," Daniel Hogg said. "And he said it looking at me, and I had no hands at the time, and I said nothing, and I have said nothing for eleven years, and now you know what I am."

***

I went back up to Kell's in the afternoon and Judith had the fourteen books out on the blue cloth and a pot of ink and she was doing the Michaelmas accounts.

I sat down without being asked and put my day book on the table and did not open it.

"Tell me about the night of the fire," I said.

She did not put the pen down at once. She finished the line she was on and blotted it and set the pen in the stand, and then she folded her hands.

"I have told it eight times," she said. "Twice to the parson, once to the man from Leeds, three times to people who came to look at the house, and twice to myself in the night, which does not count."

"Tell me the ninth."

"There was a noise and I woke up and there was a red light on the ceiling of my room and I went to the window and the whole of the north end of the warehouse was alight. And I went out onto the landing and the stair was full of smoke and Mrs. Bedloe's girl, who was our girl then, came up the stair and took me down through the counting house and out into the yard, and there were men running and a man was shouting for water and another man was shouting that the boat's man was in it."
She stopped.

"Go on."

"And I stood in the yard in my nightgown with a blanket on me and I watched it, and I have been told since that I was there for an hour, and I remember it as about four minutes." Her hands were flat on the cloth. "And then they brought him out and put him in the counting house and then they took him up the stair because the counting house was filling, and I went up after and they would not let me in the room and I sat in the passage on a chair."

"For four hours."

"For four hours. And then Mr. Tarrant came out and knelt down in front of me and took my hands and told me that my father had gone to God."

I looked at her.

"And he told you where your father had been."

"He told me my father had been at home in his bed."

"Did you believe him?"

She got up and went to the window and stood with her back to me.

"The window is not shut properly," she said. "It has been not shut properly since the summer and every October I say I will have it seen to."

She pushed the sash up and pulled it down again and turned the catch and stood with her hand on it.

Then she came back and sat down.

"I believed him for about a year," she said. "And then I was eleven and I began to think about the stair, and about the fact that the stair was full of smoke at the top and clear at the bottom, which is not how a stair is, and that the counting house door was open, which is not how a door is at midnight, and that a man who comes out of his bed and into a fire does not come out of it in his second-best coat with his boots on."

"What coat?"

"His second-best." She said it quietly. "They brought him up in his coat and his boots, and the coat was the blue one, and he wore that coat to the office and to the market and he did not wear it to bed, and I have known that since I was eleven years old."

"You have known since you were eleven."

"I have known since I was eleven, Mrs. Rooke, that my father was not in his bed when that fire started," Judith said, "and I have spent nine years since then being very careful not to know it in the daytime."

She stood up and went to the window again, and this time she did not touch the catch. She stood with her hands on the sill and looked down at the wharf, and the light was going and the lanterns were coming on at the lock, and the beam house was a dark shape at the end of the basin with its padlock and its bright screws.

"He was in that warehouse," she said, with her back to me. "I have never said that out loud. He was in it and everybody in this town has been telling me for eleven years that he was not, and I have let them, because the version where he is not in it is the only version in which he is not the man who let the coal go over a short beam for nine hundred pounds."

I did not say anything.

"Say something," she said.

"Say what?"

"Say that it does not matter. Say that he was a good man anyway. Everybody says that." She turned round. "Everybody says he was a good man, and none of them know whether he was in that warehouse, and I have wanted for eleven years for one person to say the other thing."

"What other thing?"

"That it matters," Judith said. "That whether he was in that warehouse matters, and that I am not mad for wanting to know, and that a girl who asks what her father was doing at eleven at night is not a girl who did not love him."

The room was getting dark and neither of us got up to light the lamp.

"It matters," I said.

She stood there.

"And I will tell you the thing I have not said to anybody in this town," I said. "I do not want to know whether your father was a good man. A good man is a thing a town agrees on afterwards. I want to know what he did with his hands in the last four hours of his life, because a man who is dying has nothing left to protect except the thing he has been protecting, and if he said anything in that room it will be the truest thing he ever said."

Judith came back to the table and sat down.

"There were two people in that room with him," she said. "Mr. Tarrant and the surgeon. And the surgeon is dead, and Mr. Tarrant has never told me anything except that he went to God."

"Then we will ask Mr. Tarrant what a dying man said."

"He will not tell me."

"No," I said. "He will not tell you. He will tell me, because I am a woman with a book and no standing, and a man like that will say a thing to a woman with no standing that he would not say to the daughter, because he does not think it counts."

Judith looked at me across the table.

"You are going to make him tell you," she said, "and then you are going to have to decide whether to tell me."

"Yes."

"And you will decide not to."

"I will decide nothing until I have heard it," I said, "and then I will decide, and you will not be able to make me decide sooner by asking, and I am telling you that now so that when it comes you will know it was not done behind your back."

She sat with her hands round the edge of the table.

"All right," she said.

Then she got up and went to the window again and put her hand on the sash and pushed it up and pulled it down and turned the catch, and she stood there with her back to me for a while, and when she came back she had her face arranged.

"November is coming," she said. "The first sack of November."

"The first sack of November."

## Nine · The Question She Did Not Write

The first sack of November came over the wharf on the fifth.

I know the date because I had been waiting for it the way a person waits for a boat, which is to say I had been down at the crane every morning since the first and had made myself a nuisance to a man who was only doing his work.

He filled the sack and hung it and read the poise.

"A hundred and thirteen," he said.

"Yesterday it was a hundred and twelve."

"It is cold," he said. "Cold coal is heavier."

"It is not heavier," I said. "It is the same coal."

He looked at me and then he looked past me at the yard gate, the way a man looks when he has said a thing he has been told to say and knows it will not hold.

"Sevenpence," he said.

I paid him and carried it up the hill, and Mrs. Sowerby's beam said ninety-nine, and we wrote it down in the day book with the date, and then we sat at the table and looked at the two numbers.

"Ninety-eight in October and ninety-nine in November," she said.

"And a hundred and thirteen at the crane against a hundred and twelve."

"Which is one more in the sack and one more in the reading, so the difference is the same and I have learned nothing." She rubbed her eyes. "I have been at this counter since five."

"No," I said. "We have learned something. We have learned that the number at the crane moves and the number in the sack moves with it."

She looked at me.

"Say that in plain English."

"The beam is not a beam that reads heavy. It is a beam that reads a proportion. Whatever the true weight is, it comes out a seventh part more." I turned the page. "That is not a bent arm or a worn pivot. A worn pivot wears the same way on both sides. A seventh part more, on every weight, is a poise that has been filed, or a scale that has been shortened, and it was done by somebody who understood what he was doing."

Mrs. Sowerby sat back.

"Then it was made wrong on purpose."

"It was made wrong on purpose, and it was made wrong in a workshop, and somebody paid for it."

***

Judith came to the shop at eleven with her shawl over her head against the rain, and she stood in the door and did not come in, and I went out to her.

"Not in the shop," she said.

We stood under the arch of the yard with the rain coming off the gutter in a line.

"I have been thinking about what you said," she said. "About the four hours. About a dying man having nothing left to protect."

"And?"

"And I have written something." She had a paper in her hand, folded twice, and she held it against her chest. "I wrote it at four this morning and I have carried it about all day and I have not been able to give it to anybody, and if I give it to you I will have to do the thing it says."

"Then give it to me."

She held it out and I took it, and she did not let go at once, and there was a moment when we both had hold of it.

Then she let go and put her hand flat over my hand and the paper, so that the paper was under both our hands, and she stood there with her head down.

"Read it," she said.

I read it.

*Did he sign it himself?*

I read it again. The rain came off the gutter. Her hand was on mine and it was cold and it was not shaking, and that is a thing I have noticed about the hands of people who have decided something: they stop shaking.

"Miss Kell."

"Say it is a stupid question."

"It is not a stupid question. It is the best question anybody has asked me in this town." I turned the paper over. There was nothing on the back. "But it is not the question you want to ask."

She took her hand away.

"You see," she said. "I knew you would say that."

"What do you want to ask?"

"I want to ask whether he knew." Her voice had gone flat. "I want to ask whether a man who signed a paper in October knew, in December, when the coal was going over that beam, what he had done. I want to ask whether he went into that warehouse to put it out or to be in it when it burned."

The rain came down.

"Then I will tell you why you cannot ask that," I said, "and it is not because I am tender with you. It is because a thing does not hold a mind."

She looked up.

"What?"

"A thing holds what happened to it and what was done to it and what was near it. It does not hold what a man thought. A coat knows it was rained on. A coat does not know the man was sorry." I held up the paper. "That is why your question is the right one. It is a question about what a hand did, and a hand is a thing, and a thing can be read."

"How do you know what can be read and what cannot?"

"Because I have tried both," I said, "and one of them put me on my back for a day."

She stood with the rain on her face.

"Then it is the only question," she said, "and it is not the question I have been carrying for eleven years, and I would rather have the other one and be told nothing than have this one and be told the truth."

"Yes," I said. "That is what everybody would rather have."

***

She took me up to the office and gave me his pen.

I had not asked for it. She went to the desk and picked it out of the stand and held it out, and it was a good pen, a steel nib in a bone handle, worn pale where a thumb went, and the nib had been cut and mended and cut again.

"Will you do it on this first," she said, "so that I can see."

"See what?"

"See you do it." She had her chin up. "I have had eleven years of people telling me things they could not know. I am not going to take the biggest thing in my life from a woman who has not shown me her hands."

That was fair, and it was better than fair, and I have been asked for proof by better people than her and had less right to refuse.

"Sit down," I said. "And whatever I do, do not touch me and do not speak."

She sat in the chair by the window.

I took the pen and turned it over twice in my fingers, which is a habit and does nothing, and then I put it down on the desk and put two fingers on the bone handle and let it come.

Three seconds.

The bone was warm first, which is not the pen, that is my own hand, and then it went cold, which is the pen, and then:

A thumb. A thumb pressed flat along the top of the handle with the nail gone white, and the pressure was not the pressure of writing, it was the pressure of a man holding on to something. Paper under the nib, and not paper, the drag was wrong for paper, it was vellum and the nib was catching at the tooth of it. And a sound, very close, the sound of a nib going over a rough skin, which is like a comb drawn over a table edge, and under the sound a man breathing through his nose.

Then nothing for a moment, which is a pen lifted.

Then the nib again, and a different drag, slower, and under the thumb there was a second pressure on the handle, lower down, where a hand would go if it were holding the hand that held the pen, and it was there for the length of a breath and then it was not there.

Then the whole of it went, like water going out of a churn, and there was nothing but bone under my fingers.

"Was that it?" she said. "Was that the reading?"

"That was it."

"That was four seconds."

"It was three. You counted wrong because you were frightened."

She let out a breath that was almost a laugh.

"What did you get?"

I picked the pen up and put it back in the stand, and I did not answer at once, because there is a thing about telling a person what you have read, which is that you cannot give it back afterwards.

"Your father held that pen very hard," I said. "Harder than a man holds a pen to write his name. There was vellum under it, and vellum is the only thing in this house that pen has written on, because a wharfinger writes his letters on paper, and a bill of lading is on paper, and only a deed is on vellum."

She had gone quite still.

"So he signed it with that pen."

"He wrote with that pen on vellum, and he held it hard, and he stopped in the middle and started again." I looked at her. "And that is where it ended. There was a moment after the stopping and then it went, and it went because the pen has been used ten thousand times since and a thing that is used wears its own past away. I got the beginning and I did not get the end."

"You got something at the end."

"I got a pressure," I said, "and a pressure is not a fact. It could be a man's own other hand come up to steady the page. It could be a clerk's hand on his. It could be nothing at all, a fold in the vellum, a shadow." I put the pen down. "Do not make it into a thing. Not yet."

She looked at the pen in the stand.

"Then we will need the parchment," she said.

"Then we will need the parchment."

***

We went up to the house and she put the paper on the blue cloth and we both looked at it, and I did the thing I do, which is to say the plain words out loud so that they are out of the room and not in my head.

"`Did he sign it himself?` There are three answers. He signed it. He did not sign it and somebody signed for him. He signed it with help." I put my finger on the paper. "The third is the one nobody looks for and it is the commonest of the three. A man's hand is guided on a paper every day in every county in England. A man who cannot write makes his mark and two witnesses say it is his mark. A man who can write but is weak has his hand held and the paper is good and the signature is good and nobody in a court will look behind it."

"And a thing would show that?"

"A thing would show that." I looked at her. "That is what I am going to find out, and I am going to find it out in the beam house, and I am going to do it on Thursday."

"Why the beam house?"

"Because it is the one place in this town that has no carpet and no fire and no clock, and because the covenant belongs to the beam house, and because you have told me yourself that you can bear a thing in the beam house that you could not bear in that room." I folded the paper and put it in my day book. "And because there is a door on it that I can leave open."

She nodded.

"Thursday," she said.

"The eighth."

"The eighth." She stood with her hands on the table. "Mrs. Rooke, what will it be like?"

"What?"

"The reading. What will it be like to watch?"

I thought about how to say it.

"You will not see much," I said. "You will see a woman standing at a table with her hand on a parchment. That is what it looks like from outside. Three seconds, and then I will put it down, and then I will tell you what I have."

"And you?"

"I will have a headache for a day and it will feel like a hand pressing on the back of my neck, and there will be a taste in my mouth like a penny, and I will not want to be talked to."

"That is all?"

"That is all, when it goes well."

She looked at me.

"And when it does not go well?"

"Then I will be on the floor," I said, "and you will want to send for the surgeon, and you will not, because a surgeon will ask what happened and there is no answer to that question that does not end with a woman being looked at."

She stood very still.

"You have done this before."

"I have."

"And it did not go well."

"It went well four times and badly once," I said, "and the once was a thing that had been in a fire, and I was young, and I did not know that a thing that has been burned has lost the part of itself that holds what happened, and I read the fire instead, and the fire is in a hundred places at once and it is not a thing a person can hold."

Judith sat down slowly.

"The covenant," she said, "was in a box in a room above a warehouse that burned."

"The covenant was in a tin box in a drawer in a room that did not burn," I said. "I have had my hands on it. It is whole. It has been whole since 1821 and it has not been out of that box except when you took it out and put it in the light and read it four hundred times."

She let out a breath.

"Yes," she said. "Yes. All right."

***

The letter was waiting for me at the shop when I came down at four.

Mrs. Sowerby had put it on the counter with the bread and not said anything about it, and I took it up to my room and sat on the bed in my wet things and opened it, and it was four sheets, and the first three were the office and the roof and a bill for the pump at the bottom of the yard and a long passage about a man in Collow who had put a pig in the church porch and would not be reasoned with.

Then, on the fourth sheet, at the top, before anything else:

*Do not read another man's father in another man's house.*

I did not say anything to that.

Then I read the rest of the fourth sheet, which was not much.

*I have thought about the beam every night since your letter. It is not a beam. A beam that is wrong by a seventh part on every weight is not a beam that has gone wrong, it is a beam that has been made to be wrong, and a beam that has been made to be wrong was made by a man who could not have done it without a workshop and a pattern and somebody to pay for both. Find the workshop.*

*You will want to look at the poise. The poise on a beam of that size is a piece of cast iron with a hole bored in it and the hole is bored to a weight, and if the hole is too big the poise is too light and the beam reads heavy. You can tell a bored hole from a drilled one by the shoulder. If it was bored in a shop there will be a shoulder. If it was drilled in a man's shed there will not.*

*Do not do it alone. I know you will do it alone. Do not.*

Then, at the bottom, after a gap:

*I am not sleeping well. The office is the office. The house is very quiet and Mrs. Trewin has taken to putting the cloth on at four and taking it off at seven and I have not told her to stop because I do not know what I would tell her instead.*

*Write to me about the beam and not about the weather.*

*E. Rooke*

I read the whole thing twice.

He had signed it with an initial and a surname. Seven months married and he had signed a letter to his wife with an initial and a surname, and I sat on the bed in my wet things and looked at that for a long time and could not decide whether it was the most careful thing I had ever seen or the worst.

Then I got out my own paper and wrote:

*I am reading the covenant on Thursday in the beam house with the door open and a woman on the other side of the room who will not speak.*

*I am not doing it alone.*

And then I sat with the pen for a while, and then I wrote:

*I have not slept well either. There is a window at the top of the stair that does not shut and every night I hear it and every night I do not get up.*

I folded it and sealed it before I could think about it, and then I sat on the bed with the letter in my hand and the rain on the window, and I thought about a man in a house in Collow putting the cloth on the table at four o'clock for eleven months without being asked why.

Then I got up and put on my boots and went down the stair and out into the rain to the post office before it shut, which was a thing that could have waited until the morning and did not.

## Ten · Cold Iron

Judith got the key from Tarrant on the Wednesday.

She went up to the house on the market cross and asked him for it and he gave it to her without a word, and she came away with it in her pocket and her hand over her pocket, and she told me about it that evening in the shop with her back against the counter and her eyes on the door.

"He did not ask why."

"That is the worst thing you have told me this week."

"He said, 'Take the old man off the stool for the morning, he is not well in the cold.'" She took the key out and put it on the counter. It was a good key, eight inches, with a bow and a shank and a bit cut in three steps, and it was bright. "He has had it eleven years and it is bright because he takes it out and looks at it."

"Or because he uses it."

"Or because he uses it," she said.

***

We went down at seven on the Thursday with a lantern and the covenant in its tin box and the day book, and the wharf was in that grey hour before the light when the water is lighter than the sky.

The old man was not on his stool. There was a brazier by the door with a little fire in it and a stool turned over beside it and nobody.

Judith put the key in the padlock and turned it and the padlock opened and she took the chain off the hasp and coiled it and hung it on the hook, and then she stood with her hand on the door.

"I have not been in this since I was ten," she said.

"Then open it and go in first. It is yours. Do not let me be the first."

She pushed the door and it went in about a foot and stopped, and she pushed again with her shoulder and it went in, and the air that came out of that shed was the coldest air I have ever put my face into.

Not cold like a winter morning. Cold like a well. It came out at the level of my knees and went past me and was gone, and it had a smell to it that I could not place for a moment and then could, which was iron.

"Bring the light," she said.

I brought the light.

The beam house was twelve feet by twenty, stone on three sides and bricked up where the fourth side met the warehouse, with a slate roof on three beams and a floor of pounded earth with coal dust on it and a stone sill round the wall a foot high, and on the sill there were things standing in the dust: a lamp without a glass, a horn cup, a piece of chalk, a rag, and a set of iron hooks on a ring.

And in the middle of the room, under a tarpaulin, was the beam.

Judith took the tarpaulin by the corner and pulled it off and the dust went up in the lantern light in a cloud that took a long time to settle.

It was not what I had made in my head. I had made a scale like a shop scale, scaled up. It was not that. It was a frame of cast iron, two uprights and a crossbar, and from the crossbar it hung by a knife edge on a pivot that was the size of my wrist, and the arms went out either side on a fulcrum, and one arm was long and one was short, and there was a pan hung from the short arm on three chains, and on the long arm there was a poise of iron that ran on a rod.

A steelyard. The oldest kind of weighing there is. A thing that has been in use since the Romans and has not been improved since, because it does not need improving, because it is a lever and a lever is a law.

"There," Judith said. "That is it."

I walked round it.

The iron was black and had been blackened with oil and then with dust, and where a hand went it was not black but grey, and the grey was in the shape of hands. The pan was clean. The chains were clean. Somebody had kept the working parts oiled in a room that nobody had entered for eleven years, and the oil had not been applied by a ghost.

I went along the long arm with the lantern.

A steelyard is read on the long arm. There is a scale cut into it, and the scale is not numbers, it is notches, and every notch is a weight, and the notches in a good beam are cut in the casting and they are the same depth and the same distance apart from the pivot to the end.

These were not.

They were the same distance apart. I put my thumb on the first and my little finger on the fifth and moved along, and the spacing was true, and a man who had cut them had had a rule and a steady hand.

But they were not the same depth. The first five were shallow and had been filled with dust and were almost gone. The next ten were deep and bright at the lip, where the iron had been cut and not yet had time to go black.

"Judith," I said. "Come here and put your finger on this."

She came and put her finger on it.

"New," she said.

"Ten of them are new. The first five are 1711 and the next ten are not." I moved the lantern along. "A scale does not get new notches. A scale gets worn notches, and then a man sends it back to the foundry, and the foundry either makes a new arm or it does not take the job."

"Unless a man cuts them himself."

"Unless a man cuts them himself, in a shed, with a chisel and a rule and a very steady hand," I said, "and if he does that he does not cut them to make the beam truer. He cuts them to make it read what he wants."

She took her hand away.

"Then this is not the beam."

"This is a beam," I said. "Whether it is the Beam is a different question and it is not the question I came here with."

"It is a question I would like answered."

"So would I. Tomorrow." I put the lantern down on the sill. "Today I have one question and it is yours and I have to keep my head empty of every other thing in this room, and you have just put a very large thing in it."

She looked at the notches.

"I am sorry."

"Do not be sorry. Write it down." I gave her the day book and the pencil. "Write the date and write *ten notches new, five old* and put your initials under it, and then hold the book and do not speak to me again until I have done."

"The pivot is bright," I said.

"The pivot is bright."

"Who oils it?"

She stood with the lantern.

"Somebody has been in here," she said. "The padlock is on the outside and the chain is on the outside and the screws are new, and the dust on that sill is a quarter inch thick and there is no dust on the chains." Her voice had gone careful. "The door was stiff. The door has not been opened in a long time. But the chains have been handled."

"Then somebody has a second key."

"Or the same key," she said, "and Tarrant has been coming down here at night for eleven years to oil a beam he has sworn is shut."

The lantern was making our shadows go up the wall.

"Put it out of your head for an hour," I said. "A thing you cannot answer will eat the thing you can."

***

I put the tin box on the stone sill and took out the covenant and laid it on the sill with the fold open and the two signatures towards me, and then I looked at the beam and I looked at the parchment, and I did a thing I have not done before and will not do again.

I moved the parchment onto the pan of the beam.

It was a foolish thing to do and I did it for a reason I could not have said out loud. A pan is a place where a thing is weighed. I wanted the paper weighed.

"You are not going to read it there," Judith said.

"I am going to read it there."

"Why?"

"Because a room remembers what is done in it and this room has had one thing done in it for a hundred years and that is weighing, and a thing read in the place where things are weighed will be read as a weight and not as a story." I put my hand on the edge of the pan. "And because I am frightened of it and I would rather be frightened of it in a cold room with the door open."

The door was open. I had made her prop it with the chalk.

"Stand there," I said. "By the door. Do not speak. If I go down, do not catch me, because you will hurt your back and I will hurt you, and get the surgeon and tell him I fainted in a cold room, which is true."

"Mrs. Rooke."

"Stand by the door."

She went and stood by the door.

I put both hands flat on the parchment and pressed until the heel of each palm found the board underneath.

I had a question. I want to put down exactly what the question was, because the whole of what happened next turns on it and I have spent two days working out what I did wrong.

The question I meant to carry was: *Did Amos Kell put his own hand to this and did he know what he was doing?*

That is two questions. I knew it was two questions. I had said as much to Judith on the Tuesday. And I had told her, in the same breath, that a question has to be sharp.

And when I put my hands on the parchment, the thing in my head was not the two questions and it was not the sharp one either.

It was: *Was somebody holding his hand.*

I wanted that. I want to be honest about it because this is the only page in this book where I am going to be honest about it.
