I had been in Barrowfield for three weeks and I had found a beam that was wrong and a case of brass that was never opened and a man with no hands in an almshouse, and I had begun to want the third answer, the one nobody thinks of, the one that makes the daughter's father a victim and the town's villain a man in a good coat.

I wanted it. And I put my hands on the parchment wanting it.

Three seconds.

The pan went cold under my palms, and the cold came up through the parchment and into the heels of my hands, and then it opened.

I got a room.

Not this room. A room with a good ceiling and a window with a shutter half closed and a fire at the left, and the light was the light of a fire and not of a lamp, and there was a table with a green cloth and on the table there was an inkstand and a pen and a thing I could not see because it was under my hands.

I got a hand.

A man's hand, right hand, the cuff of a blue coat at the wrist, and the hand was on the parchment and the fingers were spread and the nails were clean and there was a scar across the back of the thumb, and the hand was not moving.

I got a pen, and the pen came down and wrote, and the nib made the sound a nib makes on vellum, and it wrote for the length of a word, and then it stopped.

And then the whole of it was gone, and I was standing in a cold shed with my hands on a piece of parchment on the pan of a beam, and there was a noise in my left ear like a bell rung in another room, and the shed was going away from me at the edges.

I got my hands off the parchment and I got hold of the sill.

"Mrs. Rooke."

"Stay there."

"Your nose is bleeding."

"I know." I put my wrist under my nose. "Stay where you are."

She stayed. I will say that for her. She stood by the door with the lantern and she did not move and she did not speak, and I stood holding the stone sill with my left hand and my wrist under my nose with my right, and the blood went onto the coal dust and made little dark balls of it, and the bell in my left ear rang for a long time.

When I could see properly I said, "Put the parchment back in the box."

"Is it done?"

"Put it back in the box."

She came and put it back in the box and shut the lid and turned the key, and then she stood beside me with her hands at her sides.

"Tell me."

"I got a room and a hand and a pen and one word," I said. "And then it went."

"One word?"

"Not even a word. The length of a word." I wiped my mouth with the back of my wrist. "I did not get the end. I did not get the signature. I got the beginning and then it stopped, and it did not stop the way a thing stops when it has given you all it has. It stopped the way a thing stops when it is taken away."

She was quiet.

"What does that mean?"

"It means I read it wrong," I said. "It means I brought the wrong question and the thing would not answer it, and a thing that will not answer does not say so politely. It shuts."

"What was the wrong question?"

I looked at her.

"The one I wanted," I said.

***

I could not walk up the hill without stopping, so we went along the wharf and sat on a bollard by the lock, and the light came up while we sat there and the water in the basin turned from grey to a dirty silver and the first carts came down.

Judith had her arms round her knees.

"Will you be able to do it again?"

"Not today."

"Tomorrow?"

"Not tomorrow." I touched my left ear. "This is two days. The last time it was one."

"And then?"

"And then I will do it again, and I will do it with one question, and the one question will be hers and not mine."

Judith looked at the water.

"You wanted it to be the third answer," she said. "The hand held down."

"I wanted it."

"Why?"

"Because I have been in this town for three weeks," I said, "and I have not met one person in it who is not being paid by somebody, and I have started to want the world to be arranged so that the person who is not being paid is the one who has been done to." I put my head back against the bollard. "That is not a question. That is a wish. A wish is a thing a person brings to a reading and it is the thing that breaks it."

She sat with that.

"Then when you do it again," she said, "what will you bring?"

"Your question. The words you wrote. Nothing else."

"And if it is not the answer you want?"

"Then I will tell you the answer and not the want," I said. "And you will have to do the same, which is to hear it and not hear the version you have been carrying for eleven years."

The lock gates opened and the water went down and a boat came through.

***

There was a letter on the table in my room when I got back at nine, and it had not come through the post.

It had been left. Mrs. Sowerby had found it under the shop door at seven and had put it on the table with the bread, and it had a superscription I knew, because I had seen it on a valuation book and on a folio and on the label of a tin box.

*E. Rooke, Esq.*
*At the house of Mrs. Sowerby, Barrowfield.*

I turned it over.

The seal was broken. It had been opened, and not by me, and the paper inside had been taken out and put back, because it was in the envelope crooked.

I sat down on the bed and took it out.

It was from Tarrant's clerk, in Tarrant's clerk's hand, and it was three lines.

*Sir,*

*Mr. Tarrant presents his compliments and asks whether Mrs. Rooke's business in Barrowfield is likely to be concluded before the twentieth of November, as the question of the offices will be settled in that month and it would be a convenience to know how long the house is to be troubled.*

*He is, sir, your obedient servant.*

It was addressed to my husband.

In eleven weeks the town had written to me twice and spoken to me forty times, and the one letter that mattered had gone past me to a man a hundred and eleven miles away, because in the mind of the man who wrote it I was not a person who could be asked.

I read it three times.

Then I took out my day book and wrote, at the top of a clean page, in a hand that was not steady because my ear was still ringing:

*They will not ask me. They will ask him.*

And then, underneath:

*Find out what he says.*

## Eleven · What the Cold Cost

The bell rang in my left ear from the Thursday morning until the Saturday night, and on the Friday I could not write.

I want to put down what that means, because a person who has never lost the use of a hand for a day will read it as a small thing, and it is not a small thing, and in this town it is the whole of a life.

I got up on the Friday and had my bread and went to the table with the day book and the ink and a letter that was owed to a woman in Thorneley about her brother's burial, and I wrote *Dear Madam* and the *D* came out with a shake in it like a drunk man's signature.

I looked at it.

Then I wrote it again on a fresh sheet and the *D* shook again, and this time the *e* after it came out as an *o*, and I sat with the pen in my hand and my heart going, because a letter-writer whose hand shakes is a woman with no trade.

I tried a third time. The third time the whole line ran downhill.

I put the pen down and sat with my hands in my lap and looked at the three sheets and the bell rang in my left ear, and I thought: this is what it is like. This is what it is like to be Judith Kell and have a thing that must be done by your own hand and to have your own hand not answer.

I sat there for an hour.

***

Mrs. Sowerby came up at eleven with a bowl of soup and found me still at the table with the three sheets in front of me.

She put the bowl down and looked at the sheets and then at me and then she sat down on the end of the bed.

"Show me your hands."

I showed her my hands.

She took the right one and turned it over and looked at the palm and put her thumb on the muscle at the base of the thumb and pressed, and it hurt, and I made a noise.

"There," she said. "That is not a hand that has been shaken by a fright. That is a hand that has been gripping something for two days without being told to stop."

"It is not the hand. It is the ear."

"It is the hand," she said, "and the ear is the same thing in a different place. My man had it after the *Ariel* went on the sands. He could not hold a rope for a fortnight and he could not hear out of his left side for a month, and the surgeon said it was the shock sitting in him and it would come out when it was ready." She let go of my hand. "It came out. It came out through his temper, mostly, and I was the one who had it."

I drank the soup. It was good soup and I had not eaten since the Thursday noon.

"Mrs. Sowerby."

"Out with it."

"Can you write?"

She looked at me.

"I can make my letters," she said. "I keep the shop book and I have kept it two years and nobody has ever complained of it."

"Would you write a letter for me?"

She sat very still.

"Whose letter?"

"A woman in Thorneley. About a burial. It is a thing I do for money and I have done it four hundred times and I cannot do it today." I pushed the day book across. "I will tell you the words and you will write them and I will sign it, and if my signature shakes then it shakes, and a shaking signature on a letter about a burial is not a thing anybody will question."

She did not touch the book.

"Mrs. Rooke," she said, "you have been in this town three weeks and you have carried a hundredweight up my hill and you have put a paper in my hand that says a woman bought her coal, and I would do a great deal for you. But I will not write your letters."

"Why not?"

"Because you would look at it after." She said it without unkindness. "You would look at every letter and you would see the one I made wrong, and it would be a thing in your head for a year. I know what you are. You are a woman who has one hand and a book and a name, and the hand and the book and the name are the same thing, and you will not let another woman put her letters in your book."

I sat with the soup in my hands.

"That is a hard thing to say to a person who has just asked for help."

"It is a hard thing and it is true, and you asked me because you knew I would say it." She got up. "Now. You will eat that and you will sleep this afternoon and you will not go down to that wharf until Monday at the earliest, and if I hear that you have been down to that wharf I will lock the door of this room from the outside."

***

I did not go down to the wharf.

I did something worse first, which was to find out whether I still had it.

There was a comb on the washstand, a common horn comb with three teeth gone, and it had been in that room since before I came and it belonged to nobody. I took it up and sat on the edge of the bed and put two fingers on it and let it come.

Three seconds.

Nothing.

I tried again. I have never in my life had to try twice, and I tried again, and I held it until my fingers went numb, and there was nothing at all. Not a blank. A blank is a thing you can feel, the way you feel a wall in the dark. This was not a wall. This was the way it is when you put your hand out in a dream and there is no wall and no floor and nothing to put your hand on.

I put the comb down.

Then I got up and put on my boots and went out and walked up to the market cross and stood at the top of the street and looked down at the basin.

From up there a coal town is almost beautiful. You cannot see the men's faces and you cannot see the water in the lock or the mud on the bank, and the heaps are black shapes and the roofs are grey shapes and the canal goes out flat and straight into the haze, and there is smoke coming up in three places from the chimneys behind the wharf, and the whole of it lies there in the afternoon light like a thing that has been set out on a table for sale.

I thought about the folio with fourteen leaves in it.

Thirteen of them could be sold. One of them could not, and the one that could not was the only one in the town that had ever been shut on purpose.

I stood there long enough for the light to go off the roofs.

***

There was a thing in my box that I had not looked at since I packed it, and I took it out on the Saturday night with the candle lit and the door shut.

It was his letter. The one that had come on the sixth of November with the office and the roof and the pig in the church porch, four sheets, signed with an initial and a surname.

I have read it eleven times. I know every word of it. That is not what I took it out for.

I took it out because it was a thing a living man had held in his hands for the length of time it takes to write four sheets, and a thing a living man has held is a dead thing, and a dead thing can be read, and if I could read a comb I could read that.

I sat with it in front of me on the bed and I did not touch it.

I want to say exactly what the temptation was, because it was not curiosity. I did not want to know what he thought of me. I have never once in my life wanted to know what a person thought of me; it is the one thing I have never had to ask, because people tell you with their hands.

What I wanted was to know whether he had been all right.

That is all. That is the whole of it. Four sheets about a pump and a pig, and a hand that had written them in a room in Collow with the cloth on the table at four o'clock, and I wanted to put my fingers on the paper and know whether the hand had been steady.

I sat there for a long time.

Then I put the letter back in the box and shut the lid.

I will not make a virtue of it. I did not put it away because of the rules. The rules say a woman does not read for herself, and I have broken that rule twice and I will break it again and I have made my peace with the account.

I put it away because a letter that has been read is a letter you cannot receive. If I had put my hand on that paper I would have known the thing, and then I would have had a fact about him and I would not have had a letter, and I have very few letters.

***

That same week a woman came into the shop and asked whether the letter-writer was in.

She was about fifty and she had a good shawl and a bad cough and she wanted a letter written to her son in Manchester, and she had the words in her head and a shilling in her hand and she had been three times to the parson and had not been able to make him understand.

I sat her down at the counter and put out the paper and took the pen and my hand was not steady.

"The first line," I said. "What do you want to say first?"

"Tell him his father is dead."

I wrote it. The *D* shook and the *e* came out round and I looked at it and she looked at it and neither of us said anything.

"Go on," I said.

It took an hour. It took an hour and four sheets, because a woman who has never had a letter written for her does not know how to put things in order, and she went back and forward over the same ground, and once she cried and once she laughed and once she got up and stood with her back to me and then sat down again and said, "Put that he was not a bad man."

I wrote what she said. I did not improve it. That is the whole of my trade and it is the only rule in it.

At the end of the fourth sheet I read it back to her, the whole of it, the way I do, and she listened with her hands in her lap.

Then she said, "That is him. That is my boy." And then: "Is the writing all right?"

I looked at the four sheets.

The first sheet was the writing of a woman whose hand was not her own. The second was better. The third and fourth were mine.

"It is all right," I said.

She paid me the shilling and I took it, because a letter-writer who does not take the shilling has insulted the person who brought it, and she went out and I sat at the counter with the four sheets and put my hand flat on them and did not read them, because they were her letters and not mine.

That night I could hold a pen.

Not well. But I could hold it.

***

On the Sunday I wrote to Rooke.

I had to do it in pencil first, because a pencil asks less of a hand, and then copy it in ink when the pencil was done, and it took me the whole morning and I did not once let myself think about how long it was taking.

I wrote the beam house. I wrote the notches: five that were 1711 and ten that were not, and the pivot bright and the chains handled and the door stiff. I wrote the tin box and the case of brass and the man with no hands in the almshouse.

I did not write the reading. I did not write the bell in my ear or the three sheets with the *D* shaking on all three.

Then I sat with the pen for a while and wrote:

*I am well. My hand is tired from copying. It is nothing.*

And then I stopped, because I have never in my life been able to write a lie in a letter and leave it at that, and the pen would not put down the next line, and I sat looking at the page for a long time.

Then I wrote, in the middle of the sheet, without any introduction at all:

*I have been three weeks in a town where every question is answered by a man who is paid to answer it, and I have begun to notice that I do the same thing with you. I write to you about beams. I do not write to you about the room.*

*The room is small and cold and there is a window at the top of the stair that does not shut and I hear it every night. I have got into the habit of listening for it. I do not know what I would do if it stopped.*

*Christmas is the audit and you will be in Collow for it, and I will not ask you to leave it, and I am writing that down so that neither of us has to write the other a letter about it in December.*

I read that over twice.

Then I sealed it and put it in my pocket and took it down to the post office myself, and the postmaster took it and weighed it and said, "One penny," and I paid him, and he looked at the superscription and said, "Collow," and I said, "Collow," and he said, "That is a long way," and I said, "It is a hundred and eleven miles," and he said, "It is more than that by road," and I said, "It is a hundred and eighteen by road," and he looked at me and then he put the letter in the bag.

***

That night I counted.

I counted the days from the fifteenth of October, which was the day I left, to the nineteenth of November, which was not a date I had any business counting to, and then I counted them again because the first count had come out at thirty-five and I did not believe it.

Thirty-five days. Three weeks and a half, less one day, and I had been in Barrowfield since the eighteenth of October and it was now the eleventh of November or the twelfth, and the twentieth was eight days off, and I had read the covenant once and got a room and a hand and one word and then nothing.

Eight days.

I counted them a third time and got the same number, and then I sat on the edge of the bed with the day book open on my knee and did the thing I do when a sum will not come out, which is to write it down and look at it instead of at my head.

*20 November. She is twenty-one. I have eight days.*
*After the twentieth she can sign. Tarrant loses his guardianship. The marriage must be before it, or there is no marriage, and if there is no marriage there is no settlement, and if there is no settlement there is no buyer for the thirteen.*
*Therefore the thing I am here for will be decided in eight days, and I have one reading in me and it is not a good one.*

I looked at that.

Then I wrote underneath:

*Why eight days? Nobody told me eight days. I have been counting to a date that nobody in this town has put in front of me.*

I sat with the pen over the page.

Tarrant had told me the date. Tarrant had told me the date in the first hour of the first morning, standing at his window with his hands behind him, before he had told me the fees and before he had told me the three suitors and before he had shown me the folio.

A man does not put a date in front of a stranger in the first hour unless the date is the whole of what he wants her to think about.

And if the date was the whole of what he wanted me to think about, then everything I had done since, every question I had asked and every walk down to that wharf, had been done inside a clock that he had set going.

I put the pen down.

Then I got up and put my boots on and went down the stair in the dark and stood in the shop with my hand on the counter, and Mrs. Sowerby came out of the back room with a candle and said, "What is it, what has happened," and I said:

"Whose birthday is the twentieth of November?"

She stood in the doorway with the candle.

"Miss Kell's," she said. "Everybody knows that. It is the day she comes into her own and there will be a dinner at the house and the bell will be rung."

"And before that?"

"Before that what?"

"Before Miss Kell was twenty-one," I said, "who was the last person in this town to come into their own on a twentieth of November?"

Mrs. Sowerby looked at me for a long moment.

Then she put the candle down on the counter.

"Her mother," she said. "Anne Kell was married on the twentieth of November 1808. It was her birthday and her wedding day and they had the bell rung for both."

## Twelve · The Shire Hall

His letter came on the fourteenth with a second enclosure in it that was not a letter.

It was a quarter sheet of paper with a seal at the top and five lines in a clerk's hand and a blank space at the bottom, and in the blank space he had written my name and his own and the date, and under that, in his own hand, which was small and upright and not a scholar's:

*The bearer, Mrs. Mercy Rooke, is my wife and has my authority to inspect and take copies of any return, roll, or book of record in the custody of the Clerk of the Peace for the county, touching the offices suppressed by the Order in Council of 1806 and the proceedings thereon, and I request that she be afforded the same access as myself.*

*E. Rooke, late Auditor of Returns.*

Late. He had written *late*.

I sat with that for a while. He had been the Auditor of Returns for nine years and he had written the word *late* in front of it in his own hand on a quarter sheet of paper to get his wife into a record room, and he had not written it bitterly and he had not written it small.

At the bottom of the letter he had written:

*They will not want to let you in. Do not argue with the clerk. Ask for the Deputy. The clerk is afraid of the Deputy and the Deputy is afraid of the Lord Lieutenant and the Lord Lieutenant is afraid of being written about. You are not going to write about anybody. You are going to sit down and be quiet and be a woman with a paper, and it will take an hour and you will get what you want.*

*Take the day book and copy exactly. Not the sense. The words, and where they sit on the page, and what is in the margin.*

***

The county town is fourteen miles from Barrowfield and the carrier goes on Tuesdays and Fridays and takes three hours, and I went on the Tuesday and sat in the back of the cart with a woman taking eggs to market and a man with a crate of geese, and it rained from the fourth mile.

The Shire Hall stands at the top of the town behind the castle mound, and it is a good plain building of 1762 with a stone stair and a portico and two lions that have been rained on for seventy years and have the faces of disappointed dogs.

The record room is on the first floor at the back, up a stair of its own, and it has a door with a lock and a grille and a bell, and the bell is there because they do not want you to walk in.

I rang the bell.

The clerk who came was about twenty-four and had ink on his cuff and the manner of a young man who has been given a small authority and has decided to be exact with it.

"Records are inspected by appointment," he said.

"I have an authority."

He looked at the quarter sheet and read it twice.

"This is from Mr. Rooke."

"It is."

"Mr. Rooke is not the Auditor of Returns."

"He is the late Auditor of Returns," I said, "and he has written the word *late* himself, which I would take as a courtesy from a man who might have gone on signing himself the other thing for a year and nobody would have known."

The clerk looked at the paper again.

"I will have to ask the Deputy."

"Then ask the Deputy," I said, "and I will sit on this bench and be quiet, and I have brought a book and I can be quiet for an hour."

***

The Deputy came down in twelve minutes, which told me the clerk had run.

He was about fifty with a good coat and a bad chest and he had the look of a man who has spent thirty years in a building where nothing is ever decided quickly and has come to prefer it.

"Mrs. Rooke."

"Sir."

"Your husband is known to this office."

"He was."

"He was," the Deputy agreed, and something went across his face that was not unfriendliness. "He took a copy of a return out of this room in 1830 and he had it properly and he returned it properly and he wrote a note about a bundle that had been mislaid in 1819, which nobody had asked him to write, and I have that note in my own desk." He held up the quarter sheet. "What is it you want?"

"The returns of offices suppressed under the Order in Council of 1806," I said. "The county copy. And the proceedings on the petitions that came after, if there are any."

"There are eleven boxes."

"Then I will want the eleventh."

The Deputy looked at me for a while.

"Why the eleventh?"

"Because the boxes will be in order of the hundreds," I said, "and Barrowfield is a B, and a B will be in the first or second box, and if you have eleven boxes and a question has been asked about one of them before, the box that is not in its place is the box somebody has had out."

The Deputy stood with the quarter sheet in his hand.

"Come up," he said.

***

The record room was cold and dry and smelled of leather and dust and a little of mice, and there were presses along three walls from the floor to the ceiling with the doors of them shut and numbered, and a table in the middle with two lamps and a chair, and a stove in the corner that had not been lit.

The eleven boxes were on the floor by the press.

Ten of them were in a row and the eleventh was on the table.

"The eleventh was out in September," the Deputy said. "I signed for it myself and I do not remember why and I do not like that, so I will tell you now that I do not remember why."

"Who had it?"

"Mr. Bedloe. He is an attorney in Barrowfield and he had a matter in the Chancery and he had it for a day."

I did not say anything. I put my hand on the box.

It was a deal box with a hinged lid and a paper label and the label said, in a good clerk's hand of about 1807:

*RETURNS OF OFFICES SUPPRESSED. 1806. Nos. 280—411.*

"May I have the table?"

"You have the table."

I sat down and opened the box.

***

The returns were in bundles tied with tape, and each bundle was about thirty returns, and each return was a single sheet with a printed form on it, filled in by the clerk of the parish or the clerk of the peace or, in nine cases out of ten, by a man who could not spell the name of his own office.

Each one had, at the top, a number.

I had seen those numbers before. I had seen them in a room in Collow in a book that was not supposed to exist, and I had seen them in a copy of a copy, and I had spent one evening in a room in Nether Collow going through four hundred of them with a man who is now my husband and who at the time was not.

I do not know how to describe what it is to see a thing again in another county. It is not triumph. It is the feeling a person has when they go into a strange house and find the furniture arranged the way it was arranged in the house they grew up in.

I untied the second bundle and laid the returns out in order.

At the twenty-seventh sheet I stopped.

*307. County of —. Parish of Barrowfield. Office of the Beam, being an office of the nineteenth class, in the gift of the lord of the manor, and held by Amos Kell, wharfinger, in his own person, and therefore not within the meaning of this order, the said office being of the nineteenth class and not capable of being held by deputy.*

Underneath, in the same hand, in the space for the reason:

*Returned as not suppressed. See letter of the 4th inst.*

I read it four times.

Then I read the twenty-six before it and the twenty-six after it, and they were all suppressed, every one, and each of them had, in the space for the reason, one of two things: *not held in person* or *held in trust for*.

The Beam was the only one in that bundle that was not suppressed.

"Mrs. Rooke."

"Wait."

I turned the sheet over. There was nothing on the back.

I turned it back and looked at the margin.

There was a number in the margin. It was not printed and it was not in the hand of the return. It was small and it was in pencil and it had been written at the top right, in the blank, and it was:

*204.*

I sat with my hands flat on the table.

The Deputy came and stood at my shoulder.

"That is a number," he said.

"It is."

"It is not our number. Our numbers are at the top, in the printed box."

"I know."

"Then what is it?"

"I do not know," I said, "and I am going to sit here until I have found out whether it is in any of the other boxes."

***

It took four hours.

I went through all eleven boxes, sheet by sheet, and I did not copy them, I looked at the top right of every one, and at the end of four hours I had found nine.

Nine returns with a number in the margin in pencil, small, in the same hand, and the numbers were 73, 118, 149, 204, 219, 288, 301, 307 and 366.

Nine out of four hundred and eleven.

The Deputy had gone away and come back twice, and on the second time he brought a cup of small beer and put it down without saying anything, and stood at the window with his back to me.

"Nine," he said.

"Nine."

"And they are not in any order."

"They are in the order of the numbers," I said. "Look at them. 73, 118, 149, 204, 219, 288, 301, 307, 366. That is not the order of the returns and it is not alphabetical and it is not by county. It is in order of the number in the margin, and the numbers in the margin go up."

The Deputy turned round.

"Mrs. Rooke, I have been in this office thirty-one years."

"Then tell me what the numbers are."

"I cannot."

"You have been in this office thirty-one years and you have never seen a pencil number in a margin."

"I have seen a great many," the Deputy said. "It is a thing that happens. A clerk reads a bundle and he numbers the ones he means to come back to. It is his own mark, so that when he comes back he does not have to read four hundred sheets again." He came to the table. "It is not a code. It is a man's memory, put down because he did not trust it."

"And he numbered nine out of four hundred and eleven."

"He numbered nine."

"Then those nine are the ones he meant to come back to," I said, "and it has been forty-six years, and he has not come back."

The Deputy stood looking at the sheets.

"The hand is not a clerk's hand," he said at last.

I looked up.

"How do you know?"

"Because a clerk in this office writes an upright hand with a round *e* because we are taught to, and it is a rule, and it is in the standing orders of 1791." He put his finger on the margin. "That *4* has a closed head. Nobody in this building has written a closed *4* since I was a boy."

I got out the day book and copied the nine numbers and wrote, under them, exactly what he had said.

Then I went back through the box and looked at the bottom, because a box that has been out of its place for a day has a bottom like any other box and a man in a hurry does not always look at it.

There was a paper at the bottom of the eleventh box that did not belong to it.

It was a single leaf, cut to the size of the returns, and it was new paper, not old, and it had been folded twice and had been in somebody's pocket. There was nothing on it but a copy of six lines in a hand I had seen on a card table at an assembly, and the six lines were:

*...and we are of opinion that the said order cannot stand upon the reasons assigned, the same being insufficient in law, and that the offices in question are not thereby determined...*

*...but that this judgment is not to be taken as affecting any other office not before us...*

I sat with it in my hand.

I knew that judgment. I had sat in a room in Collow in October and heard it read out, and it had taken a man four minutes to read and eleven years to get, and the whole of it turned on the difference between the reasons and the order.

Somebody in Barrowfield had copied out two lines of it, the two lines that mattered, and had carried the copy in his pocket in September, and had come into this room with it and had taken the eleventh box off the shelf and had sat where I was sitting.

"Why would he want the reasons?" the Deputy said, from the window.

"Because the reasons are the part that goes everywhere," I said. "The order in that judgment was made for one woman in one parish and it does not travel. But the reasons are the reasons. They are the same for every office in England that was suppressed on a reason that was not true."

"And if a man wanted to keep an office that was suppressed on a reason that was not true?"

"Then he would want to know exactly what the reason was, and whether it was the same reason as his." I folded the leaf and put it back where I had found it. "And then he would want to know how many other men in England were in the same position, and what they were doing about it, and whether they had a book."

The Deputy looked at me.

"Mrs. Rooke," he said, "I am going to go and stand in the passage for a while. The room will be locked from the outside and I will be in the passage, and if anybody comes up the stair I will cough."

He went out.

I sat in the cold room with eleven boxes and four hundred and eleven returns, and I copied the six lines into the day book, and then I wrote, under them:

*A man who carries a copy of a judgment in his pocket in September is a man who expects to be in a court in the spring.*

Then I turned the sheet of the Beam over again and looked at the back, and there was nothing on the back, and I was about to put it down when I saw it.

At the bottom of the margin, below the 204, in the same pencil, so faint that it had gone into the paper and could only be seen when the lamp was at the side:

*and one not entered.*

I sat very still.

"Deputy," I said. "Come and look at this, and bring the lamp, and when you have looked at it I want you to tell me whether you have ever seen it before, and I want the truth and not the comfortable thing."

He brought the lamp.

He looked at it for a long time.

"No," he said. "I have never seen that."

"Thank you."

"Mrs. Rooke." He put the lamp down. "I am going to ask you a question and I am going to ask it as a man who has kept these boxes for thirty-one years and has never once been asked for the eleventh. What is this?"

I looked at the sheet.

"This is a man in 1807," I said, "sitting in a room like this one, with four hundred and eleven returns in front of him, and he is not doing what he is told. He is doing something else. He is marking the ones that are not like the others, and he is putting them in order, and having done it he writes four words at the bottom of the ninth and then he stops, and then he shuts the box, and then he does not come back."

"And one not entered."

"And one not entered."

The Deputy stood with his hands behind his back.

"Mrs. Rooke," he said, "I am going to do a thing I have not done in thirty-one years, which is to give an opinion about a record."

"Give it."

"Somebody has been in this box since," he said. "In September, and for a day, and it was signed out to an attorney who had a matter in the Chancery. I do not say he did anything and I would not say it in front of a magistrate, because I have no evidence and I am an old man with a bad chest. But he had the box for a day, and the label of the eleventh box has been off and put back, and the paste under the label is not the paste under the other ten."

I looked at the box.

"Thank you," I said.

"For what?"

"For telling me a thing you did not have to tell me," I said, "and for not asking me what I am going to do with it."

## Thirteen · Four Hundred and Eleven

The carrier went back at four and I got in it with the geese and a wet sack over my knees and did the arithmetic all the way home, and the arithmetic would not come out, and it was the best four hours I had spent since I came into that county.

Nine numbers. 73, 118, 149, 204, 219, 288, 301, 307, 366.

Suppose they are what they look like, which is an order. Suppose a man in a room in 1807 sat down with four hundred and eleven returns and numbered the nine that interested him, and the numbers went up in the order he wrote them, which they did.

Then the numbers are not an order. They are already the numbers of something. A man numbering nine things himself numbers them one to nine.

So there was a book somewhere, and in that book there were at least three hundred and sixty-six things, and nine of the four hundred and eleven returns in that box had been matched against it and given their number in the margin.

I got out the day book and turned to a clean page and set it out.

*366 is the highest number in the margin. Therefore the book has at least 366 entries.*
*Nine of the 411 returns are marked. Therefore the book is not a book of suppressed offices. If it were, all 411 would be in it and the numbers would go past 411.*
*What do the nine have in common?*

And then, under that, I wrote what I already knew, because I had read it that morning in a hand that was not the clerk's:

*307. Office of the Beam. Office of the nineteenth class. Held in his own person. Not capable of being held by deputy. NOT SUPPRESSED.*

I sat in the cart with the geese and looked at that for a long time.

Nineteenth class. Held in person. Not capable of being held by deputy.

A man in 1806 had gone through four hundred and eleven offices that had been suppressed for being held by deputies and had found nine that could not be held by deputies at all, and had taken their numbers out of a book that had three hundred and sixty-six entries in it, and had written those numbers in the margin, and had written four words at the bottom of the last one, and had shut the box.

*And one not entered.*

One what? One office? One number? One man?

The geese made a noise. The rain came on the tilt. I sat with the pencil in my hand and could not get past it, and by the time we came into Barrowfield at seven I had written the same four lines six times and crossed out four of them.

***

I went to Tarrant's house at eight in the morning.

His clerk did not want to take my name. I stood in the passage and said, in a voice that carried into the room beyond, "Tell him it is about the return of 1806 and the letter of the fourth of December," and the door opened before the clerk had moved.

Tarrant was at his table with his breakfast and he had not shaved.

"Mrs. Rooke."

"Mr. Tarrant."

"You have been to the Shire Hall."

"I have."

He put down his cup.

"How did you get into the eleventh box?"

"With a paper from my husband and a Deputy who has a bad chest and a conscience," I said. "Sit down, Mr. Tarrant. You are not going to stand up for this."

He sat down.

I put the day book on the table and turned it round so that he could see the nine numbers.

"Those are in the margin of nine returns in that box," I said. "The hand is not a clerk's hand. 307 is Barrowfield and the Beam, and the return says the office is of the nineteenth class and held in person and not suppressed. Under it, in the corner of the same paper, somebody has written *and one not entered*."

Tarrant looked at the page for a long time.

Then he said, "My father wrote that return."

"I know."

"How do you know?"

"Because the return says *See letter of the 4th inst.*, and a parish clerk does not refer a committee to a letter unless the letter is his own, and a letter of the fourth of December 1806 from a man in Barrowfield to a committee in London is a letter a man keeps a copy of." I sat down opposite him. "Where is the copy?"

He looked at me.

"You have a great deal of assurance for a woman who has been in this town a month."

"I have a great deal of assurance for a woman who has been in a cart with geese for four hours," I said. "Where is the copy?"

Tarrant got up and went to the window and stood with his hands behind him, and I had seen him do that once before in that room and I knew what it meant by now, which is that he was deciding how much of the truth would cost him nothing.

"My father was the agent to the manor," he said. "He was the agent from 1791 to 1825 and he did the manor's business and the parish's business and half the county's business, and when the Order in Council came down in 1806 he was the man who had to make the returns for fourteen offices in this parish and he made them and he sent them and he heard nothing more for a year."

"And then he wrote a letter."

"And then in December 1807 he wrote a letter," Tarrant said, "because a man from the committee came down and asked him why Barrowfield had returned thirteen offices suppressed and one not suppressed when the parish had been told to return the lot, and my father wrote and said that the fourteenth was of the nineteenth class and could not be held by deputy, and that the order did not touch it, and that he would be obliged if the committee would say so in writing."

"And did they?"

"They never answered him," Tarrant said. "Not a line. He wrote three times over two years and he had no answer, and in the end he stopped writing and the office stayed open and the beam stayed in use, and that was the end of it until 1821."

I sat with that.

"A committee that does not answer," I said, "is a committee that has decided not to put the thing in writing."

"That is what my father said."

"Your father was a careful man."

"My father was a frightened man by the end," Tarrant said, and turned round. "He was frightened of the London committee and he was frightened of the parish and he was frightened of the nineteen and he died in 1825 in his bed at seventy-one with nothing whatever to be frightened of, and he had spent the last four years of his life going over that box of papers every winter."

"Where is the copy of the letter?"

Tarrant looked at me.

"With Mr. Bedloe," he said. "My father's papers went to Bedloe's office in 1825, because Bedloe was his attorney and Bedloe was my attorney and Bedloe drew everything this family has signed since 1798, and I have never asked for them back because I have never wanted them."

"Why not?"

"Because I do not know what is in them," Tarrant said, "and a man who does not know what is in a box cannot be asked about it."

***

I went down to Bedloe's office that afternoon.

It was in the row behind the market cross, over a saddler's, up a stair with a rope on it, and the door had a brass plate that said *BEDLOE & SON* and under it, in smaller letters, *Attornies at Law*.

There was no son. There had been a son in 1820 and the son had died at nineteen of a thing in the chest, and the plate had never been changed, and I have thought since that a man who leaves a dead boy's name on a door for twelve years is either a man who cannot bear to take it down or a man who finds it useful, and with Bedloe I have never been able to decide which, and I have decided in the end that it is both.

The clerk showed me in.

Bedloe was at his desk with three piles of paper and a good chair and a fire that was too warm for the room.

"Mrs. Rooke. Sit down." He put his pen in the stand. "You have come about the eleventh box."

"I have."

"The Deputy wrote to me on Tuesday evening," he said. "He is a good man and he has a bad chest and he does not like a thing being out of its place. He told me that you had found the pencil marks."

"And?"

"And I have been expecting you since Tuesday. I am glad you came in the daytime. At night I would have to wonder what you wanted, and wondering is bad for my chest."

"I have something."

"Then let us have it." He folded his hands. "And I will tell you what I know, and I will tell you all of it, and the reason I will tell you all of it is that I have spent thirty-one years watching people find out half of a thing and ruin themselves with it, and I have got tired of watching it."

I took out the day book and opened it at the nine numbers and turned it round.

"Nine returns in that box have a number in the margin in pencil," I said. "73, 118, 149, 204, 219, 288, 301, 307, 366. They are all offices of the nineteenth class. They are all held in person. None of them was suppressed. And the last one has four words under it: *and one not entered*."

Bedloe looked at the page.

"Yes," he said.

"Whose hand?"

"Mr. Farrow's."

"Who was Mr. Farrow?"

"Mr. Farrow was my master," Bedloe said. "He was the clerk of the peace's clerk in that building from 1804 to 1809 and he came to Barrowfield in 1809 and he took me as an articled clerk in 1811 and he died in 1818 and I bought his practice from his widow for two hundred and sixty pounds, and the plate on that door says Bedloe and Son because his name was Farrow and mine is Bedloe and there is no son, and neither of those things has anything to do with the number in the margin." He put his finger on the page. "That is his hand and I would know it in a dark room. He wrote a closed *4*."

"The Deputy said the same thing."

"The Deputy was a boy in that office when Farrow was there." Bedloe sat back. "Now. You have asked me whose hand. Ask me the next thing."

"What is the book?"

"I do not know."

"You do not know."

"I do not know," Bedloe said, "and I am not going to pretend to you that I do. I have been thirty-one years in this trade and I have watched men swear to a thing they had only guessed at, and then have to live in the guess. I am too old to begin." He turned the day book round and pushed it back. "What I know is this. Farrow kept a book of his own, a quarto, and he called it his register, and when he died it came to me with the practice, and it is in that cupboard behind you, and I have opened it maybe twenty times in fourteen years and I have never made head nor tail of it."

"Why not?"

"Because it is not a register of anything in this county," Bedloe said. "It has got three hundred and sixty-six entries in it when he died and the entries are offices, and they are all over England, and there is not one of them anywhere near here except ours."

"Three hundred and sixty-six."

"Three hundred and sixty-six when he died in 1818," Bedloe said. "There are more now. He added to it for nine years after he left that building and I have added to it twice, and I have added to it twice for reasons I would rather not go into in front of a woman I am trying to be honest with."

I sat with my hands in my lap and looked at him.

"Mr. Bedloe," I said, "why have you added to it?"

He got up and went to the cupboard.

"Because a paper outlives the man who wrote it," he said, "and I have been in this trade thirty-one years, and I have never once seen a paper that was written down and did not in the end come out."

He opened the cupboard and took out a quarto book bound in reversed calf, and put it on the desk, and did not open it.

"There," he said. "There is the book. You may look at it and I will sit here, and I will answer what I can answer, and when you ask me a thing I cannot answer I will say so, and you will not press me, because if you press me I will show you the door and I will be sorry about it afterwards."

I put my hand on the book.

Three seconds would have told me the whole of it. Three seconds, and the room and the hands and every man who had written in it since 1804, and I would have had the thing in my head and no need of him at all.

I did not do it. I want that written down, because there are four places in my life where I could have taken a thing with my hand and did not, and this is the first of them, and I did not do it for a reason that I did not understand at the time and understand now.

I did not do it because he had told me the truth when he did not have to, and a truth that has been given to you is a thing you do not take out of the giver's hand.

I opened the book at the front and read it with my eyes.

The first leaf had a title on it in a good round hand, and the title was not *Register* and it was not *Offices*. It was:

*Offices of the Nineteenth Class in England and Wales, held in the person, not capable of deputation, with the date of the return and the name of the returning officer.*

Under it, in the same hand, smaller:

*Begun at the Shire Hall, December 1806. Continued at Barrowfield, 1809.*

The leaves were ruled in columns and the columns were headed for the number, the county, the parish or place, the office, the holder, the return, and the note.

There were four entries to a page and the pages were numbered and there were ninety-two of them, and the entries went from one to three hundred and sixty-six, and the handwriting changed twice, once in 1809 when the hand got smaller and better, and once in 1820 when it got larger and worse, which is what happens to a hand at about fifty.

I turned to 204.

*204. County of —. Barrowfield. The Beam. Amos Kell, wharfinger. Returned 4 Dec 1806, not suppressed. Note: the nine for the committee, 1807.*

I read that three times.

Then I turned to 366, at the end.

*366. County of —. Parish of —. The Common Balance. Thos. Hedley, grocer. Returned 2 Jan 1815, not suppressed. Note: —*

And under it, in the same hand, in the space that had been left:

*and one not entered.*

I sat looking at the page.

"Mr. Bedloe."

"Proceed."

"This book has three hundred and sixty-six entries and they are numbered one to three hundred and sixty-six."

"They are."

"Then find me number one hundred and eleven," I said, "and number one hundred and twelve, and tell me what is written against them."

He did not move.

"Why those?"

"Because a book with three hundred and sixty-six entries in it and no gaps is a book that was written from the beginning," I said, "and a book with gaps in it is a book that had a beginning somewhere else."

Bedloe came round the desk and stood at my shoulder and turned the leaves back, and it took him a while, and when he found the place he put his finger on it and did not say anything.

Number 111 was an office in a parish in the West Riding.
Number 112 was blank.

Not ruled and empty. Blank. The paper was there and the rule was there and there was nothing written on it, and the entry above it and the entry below it were both in Farrow's hand and both dated 1807.

"One hundred and eleven comes before one hundred and thirteen," I said.

"Yes."

"And one hundred and twelve is not there."

"One hundred and twelve is not there." Bedloe took his finger away. "There are seven of those. I have counted them. Seven blanks in three hundred and sixty-six."

"Where are the other six?"

"I do not know. They are not in any order that I have been able to find." He went back round the desk and sat down. "Mrs. Rooke, I have had that book fourteen years and I have looked at those seven blanks a hundred times and I have never once been able to make a thing of them, and I am going to tell you the only thing I have ever thought about it, and then I am going to ask you to put the book down and go home."

"Say it."

"A blank in a register is not a mistake," Bedloe said. "A clerk does not leave a number blank. He writes the number and strikes it out, or he writes it at the bottom and puts *vacat* against it. A number left blank in a ruled book is a number that was taken out afterwards, and there is no erasure, and paper does not give up ink without a trace."

I looked at the page.

"Somebody took seven things out of this book," I said, "and did it with a knife."

"Somebody took seven things out of this book," Bedloe said, "and if you ask me who, I will tell you that I do not know, and if you ask me when, I will tell you that it was before 1818, because there are seven blanks in the part of the book that was written before 1818 and there are none in the part that was written after."

"Then it was Farrow."

"Then it was Farrow," Bedloe said, "or it was somebody who had the book before it came to me, and I have told you before that I bought it from his widow for two hundred and sixty pounds with the practice, and I did not count the pages." He shut the book. "Now. I have given you the book and I have given you the seven blanks and I have given you Farrow's hand, and I am going to give you one thing more and then I am going to ask you for something."

"Give it."

"The letter of the fourth of December 1806 is in that cupboard," he said. "I have read it. It is four pages and it is the cleverest thing I have ever read by a man who was frightened, and it does not say one word about the book, and it does not say one word about the numbers, and it makes the case for Barrowfield so well that the committee never answered it, because to answer it they would have had to admit that a parish clerk in the north had found the hole in their order."

"And what do you want from me?"

"I want you to tell me when you have finished," Bedloe said, "and I want you to tell me first, before you tell the girl, before you tell Tarrant, before you write a single line of it to that husband of yours in Collow."

I stood up.

"Why?"

"Because I have been the man who draws the papers in this town for thirty-one years," Bedloe said, "and I have watched two women in this county go up against a thing that was written down, and I know how it ends, and it does not end with the paper."

## Fourteen · The Book of Marriages

I went back the next morning and he had the other book out on the desk before I sat down.

"You said one thing more," he said. "This is the one thing more, and I have had it out since six, and I have read it through for the first time in nine years, and I am not in a good temper about it."

It was a quarto, the same size as the register and bound the same way, and the label on the front said *MARRIAGES* and under that, in the same hand, *B. & F. 1804—*

"There is no end date on the label," I said.

"There is no end date because a man does not put an end date on a thing he means to go on doing until he dies," Bedloe said. "Farrow began it and I have gone on with it, and it is the only book in this office that has never been out of this room."

"What is it?"

"It is what it says. A book of marriages. Not the ones I have celebrated; I have never celebrated one and I never will, I am not a clergyman. The ones I have drawn." He turned it round. "In this county, a marriage that carries property is drawn before it is made. There is a settlement. There are trustees. There are powers. And because a woman who marries ceases to be a person in law, the whole of her property goes into the hands of trustees and her husband, and the paper that does it has to be right, because it cannot be undone."

"I know what a settlement is."

"Then you know more than nine women in ten," Bedloe said. "Now look at how it is kept."

He opened it at a page at random.

Each page was ruled in columns, and the columns were headed for the date, the parties, the property, the consideration, the trustees, and the note.

And in the property column there was not a list of things. There was a list of offices.

*1809. Mar. 14. Ellwood — Batty. Property: the market, the crane, the shambles, the coal back, the measure. Consideration: £3,100. Trustees: T. Ellwood, J. Batty. Note: measure withheld, see below.*

"Withheld," I said.

"Withheld." Bedloe put his finger on it. "That is the whole of my trade in one word, Mrs. Rooke. A man marries a woman with five offices and he gets four of them and the fifth is withheld, and the fifth is the one that cannot be held by a deputy, and it sits in the marriage like a stone in a boot, and in nine cases out of ten the woman holds it and cannot use it and the husband cannot touch it and everybody pretends it is not there."

I turned the leaves.

*1811. Jun. 2. Rennard — Thwaite. Property: the ferry, the lamps. Consideration: £640.*
*1812. Oct. 30. Hedley — Ellerbeck. Property: the bell, the almshouse. Consideration: £210. Note: almshouse returned to the parish, 1814.*
*1814. Feb. 8. Thwaite — Kell. Property: the toll at the turn. Consideration: £900. Note: —*

I stopped.

"Kell," I said.

"Amos Kell's sister married a Thwaite in 1814 and the toll at the turn went with her," Bedloe said. "It came back into the Kell line in 1820 when the Thwaite died without issue, and that is why the fourteen are fourteen and not thirteen, and it is also why the toll is the best of them, because it has been in and out of three families and every time it has come out it has come out with a price on it."

I turned on.

*1817. Sep. 9. Rennard — Hollis. Property: the pump, the scavenger.*
*1820. May 1. Kell — Fenwick. Property: — Consideration: — Note: no property. Marriage in the ordinary way.*

"That is your mother," I said.

"That is my mother," Bedloe said. "She married a man with nothing and she brought nothing and it is the only entry on that page with a dash in the property column, and I have looked at it more often than I have looked at any other line in this book."

"Why?"

"Because I drew it," Bedloe said, "and it was the first one I drew on my own, and I made a mistake in it, and the mistake was that I wrote *marriage in the ordinary way* in the note, which is not a thing a lawyer writes, and I have never been able to strike it out."

He turned the leaves back two pages and stopped.

"Look at that one," he said. "1819. Read it."

*1819. Apr. 6. Rennard — Batty. Property: the market, the crane, the shambles, the coal back, the measure, the ferry. Consideration: £4,400. Trustees: J. Batty, T. Ellwood. Note: measure withheld.*

"Six offices," I said.

"Six offices and four thousand four hundred pounds and a woman of twenty-three," Bedloe said. "I drew it. I have the draft in the press behind you and I could show you the draft and you would find nothing in it that a court would look at twice. The trustees were her uncle and her husband's partner. The jointure was three hundred a year charged on the toll at the turn, and it was never paid, and I will tell you why it was never paid, and then I will stop talking about it."

"Tell me why."

"Because the toll at the turn is not a sum of money. It is a right to take money at a gate. And a right to take money at a gate is collected by the man who stands at the gate, and the man who stands at the gate in this town has been the same man since 1822, and he does not know that a jointure has been charged on him, and if you told him he would say that nobody has ever shown him a paper." Bedloe shut the book for a moment with his hand flat on it. "She went into the almshouse in 1827. She is the woman in the first room. You have met her."

I sat with that.

"The woman who does not know what year it is."

"The woman who does not know what year it is." He opened the book again. "And her husband is a respectable man in this town who has six offices and sits on the vestry, and he has never done one unlawful thing in his life, and every step of what happened to her was drawn by me and stamped and witnessed and filed."

I turned the leaves to K.

There were four pages of K. Kell took a page and a half by itself.

*1821. Dec 23. Kell, J. (infant, by her guardian). Property: the fourteen offices. Consideration: — Note: not yet asked.*

The date was the twenty-third of December 1821. Four days after the fire. Two days before Christmas. Two days before her father was in the ground.

I sat with my hand flat on the page.

"He came here on the twenty-third of December," I said, "four days after the fire, and had her entered in this book."

"He came here on the twenty-third of December with a mortgage, a fire, six bodies and a girl," Bedloe said, "and he asked me what was to be done, and I told him, and I opened this book and I entered her, because a girl with fourteen offices is a girl who will be married, and a girl who will be married is a girl whose marriage will have to be drawn, and I enter a thing when I first hear of it and not when it is brought to me, because the first hearing is the thing that tells you what a man is thinking about."

"And *not yet asked*?"

"That is what he said." Bedloe sat back. "I asked him whether anybody had spoken for her and he said, not yet. And I wrote it down because it was the truth and because it was the first thing in eleven years that anybody had said to me about that girl that was not a lie."

I sat with my hand on the page and something went over in me the way a key goes over in a lock.

"Mr. Bedloe. When you enter a thing, do you enter it once?"

"Twice," he said. "Always twice. Once here, and once on a slip for the family, because a family that holds a paper does not come back in six months and tell me I have written what I did not write. I have done it for thirty-one years and there is a Kell bundle in that press eleven inches thick."

"And the slip is in your hand."

"The slip is in my hand and the book is in my hand, which is why I am the only man in this county who can tell you what he did on any Tuesday since 1804."

"Then I know what the line is," I said.

He put his pen down.

"Proceed."

"A girl of ten goes into her father's bureau for something else and finds a paper, and there are three words at the end of it that she can read, and the hand is not her father's and it is not her mother's and it is not the parson's. It has been in the house all her life and nobody will tell her what it is. So she keeps it. She keeps it eleven years, and then she hears there is a woman at Collow who reads paper, and she writes to that woman and does not sign her name, and the one thing she asks about is not the fire and not the offices and not the money. It is the hand."

Bedloe sat quite still.

"Not yet asked," he said.

"Not yet asked."

He looked at the book and then at the window and then at me.

"That is my handwriting," he said. "She has been looking at my handwriting since she was ten years old and she has never seen my face."

I did not say the next thing, which was that she had seen it that afternoon in the assembly room and had not known it. There are things a person should be allowed to find out in their own order.

I turned the page.

There were seven more entries under Kell, J. over eleven years, and every one of them was in Bedloe's hand, and every one of them was a note and not a settlement.

*1824. Mar. Ellerbeck, sen., enquired. Note: withdrawn, the mill.*
*1826. Aug. Whitworth and Co., enquired by letter. Note: no offer.*
*1828. Jan. Hollis, enquired. Note: the man is fifty-nine.*
*1829. Nov. Ellerbeck, jun., enquired. Note: the father came, not the son.*
*1831. Apr. Tarrant, N., enquired. Note: for his nephew. No figure named.*
*1832. Jun. Whitworth, T., enquired. Note: for the toll only.*
*1832. Sep. 14. Kell, J. — valued. £6,240. For Whitworth. See letter of 12th.*

I sat looking at the last one for a long time.

"September the fourteenth," I said.

"September the fourteenth."

"She wrote to me in May."

"She wrote to you in May," Bedloe said, "and she wrote to you before there was a figure in this book, and I have thought about that a great deal this week, and I have come to the conclusion that the girl is cleverer than either of us."

I turned the page.

The next entry was not Kell.

*1832. Oct. 2. Bedloe — . Property: — Note: enquiry from Collow. Rooke, M. Answered 4th.*

I read it twice.

"October the second," I said. "Enquiry from Collow. Rooke, M. Answered the fourth."

"Yes."

"Somebody made an enquiry about me."

"Somebody made an enquiry about you on the second of October and I answered it on the fourth," Bedloe said, "and I answered it because I answer everything that comes into this office in writing, and I answered it in four lines, and the four lines were that you were married to a man of no property, that you had no offices, that you had a reputation in one county and no money in any, and that if the enquiry was about a settlement there was nothing to settle."

"Who enquired?"

"I do not know. It came by a clerk with no name on the letter and the letter was burned, as they are."

"You burned it."

"I burn everything that is not a deed," Bedloe said. "It is not a concealment, it is a habit of thirty-one years, and if you want to know what was in it you will have to take my word, and my word is that it asked four questions about you and I answered them and I have wondered ever since why a man in Collow wanted to know whether the wife of the late Auditor of Returns had any property of her own."

I sat very still.

"Was it Tarrant?"

"Tarrant does not write by a clerk with no name on the letter. Tarrant writes on his own paper and signs it and puts a stamp on it and thinks he has been subtle."

"Then who?"

"I do not know," Bedloe said, "and I have told you that I do not know twice now and I am going to ask you to stop asking, because a man who is asked a thing he does not know three times begins to invent an answer to be rid of the question, and I have seen it happen in this office and it is how half the false evidence in England gets made."

I closed the book.

"Mr. Bedloe."

"Mrs. Rooke."

"The fourteenth of September," I said. "Who brought it? The valuation?"

"Tarrant brought it. He came in with a sheet in his own hand and he asked me to put a figure in the book, and I asked him what figure, and he said six thousand two hundred and forty, and I asked him where he had it, and he said he had had it in his head since 1826."

"And you wrote it down."

"I wrote it down," Bedloe said, "and I will tell you why I wrote it down, and it is not a thing I am proud of. I wrote it down because a figure in a book is a fact and a fact is a thing that can be produced, and I have spent thirty-one years putting facts into books so that when the day comes there is something to produce." He looked at me. "The day has come. That is what I am telling you. In September a man put a price on that girl in my book and in October a woman came from Collow to value the offices, and if you cannot see the shape of what is being done in this town then you are not the woman I took you for."

I stood up.

"I see the shape," I said. "I have seen it since the first morning. What I do not see is why you have given me the book."

Bedloe looked at the fire for a while.

"Because I am fifty-one," he said, "and I have drawn four hundred settlements and I have never once drawn one that I would put my own name to in a court of conscience, and I have got to an age where a man begins to think about what he has signed and not about what he has been paid."

"That is not an answer."

"No," he said. "It is not. The answer is that my son would have been thirty-one this year, and he died at nineteen, and since then I have not been able to look at a girl of that age without doing arithmetic. Now get out of my office, Mrs. Rooke, because I have four deeds to draw before Friday and I have already given you an hour I cannot charge for."

***

I went out into the row behind the market cross and stood in the street with my hand on the saddler's rail.

It was the eighteenth of November. There were two days to the twentieth and I had been in Barrowfield a month and I had in my day book a beam that read a seventh part heavy, a case of brass that had not been opened since 1821, a man with no hands in an almshouse, a woman in the first room who had been twenty-three in 1819, nine pencil numbers in a Shire Hall four hundred and eleven strong, seven blanks in a book three hundred and sixty-six long, and a line in a lawyer's hand that said:

*Kell, J. — valued. £6,240. For Whitworth. See letter of 12th.*

And under all of it, the thing I could not put on any page: that on the twenty-third of December 1821, four days after the fire, two days before Christmas, with her father not yet in the ground, a man had walked into that office and opened that book and entered a child of ten as property, and had done it because it was the decent thing and somebody had to.

I had come a hundred and eleven miles to answer a question about a dead man's hand.

The question was getting smaller. The book was getting bigger.

I looked down the street at the corn merchant's and the assembly rooms above it, and at the two chained posts where the carriers came in, and at the pump, and at a boy running, and it was all of it ordinary and none of it was.

Then I went to find Judith, because I had had the book for an hour and a half and there was a thing in it she had a right to know before I had thought of a way to say it, and I have never been able to keep a thing from a person who is paying for it with their own life.

She was in the room with the blue cloth and the table was covered with paper.

Not accounts. Cards. Cards and a list and a pen and a pot of ink, and the cards were written out and stacked and there were twenty-two of them.

"What is this?"

"The twentieth," she said. "I am twenty-one on Tuesday and there is a dinner and I have to ask people." She looked up. "Mrs. Sowerby says there must be a dinner. She says if I do not have a dinner the town will say I am afraid of the day, and she is right."

"Who is asked?"

"Everybody who has been kind to me and everybody who has not." She put her pen down. "Mr. Tarrant and his nephew. Mr. Bedloe. The parson. The vestry. Mr. Ellerbeck. Mrs. Ellerbeck that was, who is his sister and keeps his house. Mr. Whitworth's agent, because Whitworth cannot come himself and it would be an insult not to ask the agent." She looked at the cards. "Twenty-two people. And I have written them all myself and my hand is tired and I am not going to stop."

"Judith."

She looked up at my voice, which is a thing that happens to people, and she put her hands flat on the table.

"Tell me."

"On the fourteenth of September, Tarrant went to Bedloe's office with a figure in his own hand. The figure was six thousand two hundred and forty. Bedloe wrote it in the book of marriages, against your name, with the words *for Whitworth* after it."

She did not move.

"September," she said.

"September."

"I wrote to you in May."

"You wrote to me in May," I said, "and in September he put a price on you in a lawyer's book, and in October I came."

She stood there with her hands flat on twenty-two cards.

"Then he did not send for you because of my letter," she said. "He sent for you because he needed a figure, and my letter was the convenient thing."

"No," I said. "That is the one thing I am sure of. He wanted the offices valued and he would have had a lawyer do it and paid him. He wrote to Collow in May because you had written first, and I have seen his face when he says your name and it is not the face of a man who has forgotten what he did in 1821." I sat down. "That is what makes him dangerous. He is not a liar. He is a man who has done a wrong thing every year for eleven years, in order, and can explain every one of them."

She picked up one of the cards and looked at it.

"Then we will have the dinner," she said.

"Judith."

"We will have the dinner, and he will come, and he will sit at that table, and at the end of it I will be twenty-one and he will not be my guardian any more, and I am going to say so out loud in front of twenty-two people." She put the card down. "I have been a girl in a book for eleven years, Mrs. Rooke. On Thursday I am going to be a woman at a table, and whatever he has written against my name, I am going to make him say it to my face."

"I was a girl in a book too," I said.

She looked at me. "You? You are the one who reads them."

"I was copied into one first. In my father's hand, in a parish office, in a hand I have read a great many times and never once for myself." I leaned on the table among the twenty-two cards. "A woman at a table is a good thing, Judith. But the table will ask you what you are for, the way a book does, and the answer is not written on any of these."

She turned a card over in her fingers. "And you have found an answer?"

"I have a question I have not asked yet," I said. "That is further than I had got at twenty-one."

She put the card down and looked at me then not as a girl looks at a woman who has come to help her, but as one woman looks at another who has admitted to a want. "Then we are both of us going to be somebody new on Thursday. You will come back and tell me what you asked."

"Here, or in Barrowfield," I said, "if I am ever this way again."

"That is a promise, Mrs. Rooke, and I am holding you to it."

## Fifteen · Short Weight

The dinner was at four because in October it is dark by six and a woman who is twenty-one wants her guests to see the table.

Twenty-two covers in the long room at Kell's, with the shutters open and the two best candles lit and the good cloth out, and the servant with the pinned sleeves had been got into a cap that had been her mother's and was not the right shape, and there was a piece of beef and a dish of fowl and a pudding, and the whole of it cost, I found out afterwards, four pounds six.

I was put at the bottom of the table on the left, which is where a woman with no property is put, and I did not mind it, because from the bottom of a table you can see every face and nobody can see yours.

Tarrant sat at Judith's right. Bedloe sat at her left. The nephew sat opposite me at the bottom and I had him in view for two hours.

His name was Nicholas Tarrant too, and he was twenty-four, and he was better looking than his uncle had been at that age and worse looking than he thought, and he had a habit of putting his hand flat on the cloth when he made a point, which is a habit I have seen in men who are used to being listened to in offices and are not sure of it at tables.

The parson said grace. The beef went round.

For an hour it was a dinner.

I want to put that down, because a person reading this might think the whole of that evening was a scene, and it was not. For an hour it was twenty-two people eating beef and talking about the price of coal, and Mrs. Ellerbeck said a thing about the wet summer, and the parson told a story about a bishop, and the man from the bank said the tolls would be up in the spring, and two of the women talked across the table about a christening, and the nephew asked me twice whether I found the town agreeable.

At a quarter to six Judith put her spoon down.

"Mr. Tarrant," she said.

The table did not go quiet at once. That is a thing that only happens in books. It went quiet in a wave, from the bottom up, as people stopped and looked and then stopped other people.

"Miss Kell."

"I am twenty-one today."

"You are twenty-one today," he said, "and I have brought you a book, and it is a very dull book, and it is the history of the drainage of this valley, and I have written in the front of it."

"Thank you. I will read it." She had her hands in her lap. "I want to say a thing at my own table, and I want to say it in front of everybody who has been kind to me, because there are twenty-two people here and I do not want to say it twenty-two times in the street."

"I am listening, Miss Kell," Tarrant said.

"From this morning I am of age. The fourteen offices are mine to hold and to order, and my guardian's time is over, and I am sensible of what he has done for me and I would like the table to drink his health."

They drank his health.

Tarrant sat with his glass in his hand and looked at her over it, and I watched his face and it was not angry and it was not pleased; it was the face of a man who has just been told the weight of a thing he had been carrying and had got used to.

"And there is a second thing," Judith said. "On Monday morning I am going to send for all fourteen of the deputations and read them, every one, with my own eyes, and take a copy of each, and I am going to begin with the beam."

The parson put his glass down.

"Miss Kell," he said, "the beam is not a matter for a dinner table."

"No," Judith said, "it is a matter for a shed with a lock on it and a man on a stool in front of it, and it has been a matter for that for eleven years, and I have decided to bring it in where it is warm."

There was a noise from the bottom of the table that was the nephew laughing, one short breath, and then not laughing.

Tarrant put his glass down on the cloth very carefully.

"Miss Kell," he said, "you are of age and you may send for what you like. I will have the fourteen deputations in your hands on Monday by ten. But I am going to say one thing in front of this company, and I am going to say it once." He put his hand flat on the cloth, which was his nephew's habit and not his, and I saw where the nephew had got it. "The beam is not shut by my order. It is shut by a covenant under your father's hand, and the covenant has a term, and the term is ninety-nine years, and it is a covenant that runs with the office, which means it is not a thing that ended when you came of age. It is a thing that is in the office you have just taken."

"I know what the covenant is."

"Then you know that the office you are so anxious to use is an office that will not pay a fee and cannot be let and is worth nine pounds twelve a year and has a paper against it that would cost more to fight than the office is worth." He said it without heat, and that was the worst of it. "I am not your enemy, Miss Kell. I have been your guardian for eleven years and I have kept the accounts in my own hand and I have taken nothing out of this estate but my expenses. If you send for the deputations, send for them. But do not send for them at a dinner, and do not do it in front of the parson, because the parson will write to the bishop and the bishop will write to somebody, and once it is in a letter in a bishop's desk you cannot put it back."

Judith sat with her hands in her lap.

"Mr. Tarrant," she said, "I have not said one word against you at this table."

"You have not."

"And I have not said that the beam is shut by your order."

"You have not."

"Then let us have the pudding," Judith said, "and on Monday I will send for the deputations, and you will send them, and we will both of us be in the right and neither of us will have said a thing at a dinner that cannot be said at a dinner, and the parson may write to the bishop whatever the parson likes, because I have been written about all my life and it has never once changed what I had for breakfast."

The table laughed. It was a real laugh, the kind that comes when twenty-two people have been holding their breath and one of them decides to let it go, and the parson laughed loudest of all, and the pudding came in, and the nephew leaned across to me and said:

"Your husband is an auditor, I understand."

"He was."

"And what does an auditor do, exactly, Mrs. Rooke?"

"He asks for the book," I said, "and then he asks who wrote in it."

He smiled at that, and it was a good smile and he knew it was, and he said, "Then I hope he has a good winter of it," and turned to the woman on his right, and I sat with my pudding and understood that I had just been told a thing by a young man who did not know he had told me anything.

***

I went out at half past eight with Bedloe.

We stood in the street with our coats on and the door shut behind us and the noise of the table going on inside.

"Well," he said.

"Well."

"She did it better than I would have done it," Bedloe said. "I would have sent for the deputations and said nothing and let it be a surprise on Monday. She told the whole table and made her guardian drink his own health first." He put his hat on. "That is a wharfinger's trick. You give a man a thing he wants and then you take a thing he wants, and he cannot complain of the second without seeming to quarrel with the first."

"Mr. Bedloe."

"Mrs. Rooke."
