"The covenant has a term of ninety-nine years. Is that in the deed?"

"It is in the deed."

"And it runs with the office?"

"It runs with the office."

"Then how is it that a covenant with ninety-nine years to run on it was signed by a man who was fifty-one years old and had no son?"

Bedloe stood quite still in the street.

"Say that again."

"A man of fifty-one signs a covenant for ninety-nine years," I said. "It cannot be performed in his lifetime. It can only be performed by whoever holds the office after him, and he had a daughter of ten and no son, and under the law of England his daughter inherits the office and her husband holds it, and a man who signs a ninety-nine year covenant in 1821 knows that he is not binding himself. He is binding a woman not yet born."

The candle in the window of the long room moved, and somebody inside laughed.

"Mrs. Rooke," Bedloe said, "you have said a thing that I have thought for eleven years and have never said out loud, and I am going to tell you why I have not said it."

"Because it would mean he knew."

"Because it would mean he knew," Bedloe said, "and a man who knows he is binding a woman not yet born is not a man who has been cheated into a signature. He is a man making a bargain. And if he was making a bargain, then the answer to your question is that he signed it himself, in his own hand, with his eyes open, and that the nine hundred pounds was not a rescue, it was a price."

***

I did not sleep that night.

At six I got up and went down to the shop and lit the stove and got out the day book and the case of brass that Judith had lent me, and I did the sum I had been putting off for three weeks.

I did it with the brass because a sum done with a real weight is a sum a person cannot argue with.

Fifty-six pounds in the pan and coal in the other until the beam came up. Then the same coal weighed out in fourteens against the seven and the four and the two and the one. It took an hour and a half and I did it four times and every time I got the same answer.

A hundredweight bought at the crane is ninety-eight pounds of coal. That is the whole of it. The beam reads a hundred and twelve and the coal is ninety-eight, and the fourteen pounds is not coal, it is the tax.

I sat down and did the town.

*Barrowfield goes over the wharf 3,800 tons in a year. Mr. Sowerby's book has it: 3,812 tons in 1831, 3,764 in 1830, 3,795 in 1829.*
*3,800 tons is 8,512,000 pounds.*
*At a hundred and twelve pounds to the hundredweight that is 76,000 hundredweights.*
*Fourteen pounds on every hundredweight is 1,064,000 pounds in a year.*
*Which is 475 tons.*
*Eleven years is 5,225 tons.*

I sat looking at that.

Five thousand two hundred and twenty-five tons of coal that went over that beam and was paid for and did not exist.

At sevenpence the hundredweight, which is what the poor pay, nine thousand five hundred hundredweights is near enough three hundred pounds a year.

I did it again with the pencil, the long way, because a number that size is a number a person wants to be wrong.

*9,500 hundredweights at sevenpence is 66,500 pence.*
*66,500 pence is £277 1s 8d.*

I put the pencil down.

Two hundred and seventy-seven pounds a year, and eleven years of it is three thousand and forty-seven pounds.

Not four hundred and eighty. Three thousand.

And every penny of it out of the pockets of people who buy coal by the hundredweight in a town where a collier's wage is nine shillings a week.

I went back over it three times.

It came out the same.

The difference is this. The fees of the thirteen offices come to three hundred and twelve pounds a year, and fourteen parts in the hundred of three hundred and twelve is forty-three pounds fourteen, and eleven years of that is four hundred and eighty, and four hundred and eighty is the sum a court would give you, because four hundred and eighty is what the office-holder took.

But the office-holder is not the man who set the beam.

The man who set the beam took the other three thousand three hundred, and he took it in coal, a hundredweight at a time, off every woman in that street, and there is no book of it and no receipt and no plaintiff.

I sat in the back room of a shop at eight in the morning with the stove going and the brass out on the table and the whole of it in front of me.

Then Mrs. Sowerby came in with the bread and looked at the table and looked at my face and said, "What have you done?"

"I have done the sum."

"Read it to me."

I read it to her.

She stood there with the bread in her arms and did not say anything for a long while.

"Three thousand eight hundred," she said at last.

"Three thousand eight hundred and twenty-three."

"And how much of that will anybody get back?"

"Four hundred and eighty," I said. "If there is a court and if it finds for us, and if the man who set the beam can be shown to have taken a fee, which he cannot, because he did not take a fee. He took coal."

Mrs. Sowerby put the bread down on the counter.

"Then the law is a fool," she said.

"The law is not a fool. The law is exact." I closed the day book. "The law will give you back exactly what was taken from you in a form it can count. The coal was not taken in a form it can count. It was taken fourteen pounds at a time off a hundredweight, from people who did not weigh it, and there is no register of it anywhere, and a court cannot give a woman back a thing that was never written down."

She stood with her hands on the counter.

"Then what is the use of it?"

"The use of it is that it can be written down now," I said. "Not the eleven years. The years are gone. But the next hundredweight and the next after that, and the first of January and the first of February, and a book with the dates in it and the weights in it and two women's names under every line."

***

I went round to the almshouse at noon and sat on the bench with the woman from the first room.

She was sitting in the sun with a shawl round her and her hands in her lap, and she was about forty, and she had been twenty-three in 1819, and she did not know what year it was.

"There is a sum," I said. "I have done it and it comes to three thousand pounds, and it is the money that was taken out of the pockets of this street in fourteen pounds at a time, and nobody will ever get it back."

She looked at me.

"There was a bell," she said.

"What?"

"There was a bell on the crane and it rang when the load was over, and my husband could hear it from the market cross and he would look at his watch." She turned her hands over. "He is a very respectable man."

"He is."

"He has six offices."

"He has."

She nodded, and looked at the pump, and said nothing else, and after a while I understood that she had told me the whole of it and that I had been the one who had not understood.

***

When I came back at two Mrs. Sowerby was still at the counter and the brass was still on the table.

"You will want the old beam," she said.

"What old beam?"

"The one in the loft over the beam house," she said. "The one that was there before. My man's father was a keelman and he told me there were two beams in that shed and the old one went up into the loft in 1809 when the new one came, and the new one was a present from somebody, and nobody has been up there since."

I sat very still.

"How do you know there is a beam in that loft?"

"Because a thing that goes up a ladder does not come down a ladder unless somebody carries it," she said, "and nobody in this town has carried anything out of that shed since 1821 except a man with a key."

## Sixteen · The Wharf House

Judith gave me the key on the Friday and said, "Do not go at night," and I went at night.

Not out of wilfulness. There is a thing about a shed with a lock on it that a woman who has been in the town a month has learned, which is that in the daytime there are seven men on that wharf and every one of them will look at a woman going into the beam house and every one of them will have a version of it by dinner.

At night there is nobody but the lock and the water.

It rained. It had rained since four and the gutters were going and the basin was black, and the lantern made a yellow hole in it about six feet across and the rest of the world was water falling.

I opened the padlock and put the chain on the hook and went in and shut the door behind me, and the noise of the rain on the slate roof was so loud in that stone room that I stood still for a moment to be sure I had not been followed.

Then I lit the second candle and put it on the sill.

The beam was there where I had left it, with the tarpaulin off, and the pan hanging, and I did not look at it. I looked up.

The roof had three beams and between the second and the third there was a hatch, and there was a ladder hooked to the wall under it, and the ladder was iron and it was bolted and the bolts were old and the rungs were worn in the middle in the way that iron wears, which is to say shallow and smooth.

I unhooked the ladder and set it under the hatch and went up with the candle in one hand.

The hatch cover was a board and it moved when I pushed it, and there was a smell came down that was dust and old rope and something sweeter that I could not place, and I put the board aside and went up into the loft.

***

The loft was the whole of the shed and higher than I had thought, high enough to stand in at the middle, and the roof came down on both sides, and there was a floor of boards with a foot of dust on it and a window at the gable end with four panes and no glass in two of them.

And there were three things in it.

There was a coil of rope, tarred, on a peg.
There was a chest with a padlock and the padlock had rusted shut.
And there was the beam.

It was lying on two trestles, and it had been taken apart and laid out the way a man lays out a thing he means to put together again, and it had been there long enough for the dust to settle on it and be disturbed and settle again.

It was smaller than the one below. That was the first thing. The one below had a frame of cast iron and a pivot the size of my wrist and arms that a man could not span. This one was iron too but it had been made by a smith and not a founder, and you could see it in the shoulders, and the arms were shaped by hand and the taper was not even.

And on the long arm there was a plate of brass screwed on, and the plate had a scale cut into it, and the scale was in notches and the notches were all the same depth and every one of them had been cut by the same hand at the same time.

I put the candle down on the chest and went along the arm with my fingers.

Fifteen notches. The first at four inches from the pivot and the last at the end, and they were true, and there was no new iron anywhere on that arm.

And on the short arm, near the pivot, cast into the iron where a man would see it every time he hung the pan, there was a line of letters.

I held the candle close.

*J. HALLAM. ROTHERHAM. 1711.*

And under it, in the same casting, in smaller letters, one line:

*WEIGHT IS A PROMISE.*

I sat down on the chest.

I want to put down what I did in that loft, because it was not a reading and it was not a search and it took a long time and I have never told anybody the whole of it.

I sat on a chest in a loft over a locked shed in the rain and I put my hand flat on the iron arm and I did not read it.

I did not read it because it did not need reading. It was made in 1711 by a man in Rotherham and it had a scale cut in it and the scale was true, and it had been in use for ninety-eight years and then it had been taken apart and laid on trestles and left, and the man who left it there had done the most honest thing anybody in that town had done in eleven years, which was to put the true beam up out of the way rather than break it.

Somebody had known. That is what the loft was. Somebody in 1809 had known that the new beam was wrong and had not been able to say so and had taken the old one apart and carried it up a ladder and left it whole.

I sat there for a long time with my hand on the iron.

Then I looked at the chest.

It was a deal chest with two bands of iron and a padlock that had rusted into a lump, and the hasp had been put on with four screws and one of the screws was missing, and a man with a candle and a cold chisel could have had it open in a minute.

I did not have a chisel. I had a penknife and a hairpin and the hinge of the lid was the old kind, a strap hinge, and the screws in the hinge were brass and brass does not rust.

I got the lid up in about a quarter of an hour and it made a noise like a tooth coming out.

There were four books in the chest and a bundle of papers tied with tape and a bundle of quittances on a spike, and the books were account books, and they were the books of the wharf from 1809 to 1821, and I knew that before I opened one because the spine of every one of them was labelled in a hand I had seen on a burial entry and on a covenant and on a paper of dates that a girl had written out for me at my asking.

I opened the last one.

It was a wharfinger's book and it was kept the way a good wharfinger keeps a book, which is to say badly and honestly: the dealings, the boat, the ton, the man, the price, week by week, in a hand that got smaller as the year went on.

Then, at the back, there were four pages that were not dealings.

The first page had a heading: *Beam.*

Under the heading, in the same hand, in columns:

*Oct 1809. The new beam hung. Difference at 100 lb: 13 lb. Told Mr. T. He says it is the plate.*
*Nov 1809. Difference 14. Told Mr. T. He says it will be put right.*
*Jan 1810. Difference 14. Wrote to Mr. T. No answer.*
*Apr 1810. Difference 14. The old beam in the loft. If it were hung they would know.*
*Sep 1812. Difference 14. I have taken the difference out of my own fee this quarter. It is £11 4s. It does not reach the shop.*
*Jun 1815. Difference 14. Six years.*
*Mar 1819. Anne is dead. I have not the heart for the beam and I have not the heart for anything and the difference goes on.*
*Sep 1821. Mr. Bedloe came about the mortgage. There is no money. There is nothing but the offices and the offices are the town's bread and I do not know what I am to do.*

I turned the page.

The next entry was not in the columns. It was written across the whole page, in a hand that had gone bigger and worse, and it was dated the eighteenth of December 1821, which was the day before the fire, and it was four words:

*The north end is wet.*

Under it:

*I will go down tonight and take the tarpaulin off myself.*

I sat in that loft for a long time with the rain on the roof.

He had known. He had known for twelve years and he had taken the difference out of his own fee and it had not been enough and he had written it down in a book that nobody was ever meant to read, and on the eighteenth of December he had gone down to the north end at eleven at night to take a tarpaulin off a heap of wet coal because a night man had not.

He had gone down there to stop a fire.

I turned the page over. There was nothing on the other side.

I put the book back in the chest and put the lid down and stood in the middle of that loft with the candle and the rain, and I thought about a girl of ten in a passage with her hands in her lap, and about a man in a good blue coat with his boots on, and about the difference between a man who signs a thing and a man who lets a thing be signed.

Then I went down the ladder and put the hatch back and locked the shed and walked home in the rain with the candle out and my hand on the wall of the warehouse most of the way.

***

I wrote to him that night.

I want to be exact about this because it is the only place in this book where I am going to be exact about it.

I had four sheets on the table and the candle and a glass of water and the rain on the window of the room over the shop, and I wrote the beam first, the way I always did, and I told him about the loft and the trestles and the brass plate and the notches that were all the same depth.

Then I turned the sheet and wrote:

*I am going to write down a thing now and then I am going to put this page in the fire, and you will never read it, and I am writing it because if I do not write it somewhere I will say it out loud to the wrong person.*

*I have thought about the inn at Thorneley every night for five weeks. Not the way a person thinks about a thing that happened. The way a person thinks about a thing they want to happen again.*

*I remember your hand on the back of my neck. I remember it because you did it before I had said anything, and I have spent eight months being a woman who does not have to say anything, and I did not know until that night how much of it I had been doing as a kind of hiding.*

*I remember the noise you made when I put my mouth on your shoulder. I have thought about that noise more than I have thought about the beam, and the beam is the reason I am in this town, and I am not sorry.*

*What I want, and I mean to write it plainly and then burn it, is to be in a room with you with nothing to do. Not a room with a table and a paper on it. Not a room where either of us is being careful. A room with a bed in it and rain on the window and no clock, and I want you to put your hand flat on my back the way you did, and I want to be the one who says the first word.*

I stopped there.

I read it over.

Then I took the sheet by the corner and held it to the candle.

It caught, and I put it in the grate, and it burned, and I watched it burn, and when it was ash I sat looking at the ash for a while, and then I wrote the letter I was going to send, which was three sheets and had the loft in it and the notches and the word *promise* cast into the iron, and at the end of it:

*I am well. The rain is very bad here and the window over the stair still does not shut and I have stopped minding it.*

I sealed it and put it by and went to bed.

***

In the morning the ash in the grate had been swept.

I did not think about it. Mrs. Sowerby swept the grate every morning at five and she had been doing it for two years and she did not read the ash and there is nothing to read in ash.

But the paper had not burned the whole way.

I know that because when I came down at seven there was a corner of it on the hearth, charred black on three sides and clean on the fourth, and on the clean part there were four words in my own hand, and the four words were:

*put your hand flat*

I picked it up.

I stood in the back room with a corner of my own letter in my hand and the fire out and the shop not open, and I thought: she swept it at five and she would have swept the whole of it into the pan and it would have gone out with the ash.

Unless she had found a corner that had not burned and had picked it out and put it back on the hearth.

I put it in the stove and watched it until there was nothing.

Then I opened the shop and sold four pounds of flour and a quarter of tea, and at nine Mrs. Sowerby came down and said, "You are up early," and I said, "I did not sleep," and she said, "It is the rain," and I said, "It is the rain," and neither of us said anything else about it.

***

Judith came at eleven with her shawl over her head.

"The loft," she said.

"The loft."

"Is it there?"

"It is there and it is whole and it is true and it was made in Rotherham in 1711 and there is a line cast into the arm that says *weight is a promise*." I gave her the day book with the drawing in it, the arm and the notches and the plate. "Somebody took it apart in 1809 and carried it up a ladder and put it on trestles, and that person did not break it and did not sell it and did not tell you."

She sat down on the bench by the window.

"Nineteen years," she said.

"Nineteen years."

"Somebody in my father's employment took the true beam apart and hid it in the loft of his own shed and went on working for him for twelve years and never said a word." She had her hands in her lap. "Why would a man do that?"

"Because he could not do anything else," I said. "Because a man who is told to hang a new beam and take the old one away has three choices. He can hang it and say nothing, or he can refuse and lose his place, or he can put the old one up a ladder and hope that one day somebody comes with a reason to bring it down."

"And nobody came."

"Nobody came," I said, "until a girl of ten grew up and wrote a letter to a woman in Collow who is a fool for a dead man's hand."

She looked at the drawing.

"Can it be hung?"

"It can be hung. It wants a new pin at the pivot and the plate wants cleaning and the pan chains want oiling and it wants a man who knows which end is which." I closed the book. "It cannot be hung in a shed that is locked, and it cannot be hung by a woman who is not the holder of the office, and it cannot be hung on a day when the town has not been told."

"Then when?"

I sat down opposite her.

"Your guardian's time is over," I said. "The deputations come to you on Monday. The covenant runs to 1920 and it is in the office and it will not go away because you are twenty-one. The marriage is being drawn. There is a man in Hull who has paid a lawyer to put a figure against your name, and there is a beam in a loft, and there is a book in the Shire Hall with nine numbers in it and seven blanks."

She waited.

"On the fourteenth of January," I said, "the town settles its accounts. That is what the fourteenth is. Every office in this parish is accounted for on that day and every fee is paid and every book is balanced in the market hall in front of whoever wants to stand there."

"How do you know that?"

"Because it is in the folio," I said. "The fourteenth of January, *the audit of the town's accounts*, and it has been in that folio since 1809 and nobody has ever thought to tell me about it because it is a thing everybody knows."

Judith sat quite still and did not look at me.

"On the fourteenth of January," she said, "in the market hall, in front of the whole town, I am going to hang that beam."

"You are going to ask for it to be hung," I said. "There is a difference and you will want it on the day. A holder does not hang a beam. A holder orders the beam hung and stands by it while it is done, and the man who hangs it is the man whose hands are on the iron, and when it comes out wrong it is his hands the town will look at."

She looked at me.

"You have thought about this a great deal."

"I have thought about it every night for three weeks," I said, "and I have not thought of a way to do it that does not end with you standing in front of every man in this town with a true weight in your hand and a false one in your past."

## Seventeen · Mr. Tarrant Junior

He came into the shop on the third of December for tobacco and stayed an hour and a quarter.

I know the time because I had the day book open and I wrote the hour down when he came in, which is a habit I have, and I wrote it down again when he went out, and I have looked at that entry more than once since and thought about what an hour and a quarter costs.

He was in a good grey coat and he had come on foot and he had not brought a servant, which is a thing a young man does when he wants to be thought a plain man, and he stood at the counter and said, "Two ounces of the dark, Mrs. Sowerby," and then he said, "and I will take a pipe of it here if I may, because my uncle's house is full of women this week and they have all decided he is dying."

"Is he?"

"No," he said. "He has a cold in the chest and he is sixty-two and he has decided to be interesting about it."

Mrs. Sowerby cut the tobacco and put it in a paper and he filled his pipe at the counter and lit it with a spill from the stove, and then he sat down on the bench by the window without being asked, and I understood that we were going to have a conversation.

"Mrs. Rooke."

"Mr. Tarrant."

"You will have heard that I am a candidate."

"I have heard it from your uncle."

"My uncle is not a man who leaves a thing to be said by the person it concerns." He drew on the pipe. "What did he tell you?"

"That you want the wharf."

"He would say that." He turned the pipe in his fingers. "It is true and it is not the whole of it and I am going to tell you the rest of it, and I would be obliged if you would not decide what I am until I have finished, because I have been decided about by this town since I was seventeen and it has saved everybody a great deal of trouble and cost me a great deal of money."

"Go on."

"There is a pump in the yard behind the shambles," he said. "It is the town pump and it is one of the fourteen, the office of the pump, and it is fourteen pounds a year and the office is held by a man named Hollis who is seventy-one and has not been able to work it for four years. In August the pump broke. Not the handle. The barrel. The wood had gone and the iron band had come off and the water was coming up through the ground round the base and running into the shambles, and by the middle of September there was a hole in that yard you could put a dog in."

"And?"

"And nothing," he said. "Nobody did anything. Hollis is seventy-one and the office is his and he cannot mend a pump and he has not the money to pay a man, and the town will not mend it because the pump is not the town's, it is an office, and an office is a private thing with a public duty and when the man who holds it cannot do the duty there is no machinery in England for making him, except a court, and a court costs more than a pump."

"So you mended it."

"I went to the vestry first," he said. "In September. I stood in front of eleven men and I said there was a hole in that yard and I would pay for the pump out of my own pocket and they might put it in the book as a gift to the parish. The vicar said that would be very handsome. Mr. Bedloe said that a gift to a parish from a candidate is not a gift, it is a declaration, and that the book would remember it at the election and so would every man in the room. He was right. I went out and did it without them and told nobody, and that is the only way a thing gets done in this town by anybody who means to go on living in it."

"It cost me eleven pounds four," he said, "and I did not send the bill to anybody and I have not told a soul until now." He looked at me. "There. That is the thing my uncle told you to find out. He said, did he not, that you were not to decide what I am until you had heard me talk about the pump."

I sat down at the counter.

"He did."

"Then he is cleverer than I have been giving him credit for these ten years," the young man said, "because if I had told you that in the first five minutes you would have thought I was buying you, and now you have had to sit through a quarter of an hour of tobacco first and it will stay with you."

"Why did you mend it?"

He looked at the window.

"Because I walk past it every day," he said. "That is the whole of the reason and it is not a noble one. I walk past it and it was in my road and it was a hole in my road, and I have mended it, and if I had been a better man I would have mended it in August and not in October."

"And now you want something."

"Now I want something," he agreed. "I want you to tell Miss Kell that I mended the pump and that I did not send the bill, and I want you to tell her that I am the only man in this town who has done one thing in eleven years that cost him money and got him nothing."

"You could tell her yourself."

"I have tried. She will not be in a room with me for more than four minutes." He knocked the pipe out into the stove. "She will be in a room with you for an hour. That is not flattery. It is the reason I am sitting on this bench."

***

I got up and shut the shop door and turned the card round, and came back and sat down.

"Mr. Tarrant," I said. "You have an hour and a quarter and you have used a quarter of it. Say the rest."

He put the pipe down.

"I will be plain with you. You have been in this town seven weeks and you have found more in it than I have found in seven years, and I have decided not to mind that." He put his hands on his knees. "There are three ways this ends. Whitworth buys the toll and marries her and never comes here and the wharf is run by an agent in Hull and in ten years there is no wharf. Ellerbeck marries her and he is fifty-six and he will be dead in fifteen years and there will be a widow with fourteen offices and four stepchildren and a lawsuit. Or I marry her."

"And if you marry her?"

"Then the offices stay in this town and the man who holds them lives in this town and has to look at the pump every day." He said it without any warmth and it was the most convincing thing he had said. "I am not going to tell you I am in love with her. I have spoken to her nine times. She is clever and she is frightened and she has a way of looking at a man as if she were weighing him and had found him short, and I do not much like it and I do not much dislike it."

"Then why?"

"Because I have been in my uncle's office since I was seventeen and I have done the accounts of fourteen offices for seven years and I know what they are, and the man who marries her will hold thirteen of them, and the thirteen are three hundred and twelve pounds a year, and three hundred and twelve pounds a year for thirty years is nine thousand three hundred and sixty pounds, and I am not going to pretend to a woman with a day book that I have not done that sum."

I looked at him.

"Thank you," I said.

"For what?"

"For not saying the other thing."

"The other thing is that she is pretty and I have thought about her," he said. "I have thought about her a great deal. I am twenty-four and I am not a monk and I am not going to stand in a shop and lie about it. But a man who marries for that in this county is a man who has not done the sum, and I have done the sum."

"Nine times," I said. "You have spoken to her nine times. What have you asked her?"

He thought about it, which I liked him for.

"Whether she was warm," he said. "The first time. It was the day of the frost and she had come down to the wharf without a shawl and I asked her whether she was warm. And I have asked her whether the dog was hers, and whether she had been in Hull, and whether she liked the church."

"Nine times."

"Nine times is not nine conversations." He was counting on his fingers now. "There was the assizes, and the christening at the chapel, and the day the crane chain broke."

"And in all of that, did you ask her one thing she could have said no to?"

He looked at me.

"That is a strange question."

"It is the only question," I said. "You asked her whether she was warm. A woman cannot say no to that. You asked her whether the dog was hers. A woman cannot say no to that either; she can only be wrong. You have never asked her a thing where no was a thing she was allowed to say, and until you have, you have not spoken to her at all. You have been pleasant at her nine times."

He sat back.

"That is hard," he said.

"It is arithmetic," I said. "It is the same as the pump. The pump was in your road and you mended it and you told nobody, and that is a good thing, and it cost you eleven pounds four, and it did not require her to be anything at all. You have been doing the same with her for nine times. You have been a good thing in her road that did not require her to answer."

He was quiet for a while then.

"Then what do I ask her?"

"I am not going to tell you."

"Why not?"

"Because if I tell you, you will ask her the thing I have given you, and she will answer it, and she will have answered me and not you." I put my hand flat on the counter. "You want me to tell her about the pump and you want me to tell you what to say to her, and if I do both of those things you will be married to a woman who has had one conversation with you and it will have been mine."

He looked at the window.

"Then I will ask her something and get it wrong," he said.

"Yes."

"You are not going to help me."

"No."

He turned the pipe over in his fingers.

"I have never in my life asked a woman a question I did not know the answer to," he said. "That is a thing I have just found out about myself in a tobacconist's shop at the age of twenty-four, and I would rather you did not tell anybody."

"I write everything down," I said. "You may rely on it."

***

He was quiet for a while.

Then he said, "I want to say a thing and I cannot find the way to say it."

"Say it badly."

"My uncle is not well."

"You said he had a cold."

"My uncle has a cold and he is sixty-two and last Tuesday he could not remember the name of the man who has held the ferry for nine years." He looked at his hands. "I have worked with him every day for seven years. He is the cleverest man I have ever met and he has been the cleverest man in this county since 1799 and on Tuesday he called Hollis *Hobson* and then he called him *Hollis* and then he sat down and put his hand over his eyes."

I did not say anything.

"He has been carrying that wharf since 1821," the young man said. "Not the wharf. The whole of it. The estate and the parish and the manor and her. Nine hundred pounds of his own money and eleven years of being the man who took advantage, and he has never once asked anybody for anything, and I am not going to be the man who tells him he is finished."

"Then what are you going to do?"

"I am going to marry her before he cannot sign the settlement," he said, "and I am going to do it in a way that lets him think it was his idea, and I am going to be at his table on Sunday for the rest of his life, and he will die in his own bed with everything in order." He looked up. "That is not a noble reason either. But it is the reason."

***

There is nothing to be said to that, and he knew it, so we sat in the shop with the stove ticking and let it stand.

"Mr. Tarrant," I said, "there is a thing in this that you have not said and I am going to say it for you, and then I am going to ask you one question."

"I am listening."

"Thirteen of the fourteen offices pass to the husband. The fourteenth does not, because it cannot be held by a deputy and cannot be devised and cannot be let, and it is the beam." I put my hand flat on the counter. "So the man who marries her gets three hundred and twelve pounds a year and the town's coal, and she keeps a shed with a lock on it and a paper that says she must not open it, and the one thing in the fourteen that matters is the one thing her marriage takes away from her without even taking it."

He was quiet.

"Yes," he said.

"And if she asked you to hang the old beam and open that shed?"

"I would say no," he said. "I would say no in the kindest way and I would say it in private and I would give her a reason that sounded like a reason, and the reason would be that the covenant would cost more to fight than the office is worth."

That was honest and it was worse than a lie.

"And if she asked you in public?"

"She will not ask me in public," he said. "She is not a fool and she knows what a public question costs a man who has just married her."

I looked at him for a long moment.

"Then she will not marry you," I said, "and it will not be because you mended a pump or did not mend it. It will be because you are right."

He stood up and put his coat straight.

"I know," he said.

He got as far as the door.

"Mrs. Rooke."

"Mr. Tarrant."

"I would let her keep the beam," he said. "I want that written down somewhere, even if it is only in your book. If she wants to stand in that shed and weigh coal with her own hands I would let her do it, and I would stand at the back and say nothing, and if Tarrant the elder did not like it he could have it out with me."

I looked at him.

"Would you?"

"Yes."

"Could you?"

He stood with his hand on the door.

"In law," he said, "the office is hers and the marriage does not put it in my hands, and if she chooses to exercise it I cannot stop her, and no court in England would make her stop." He said it slowly and he was working it out as he said it, and I watched him find the thing that his own trade had put there and had never had to look at. "Unless a court found that the covenant bound her, and a court would not, because the covenant is a covenant and a covenant is not the office."

"Then you could let her."

"I could let her," he said.

And then he stopped, and stood in the doorway with his hand on the door, and the light from the street came in, and I watched a young man hear what he had just said.

"Within the law," he said.

He said it quietly, to himself, and not to me, and he heard it as he said it, and his ears went red.

Then he said, "Good afternoon, Mrs. Rooke," and went out, and the bell over the door went on ringing for a while after.

## Eighteen · A Hand Over a Hand

The sixth of December was a Thursday and it was fine and cold, and I had spent six weeks finding out what a question costs, and I had one question left and I had taken every wish out of it.

Judith opened the shed at eight.

She had the key by then; Tarrant had sent the fourteen deputations a fortnight before by his clerk with a note that said, *These are yours. I have kept copies. N.T.*, and he had sent the key of the beam house with them in a paper of its own, and the paper said, *The key of the shed. I have no further use for it.*

"Where do you want to be?" I said.

"Here." She put her hand on the sill. "Not by the door. Here."

"Then here, and you do not speak, and if I go down you do not catch me."

"I know."

"And Judith." I had the tin box under my arm. "The question is yours. I am going to say it out loud before I put my hand on the paper and I am going to say nothing else. If you have anything in your head that you want answered, say it now and I will tell you to put it away."

She stood with her hand on the stone.

"I want to know who held his hand," she said.

"That is not the question."

"It is not the question and it is what I want."

"Then put it away," I said. "Put it away now, out loud, and hear yourself do it."

She looked at the beam.

"I put it away," she said.

"Say it again and mean it."

"I put it away." Her voice was steady. "I want to know who held his hand and I am not going to know it today, and I am going to stand here and hold this sill and not say one word."

***

I put the tin box on the pan of the beam and opened it and took out the covenant and laid it flat.

The shed was cold. There was frost in the corners of the floor and the light came in through the one window and lay on the stone in a white bar, and the iron of the beam had gone dull with the cold and there was a smell of iron and coal dust and nothing else.

I put both hands flat on the parchment.

I said, out loud, once:

"Did Amos Kell sign this with his own hand?"

And I let it come.

***

Three seconds.

The cold came up first and it was not the cold of the shed. It was the cold of a room with a good fire in it, which is a different cold, the cold that sits at the back of a room away from the fire, and I knew it was a good fire because I could smell it, coal and a little green wood, and I could hear it settle.

Then the room.

Not this shed. A room with a ceiling and a cornice and a window at the left with the shutter half closed so that the light came in a stripe. A table with a green cloth. On the table an inkstand and a pen and a paper and a hand.

The hand was a right hand and it was on the paper and the cuff was blue and there was a scar across the back of the thumb, a small one, old, the kind a man gets from a knife when he is young.

The hand was writing.

I could not read the words. That is not how it works. A thing does not give you letters, it gives you what it was like to be there, and what it was like was this: the nib going over vellum, which is a rough sound, a comb on a table edge; the man's breath going in through his nose and out through his mouth; a chair moving somewhere behind him; and under the hand, through the paper, the edge of the table.

The hand wrote a word and stopped.

Then the other hand came.

It came from the right and from above, and it was bigger, and it came down flat on the back of the writing hand and closed over it, and it did not grip and it did not hold him still. It lay on him. A thumb on the wrist, four fingers across the knuckles, and the pressure was even, the way a man puts his hand on a horse's neck, and there was a ring on the fourth finger, a seal ring, gold, with the flat of the seal turned inward so that it pressed into the skin.

The writing hand did not pull away.

I want to be exact about that, because I have thought about it every night since. The writing hand did not pull away. It stopped, and it stayed stopped for the length of two breaths, and then it went on.

And it wrote the name. I could not see the letters but I could feel the shape of the writing change: it got smaller and it got slower and the last stroke went down hard enough to score the vellum.

Then the pen came off the paper and both hands stayed where they were.

Both of them. The writing hand and the hand over it, for the length of a breath, neither of them moving, the way two people stand in a room after a thing has been said and neither of them can leave.

And there was somebody else in the room.

Not a hand. Not a cuff. A weight and a breath. Somebody standing back from the table on the left, near the fire, and I knew he was there the way a person knows there is somebody behind them in a street: the air is different and the small sounds are different and once he moved his feet on the boards.

He was breathing slowly. That is the thing I have thought about most. A man who is watching a hard thing being done breathes fast or he holds his breath. This one breathed slow and even, the way a man breathes when he is waiting for the end of something he has arranged.

Then the hand came off, and the cuff went out of the frame, and the ring was gone.

And then the whole of it went, and I was standing in a shed with my hands on a piece of parchment and my nose bleeding into the coal dust.

***

I got my hands off the paper.

"Mrs. Rooke."

"Do not." I had my wrist under my nose. "Do not come near me for a minute."

She did not come near me. She stood with her hand on the stone sill and her knuckles white and she did not say anything, and I stood in the middle of that shed with my blood going onto the floor and waited for the room to come back.

It took longer than the first time. There was no bell in my ear this time, which I have never been able to explain, but there was a weight on my chest, low down, like a hand pressing, and it stayed for six days, and I have never had that before or since.

When I could stand up straight I said, "Put the parchment in the box."

"Is it done?"

"Put it in the box and put the box in your hand and stand there."

She did it.

Then I sat down on the floor with my back against the stone and my wrist under my nose and said, "He signed it."

She stood with the box in her hands.

"He signed it himself. His own hand. There was no other hand on the pen." I looked up at her. "And there was a hand on his."

She did not move.

"Say it again."

"Your father wrote his name with his own right hand. There was a scar on the back of his thumb and the cuff of a blue coat and he wrote it slowly and he stopped in the middle." I got my breath. "And while he was stopped a second hand came down on the back of his and lay there, and it did not force him and it did not guide him and it did not hold him down. It lay on him. And he felt it and he stopped and he thought about it for two breaths, and then he went on and finished the name with that hand on him."

Judith stood in the middle of the shed with the tin box in her hands.

"And then?" she said.

"And then neither of them moved," I said. "The pen was off the paper and neither of them moved, for as long as a breath, and then the other hand went away."

She looked at the box.

"There was somebody else in the room," I said. "Standing back from the table, on the left, near the fire. He was not writing and he was not signing and he was not the man with the ring. He was breathing slow and once he moved his feet."

She sat with the box in her lap.

"Three men," she said.

"Three men and a covenant and a fire."

"And you do not know which was which."

"I know which was your father, because of the scar and the blue coat," I said. "The rest is a hand and a ring and a man breathing, and none of them has a name on it, and I am not going to put one on."

She looked at the box.

"Do you know who it was?"

"No."

"You saw the hand."

"I saw a hand and a ring and a cuff," I said. "I did not see a face and a thing does not give me a face. I got what the paper had in it, and the paper had a table and two hands and a fire, and it did not have a man's name in it and it never will."

She came and sat down on the floor beside me with the box in her lap, which is not a thing I had expected, and we sat there with our backs against the stone and the frost in the corners.

"He knew," she said.

"He knew."

"He knew there was a hand on him and he went on and finished it."

"Yes."

She sat with that for a while.

"Then he was not forced," she said.

"No."

"And he was not tricked."

"No."

"Then what was he?"

I thought about the four hours in the passage and the account book in the loft and a man going down to the north end at eleven at night with a tarpaulin on his mind.

"He was a man who had a hand on him," I said, "and who had been carrying a thing for twelve years that he could not mend, and who was told by a man he trusted that if he signed this paper the wharf would not go to the bank in November and forty men would keep their work, and he thought about it for two breaths with that hand on him and he decided to be the man who signed."

Judith put her head back against the stone.

"That is worse," she said.

"It is not worse. It is exact."

"It is worse," she said. "If he had been forced I could have hated somebody. If he had been tricked I could have hated somebody. He was not forced and he was not tricked. He was asked, and he said yes, with a hand on him and his eyes open, and there is nobody in England I can be angry with except my father."

I did not say anything, because there was nothing to say that was not a comfort, and I had promised her no comforts.

We sat on the floor of the beam house for a while.

Then she said, "What did the ring look like?"

"Gold. Wide. On the fourth finger of the right hand. The seal was turned inward, so I did not see the device, only the flat and the edge of it." I wiped my mouth. "There was a mark on the skin where it pressed. He was a heavy man or he had big hands. The fingers came across the knuckles."

"Mr. Tarrant has a ring like that."

I turned my head.

"Does he?"

"He has worn it since I was a child. It is his father's and it has a bird on it, or a thing with wings, and he turns it inward when he is thinking." She looked at me. "He turns it inward, Mrs. Rooke. I have watched him do it at this table for eleven years and I have never once thought about it."

I sat with my back against the cold stone.

"Judith," I said, "there were two men at that table. There was your father and there was the man with the hand. And a covenant needs a witness, and a witness is in the room, and Mr. Bedloe witnessed it."

She looked at me.

"You are saying it was Bedloe."

"I am saying I do not know." I got up carefully. "And I am saying that a ring is not a man and a hand is not a name, and that I have been wrong once in this town by wanting an answer, and I am not going to be wrong twice by giving you one."

***

I went home and lay on the bed in my clothes until four in the afternoon.

Lying there I thought about a cuff.

Not the blue one. There was another cuff, and it was eleven months old, and it was on a coat that had been in a room in Collow in the January of 1832, and I had put my hand on it in a house where a man had died and I had got three seconds out of it and the three seconds had been this: a table, a paper, a hand writing, and another hand come down on it and lying there while the name was finished.

I had said so at the time. I had said it in a room with four people in it and one of them had written it down, and it had gone into a report that went to a court, and it had been the thing that broke that case open, and I had not thought about it since except as a thing I had done well.

That was eleven months ago.

I lay on the bed in Barrowfield and looked at the ceiling and understood, in the way a person understands a thing that has been in front of them for a year, that I had read the same gesture twice. Once on a coat in Collow and once on a covenant in Barrowfield, and the two men who had done it had never met and the two papers had nothing to do with one another, and the gesture was the same.

A hand laid on a hand. Not force. Not a guide. A weight, and a slow breath, and a man waiting for the end of something he has arranged.

When I could get up I got up and washed my face and went down and found Mrs. Sowerby in the back room with the accounts, and I sat down opposite her and said, "I have done it and it is done, and I am not going to tell you what it was, because it is not mine."

She put her pen down.

"Are you all right?"

"No."

"Will you be?"

"Yes." I put my hands on the table. "In six days."

She looked at my hands and then at my face and then she got up and put the kettle on the fire, and we sat there while it boiled and neither of us said anything.

Then she said, "I have been meaning to tell you a thing for a week and I have not told you because you had enough on you."

"Tell me."

"That paper," she said. "The one in the grate."

The kettle began to go.

"I swept it at five," she said, "and there was a corner of it that had not burned, and I picked it out, and I read it."

She said it looking at the fire and not at me.

"I could not read all of it. There was a word I did not know and a line I could not make out. But I read enough of it to know what it was, and I put it in the stove and I burned it, and then I went and stood in the yard for a while because I was ashamed."

I sat with my hands on the table.

"Mrs. Sowerby."

"Out with it."

"What did you do with the rest of the ash?"

She looked up.

"What?"

"The pan," I said. "The ash pan. What did you do with it?"

"I put it in the midden with the rest," she said. "It goes into the yard and then it goes onto the garden in the spring. Why?"

I got up and got my coat.

"Because there was a corner left on the hearth at seven," I said, "and you had swept at five, and you burned what you found in the stove, and the pan went out to the midden, and somebody put that corner back on the hearth between five and seven."

## Nineteen · The Cuff

I wrote to him on the eighth and I did not spare him, because he had asked me not to and because a man who has been an auditor for nine years does not want a letter with the corners rounded off.

I wrote the reading. I wrote the table and the green cloth and the blue cuff and the scar, and the second hand coming down and lying there, and the two breaths, and the name finished under it. I wrote the ring: gold, wide, fourth finger of the right hand, seal turned inward, a mark left on the skin.

I wrote the third man: the weight, the breath, the feet moving on the boards.

And then I wrote:

*I have read this gesture before. In Collow, in January, on a cuff. It is in the report and it is one of the things that broke that case, and I did not think about it again for eleven months.*

*Two men in two counties have done the same thing to two different hands, and I do not believe in a fashion in gestures. I believe in a way of doing a thing that is taught. Somebody taught them. It is not a thing a man arrives at by himself in an office with a fire in it.*

*Find me a school.*

***

The days between a letter and its answer are the longest days there are, and I filled them the way I fill everything, which is with work and with a list.

The ninth: the fourteen deputations, read and copied, all of them good, all of them in order, and not one of them mentioning the beam.

The tenth: the clerk of the peace's office in the county town by carrier, to ask whether the Farrow book had ever been deposited, and the answer, in four lines, that it had not and that a private book is a private book.

The eleventh: the marriage. It was being drawn. Bedloe had the instructions and would not show me the draft, and said so to my face, and said why, which was that a draft is not a thing anybody is entitled to see until it is engrossed, including the woman it is about.

The eleventh: Judith.

She came into the shop at four in the afternoon with snow on her shawl, the first snow of that winter, and she stood by the stove and held her hands out to it and did not say anything for a while.

"You know," she said at last.

"I know."

"You have known since Thursday."

"I have known since Thursday."

She turned round with her back to the stove.

"Then why have you not told me?"

"Because you asked me not to tell you at once," I said, "and because I have been waiting for you to come and ask, and because a thing like this is not handed to a person in a shop with the door open."

She stood there with the snow melting on her shoulders.

"Mrs. Rooke," she said, "I have thought about it for six days and I have come to a conclusion, and the conclusion is that I do not want to know all of it."

I put down the pen.

"Say that again."

"I do not want to know all of it," she said. "I have had eleven years of wanting to know all of it and it has eaten me, and now there is a woman in this town who has been in a room with that paper and has come out of it bleeding, and I find that I do not want the whole of it. I want one thing."

"Ask it."

"Did he stop?"

I looked at her.

"Yes," I said. "He stopped."

"For how long?"

"Two breaths."

She stood with her hands out to the stove.

"Two breaths," she said. "That is not very long."

"It is a very long time," I said, "when your hand is on a paper and another man's hand is on yours and there are forty men's wages in the room."

She nodded slowly.

"Then that is what I will have," she said. "That is the thing I will keep. He stopped for two breaths, and I am going to hold on to that for the rest of my life, and you will not tell me the rest until I ask you, and I am not going to ask you."

"Judith."

"I know what I am doing," she said. "I am twenty-one years old and I have just decided to be ignorant about one thing, and it is the first thing I have ever decided for myself. I am not going to be talked out of it."

She took her shawl off the chair and put it round her shoulders.

"There is a dinner on the twenty-fourth," she said. "Christmas. You will come."

"I will come."

"And your husband?"

"He is at Collow for the audit," I said. "He cannot leave it. I wrote to him in November and told him not to ask me about it and he has not asked."

Judith stood with the door half open and the snow coming in round her.

"Then there is not one man in this town who is not afraid of Mr. Tarrant," she said, "and I am going to have to do it myself."

She went out into the snow.

***

The twelfth: Tarrant.

I went up to the house on the market cross because his clerk sent word that he was not well and would see nobody, and I went anyway, and the clerk let me in because a clerk will always let in a woman he cannot think of a reason to keep out.

He was in a chair by the window with a rug over his knees and he had a cold in the chest and he looked ten years older than he had at the dinner.

"Mrs. Rooke."

"Mr. Tarrant."

"You have come to look at me and decide whether I am dying."

"I have come to look at your hands," I said, "and I am going to tell you why, because I am too tired to be clever."

He looked at me for a moment.

Then he put his hands out on the arms of the chair, palm down, and turned them over, and there was a ring on the fourth finger of the right hand, gold and wide, with the seal turned inward.

"That is my father's ring," he said. "I have worn it since 1825."

"I know."

"You have seen it before."

"I have seen the mark of it," I said, "on a man's skin, in a room in this town, in October 1821, with a fire in it."

Tarrant sat quite still.

Then he said, "Say the rest of it, and say it in the plain words, because I have a cold in my chest and I have not the strength for a scene."

I sat down opposite him.

"Your father held Amos Kell's hand on that covenant while he finished his name," I said. "He did not force him and he did not guide him. He laid his hand on the back of Amos Kell's hand and left it there, and Amos Kell felt it and stopped and thought for two breaths and went on."

Tarrant closed his eyes.

"Yes," he said.

I had not expected that.

"You know."

"I have known since I was nineteen," he said. "I was in the counting house that afternoon. Not in the room. In the counting house, with the door open, and I heard my father say, in the voice he used when he had decided a thing, 'Put your hand to it, Kell.' And I heard nothing else for a long while, and then I heard the pen."

He opened his eyes.

"I have worn his ring for seven years," he said, "and I turn it inward when I am thinking, and I have done it since I was a boy because he did it, and I did not know I did it until a girl of nineteen told me at a dinner table that I did it."

"Judith?"

"Judith," he said, "at the dinner, in front of twenty-two people, and she said it kindly, and I have not been able to wear the ring since without thinking about it." He looked at his hand. "Her face when she said it. She had a piece of mutton on her fork and she put it down and did not take it up again, and then she asked the parson to pass the salt."

"Was your father a wicked man?"

"My father was a frightened man," Tarrant said, "and I have spent seven years being the man who says that in a tone that excuses him, and I am not going to do it today. He was frightened and he was clever and he did a wicked thing in a good cause, which is the worst kind, and then he went home and had his dinner."

***

I went to Bedloe's office on the same afternoon with the afternoon going off the windows.

He was at the desk with the shutters half closed and a candle lit, and he looked at me and said, "You have been to Tarrant's."

"How do you know?"

"Because your face has the look a person has when they have been told a thing they already knew." He put his pen down. "Sit down."

I sat down.

"Who taught you?" I said.

He did not pretend not to understand.

"Farrow."

"And who taught Farrow?"

"He was in the Six Clerks' Office in Chancery from 1796 to 1804," Bedloe said. "Before that he was articled in a house in Symond's Inn, and before that he was a boy in a school in Croydon, and I do not know what they taught him there." He folded his hands. "You are asking after a school, and there is no school. You are asking after a method, and the method is in every office in England and it has been in every office in England since there were offices."

"How does a man learn it?"

"He has it done to him," Bedloe said. "I had it done to me in September 1809, in this room, at that table, when I was seventeen years of age and had been three weeks in this house. Farrow put the articles in front of me and put his hand on the back of my hand and said, 'Sign, boy,' and I signed."

"What were the articles?"

"Articles of clerkship. Five years. One hundred and twenty pounds premium, paid by my uncle, and it was the best hundred and twenty pounds anybody ever spent on me." He looked at the table. "It was a fair document. I have read it since, twice, and there is nothing in it that a court would look at. And I can tell you exactly what I felt when that hand came down, and what I felt was that I had been chosen."

I sat with that.

"Mr. Bedloe."

"Proceed."

"When you drew the covenant in 1821 and Amos Kell came to that table, did you put your hand on him?"

Bedloe looked at me for a long time.

"No," he said. "I was the witness. I stood at the end of the table with the second skin and the seal, and I watched Mr. Tarrant the elder put his hand on that man's hand and I watched him sign." He picked up his pen again and put it down again. "And I have thought about it every year since, and the thing I have thought is not that I should have stopped it. The thing I have thought is that I was glad it was not me."

***

The letter came on the thirteenth.

It was four sheets and the last one was half full, and it was in his hand, which was small and upright and not a scholar's, and it began without any of the ordinary things.

*I have read your letter four times. I am going to write to you now about being a boy, which I have never done, and I am going to ask you to burn this when you have read it, and I know that you will not.*

*When I was seventeen my father put my hand on a deed and put his hand on mine and made me sign it.*

*I want to be exact, because you will want it exact. He did not force me. He stood behind my chair and he put his right hand flat on the back of my right hand and he said, "Sign it," and I signed it. I did not know what it was. I was seventeen and I had been in his office a year and I signed what was put in front of me and I did not read it, and my hand did what it was told with his hand on it.*

*I have thought about that hand for twenty-seven years.*

*And I am going to say the thing that I have never said to anybody, and I am going to say it to you because you have just read a covenant and because you are the only person I know who will not make it into a story.*

*I did not mind it.*

*That is the truth of it. I did not mind it. It was warm and it was my father and he was pleased with me, and I signed my name and he took his hand off and put it on my shoulder and said, "That is my boy," and I have never in my life been as happy as I was in that minute.*

*I found out what the deed was nine years later and it was nothing. It was a lease of a warehouse and it was lawful and it was fair and it did not hurt anybody, and the whole of the thing I have been carrying for twenty-seven years is a warm hand and a man saying "That is my boy."*

*So when you tell me that a hand was laid on Amos Kell's hand in a room with a fire in it, I am not going to tell you that he was forced. A forced man pulls away. You said he did not pull away. He stopped and thought for two breaths and went on, and I know exactly what those two breaths were, because I have had them.*

*He was thinking: if I do this, my father's hand is on me and I am his and he is pleased.*

*And then he signed.*

*A hand held down is not a hand that has not signed. The signature is real. The signature is his. That is what you must tell the girl, and it is the worst thing in the letter and I have left it to the end of the page on purpose: her father signed, and he signed because a man he trusted put a hand on him and made him feel that he was still a son.*

*I have written that down and I am not going to cross it out.*

*The school you want does not exist. There is no school where they teach a man to put his hand on another man's hand. There is only a way of doing business in England that has been handed down in offices from master to clerk since there were offices, and it is taught by being done to you when you are seventeen.*

*I was taught it by my father. Somebody taught your Mr. Bedloe. Somebody taught whoever taught him.*

*Ask Bedloe who taught him. Ask him when he was seventeen.*

Then, under that, after a gap:

*I signed a thing when I was seventeen and I have never asked what it was, and I found out nine years later by accident and it was nothing, and I have never told anybody that either.*

*I am coming on the twenty-third. I am not asking. The audit is on the twenty-second and I can be at Barrowfield by the afternoon of the twenty-third if the roads hold, and if they do not hold I will be there on the twenty-fourth, and I will not write again to say which.*

*E. Rooke*

***

I read it four times and then I sat with it in my lap.

He had done a thing I had not asked him to do, which was to put down on paper the worst thing about himself, and he had done it in order to be of use to a girl he had never met.

That is not the same as being asked.

I have thought about that a great deal since. A man writes down the worst thing about himself in a letter to his wife, and it is courage, and it is also a gift that has not been asked for, and a gift that has not been asked for is a hand on the back of a hand whatever else it is.

I put the letter in the box with the others and I did not answer it for two days.

When I did answer it, I wrote four lines and I did not thank him, because I did not know at the time whether thanks was the right thing, and I have never been able to decide since.

## Twenty · The Letter He Signed

The post came at half past ten on the sixteenth and it was a big envelope, stiff, with a seal on the back the size of a shilling and the seal was the consistory court at York.

I knew the seal. I had seen it twice in my life and both times it had meant six weeks of work and two pounds eleven.

I paid the twopence and took it up to my room and sat on the bed with my coat on and opened it with the penknife.

There were two papers in it.

The first was printed and had blanks filled in by a clerk, and it said that a petition had been presented by Mercy Rooke, wife of Edward Rooke of Nether Collow, in the county of, and that the said petition would be heard in the consistory court on the eighth day of January next, and that the petitioner should appear by herself or her proctor, and that the respondent should appear, and that the respondent was the Reverend —

I did not read the rest of it at once.

I read the second paper, which was the petition itself.

Three sheets, copied fair, in a clerk's hand, with a stamp at the head.

*In the Consistory Court of York.*

*The humble petition of Mercy Rooke, of Nether Collow, in the county of, wife of Edward Rooke, esquire.*

*Sheweth:*

*That the parish of Barrowfield in this diocese holds, among other charitable endowments, a house of alms for four poor persons, being the office of the almshouse, of the annual value of one pound six shillings.*

*That the said almshouse is administered by the holder of the said office, and that no account of the said charity has been presented to the ordinary since the year 1821, nor any visitation held.*

*That there are at present two persons in the said house, one of them a woman of the age of forty years who has been maintained there since the year 1827, and that the said charity is in the gift of a person of the age of twenty-one years.*

*Your petitioner therefore prays that a visitation may be directed, and that the accounts of the said charity may be examined, and that such further order may be made as to this court shall seem just.*

*And your petitioner will ever pray.*

*Mercy Rooke*

*By the hand of her husband, Edward Rooke, esquire, the petitioner being a married woman and unable to sign or sue in her own person.*

***

I sat on the bed with the two papers and did not move for a long time.

It was my petition. Every line of it was a thing I had thought of in the back room of that shop with the day book open. The almshouse was the one hole in the fourteen that a court could look into without touching the covenant and without touching the beam and without costing a hundred pounds in fees. I had thought of it on the ninth of December and I had written it out and I had looked at it and I had put it in the stove.

I had not told him about it. I had told him about the almshouse and the woman in the first room and the two of them in that row of four, and he had done the rest.

And at the foot of the last sheet, in a hand I knew better than my own:

*By the hand of her husband, Edward Rooke, esquire, the petitioner being a married woman and unable to sign or sue in her own person.*

I read that line eleven times.

There is a thing in it that no woman in England can get round and it is three words of it: *unable to sign*. Not unwilling. Not forbidden by her husband. Unable, as a matter of law, as a fact about her person, in the same way that a man under age is unable. I had known it since I was ten years old and it had never once cost me anything, because until that winter there had never been a paper in England that I wanted to put my name to. A woman may hold fourteen offices and may not sign her name to a sheet of paper about them. It is not a flaw in the law. It is the law working exactly as it was made to work.

Then I got up and put my hat on and went out and walked to Bedloe's office so fast that I had to stop at the corner of the row and hold the rail.

***

He was with a client. I stood in the passage and would not go away and the client came out after four minutes and looked at me and I went in.

Bedloe was standing behind his desk.

"Mrs. Rooke."

"Did you draw this?"

I put the petition on the desk.

He looked at it.

"No," he said. "I have never seen it."

"Then who did?"

"A proctor in York, or a clerk in the registry, or any of nine men in this diocese who do that work." He picked up the first sheet and read it. "It is well drawn. It is better drawn than I would have drawn it. Whoever did it knew exactly how far a consistory court will reach and stopped one inch short of the place where it would be told to mind its own business."

"Read the last line."

He read the last line.

"Ah," he said.

"Mr. Bedloe."

"Sit down."

"I do not want to sit down."

"Sit down, Mrs. Rooke, because if you do not sit down you will say something in this passage that the clerk will hear and that will be all over this town by four." He waited until I sat. "Now. Is it your petition?"

"Yes."

"Did you write it?"

"I wrote a version of it and put it in the stove."

"Then he has not forged anything," Bedloe said. "He has taken a thing you told him in a letter and he has had it drawn properly and he has signed it in your name as your husband and he has a right to do it, and there is no court in England that will hear a word against it."

"He has signed my name."

"He has signed his own and described himself as your hand." Bedloe put the paper down. "Mrs. Rooke, I am going to tell you the law and then I am going to tell you what I think, and you will not like either. The law is this: a married woman cannot petition, plead, sue, contract, or be sued, and everything she has is her husband's to administer. If she has a grievance the law knows of, her husband brings it. There is no other door. Your husband has walked through the only door there is and he has done it properly and it will be heard on the eighth of January."

"And if I want it stopped?"

"Then you will sign a notice of withdrawal and he will countersign it," Bedloe said, "and you will not be able to sign it without him."

I sat in that chair and looked at the two papers on the desk.

"There," I said. "That is it. That is the whole of it."

"It is."

"He did not do it to hurt me."

"No."

"He did it because he is a man who sees a door and walks through it and it did not once occur to him that the door being there was the injury." I put my hand flat on the petition. "And he wrote that line. He wrote it out himself. *Being a married woman and unable to sign or sue in her own person.* He wrote it down and he sent it to a court and he thought he was helping me."

"Yes."

"And that is the worst thing that has happened to me since I came to this town," I said, "and it was done by a man who loves me, out of a room a hundred and eleven miles away, with a pen."

Bedloe sat down behind his desk.

"Mrs. Rooke," he said, "may I say a thing that is not law?"

"Say it."

"I have drawn four hundred settlements and I have watched four hundred women go through that door and not one of them ever turned round and looked at it." He folded his hands. "You have turned round. That is not a small thing and it is not going to do you any good at all, and I want you to know that I have seen it, because I am fifty-one and I do not expect to see it again."

***

I walked home the long way round by the wharf.

It was cold and the basin had a skin of ice on it at the edges and the boats were laid up and there was nobody on the wharf but the old man on his stool with a brazier, and he had a blanket round him and his one sharp eye and he said, "Shut," as I went past, which was his whole conversation and had been for eleven years.

I stopped.

"Who pays you?" I said.

He looked up.

"What?"

"Nine shillings a week and a coal allowance. Who pays it?"

"Mr. Tarrant," he said. "These eleven years."

"And before that?"

"Before that it was Mr. Kell, and I had a different stool and it was by the gate." He pulled the blanket up. "Why?"

"Because the office of the almshouse is one pound six a year," I said, "and there are two people in it, and that is not one pound six a year of anything, and somebody has been paying the difference for eleven years."

The old man looked at me with his one sharp eye.

"You are the letter," he said.

"I am."

"Then I will tell you a thing and you can put it in your book." He leaned forward. "There is no coal allowance. There never was. Mr. Tarrant gives me a shilling at Christmas and says it is the allowance and I say thank you, and the nine shillings comes out of the office of the market, which is his, and it is not in any book anywhere, and I have been sitting on this stool for eleven years being paid out of a man's own pocket and told it was a fee."

I stood there with my hands in my pockets.

"Why are you telling me?"

"Because you asked," he said. "Nobody has asked in eleven years. They ask what is inside the shed and they ask who has the key and they do not ask who pays the man on the stool." He sat back. "And because I am seventy-one and I have not got long, and a thing that is not in a book is a thing that is going to be lost."

***

Mrs. Sowerby was at the counter with a woman from the end of the street when I came in, and she looked up and I saw in her face that she knew.

The woman went out.

"The postmaster," Mrs. Sowerby said.

"The postmaster."

"He does not read the letters. He reads the seals." She put her hands on the counter. "A consistory seal, in this town, in December. It was all over the market by eleven and by two it was that you had cited the parson."

"I have not cited the parson."

"That is what I told them." She looked at me. "What have you done?"

"I have not done anything. My husband has petitioned the consistory court in my name for a visitation of the almshouse, and it will be heard on the eighth of January, and I found out this morning."

Mrs. Sowerby stood with her hands on the counter.

"Your husband."

"My husband."

"Without asking you."

"Without asking me."

She was quiet for a moment.

"Mrs. Rooke," she said, "I am going to say a thing and you will not like it. I have kept a shop in this town nine years and I know how a street talks. Half of them will say you set your husband on. The other half will say he set himself on and you are to be pitied. Neither half will call it a scandal. They will say it to me over this counter with a quarter of tea in their hand. By Friday it will be a thing that was done, and the only person in this town still angry about it will be you."

I stood in the door with my hand flat on the frame and the cold coming off the street, and for a moment I could not think of one thing to say.

"That is the worst thing anybody has said to me since I came here," I said.

"It is the truest."

"Yes."

***

I went up to my room at four and got out the box and took out the paper I had written on the twentieth of November and crossed through and put away.

I want to put down what was on it, because it is the only thing in this book that I have never shown anybody, and because the whole of the rest of it turns on this.

It was a petition. Not to the consistory. To the bishop, in my own hand, in the plain form, asking for a visitation of the parish of Barrowfield. Eleven lines. And under the eleven lines, in the space where the petitioner signs, I had written *Mercy Rooke, wife of Edward Rooke*, and then I had crossed out the whole of it, hard, three times, with the pen.

I had crossed it out because a woman who petitions is a woman who has made herself a party, and a party cannot be a witness, and I had known even then that if I put my name to it I would lose the only thing I had, which was standing outside it with a book.

And a man a hundred and eleven miles away had taken the same eleven lines and put my name to them and sent them to York.

I sat on the bed with the two papers in my lap, the one I had crossed out and the one he had signed, and I looked at them for a long time.

And I could not think of one person in England to ask about it.

That is the thing I want to put down. Not the anger. The other thing, which came after the anger and was worse. I sat on a bed in a coal town with two papers in my lap and I went through every person I knew, one at a time, and there was not one of them I could put this in front of.

Judith was twenty-one and had her own father in her hands.

Mrs. Sowerby would have said, "Men are like that," and meant it kindly.

Bedloe would have told me the law, which I already had.

Tarrant would have said something shrewd and true and would have gone home and had his dinner.

And the man who had done it was the man I would have asked.

***

I sat at the table with a candle and wrote to him.

I wrote four lines.

*I have the citation. I did not ask for it and I did not know of it.*

*I will not withdraw it. I cannot withdraw it without your signature, and I will not ask you for your signature, and if you cannot see what is in that sentence then I will not be the one who explains it.*

Then, because I had sat with it for an hour and could not leave it there:

*You took my eleven lines out of a letter and you put them into an office and you signed your description of me at the foot of them, and you did it in one afternoon what has taken me seven weeks and a room full of paper. I am not writing to say that you were wrong about the almshouse. You were not wrong about the almshouse. It is the only hole in that wall and you found it from a hundred and eleven miles away in a week.*

*I am writing to say that I have spent my whole life being a woman who is useful to people, and that I have been proud of it, and that this is the first time anybody has been useful to me without asking, and I have found out that I would rather be the one who does it.*

*If you do not understand that, we are going to have a very long winter.*

I sealed it and put it by and sat looking at the candle.

Then I took the crossed-out petition out of the box and read it again, the eleven lines and the name and the three hard strokes through it, and I put it on the table beside the citation, the one I had written and not sent and the one he had written and sent, and I looked at the two of them for a long time.

Then I put them both back in the box and went to bed.

## Twenty-One · What He Was Doing

On the eighteenth of December I went to Bedloe and asked him what would happen on the eighth of January if the court did what the petition asked.

He was in his office with the shutters open and no fire, which was the first thing that told me something was wrong with him.

"Sit down," he said. "And do not ask me how I am."

"How are you?"

"Worse than I have been and better than I look." He put his hands on the desk. "You want to know what a visitation is."

"I want to know what it will find."
