"A visitation is the bishop's officer coming into a parish with a commission and a clerk and a right to see every book that has anything to do with a charity or a faculty or a pew or a burial." He said it slowly, the way a man says a thing he has said a hundred times and is saying properly because the person in front of him will use it. "He will come in February or March. He will sit in the vestry with the parson and the churchwardens and he will ask for the accounts of the almshouse, and he will find that there are none, and then he will ask for the accounts of the parish, and the parish will produce them because the parish has nothing to hide."

"And the fourteen offices?"

"The offices are not charities and they are not faculties and a visitation does not touch them." Bedloe looked at me. "But."

"But."

"But a visitation sits in a room with the parson and the churchwardens and any man in the parish who has a grievance and a shilling, and a visitation has a clerk with a book, and any man who stands up in that room and says a thing under oath has said it on record and there is no getting it out again." He put his finger on the desk. "You have not petitioned for the beam. You cannot. But you have opened a door into a room where a man can say a thing about the beam and be written down, and there are forty men on that wharf who know, and one of them will say it."

I sat with that.

"That is what he did," I said.

"That is what he did," Bedloe said. "Your husband has never been to Barrowfield and does not know a coal beam from a gate, and he has found the one door in this wall that leads to a room where the truth of the whole thing can be said by somebody else under oath." He sat back. "Mrs. Rooke, I have been in this trade thirty-one years and I would not have found it. I would have gone at the covenant and lost, and I would have gone at the beam and lost, and it would have cost four hundred pounds."

***

I walked home by the market cross and stopped at the pump, which was mended, and stood looking at it.

A man a hundred and eleven miles away, in a house in Collow, with a cloth on the table at four o'clock, had read a letter about a beam and a shop and an almshouse, and had found the one door.

I had been in Barrowfield for two months. I had read the covenant and the book and the brass and the loft. I knew the weights of everything and the price of everything and the name of every man on that wharf.

I had spent two months standing outside it with a book, and he had stood outside it for one afternoon and seen the door.

That is not a thing a woman can be told about herself without it going in deep.

***

I burned the petition on the nineteenth.

I did it in the grate in my room with the door shut, and I did it deliberately, and I want to put down why.

Because a woman who keeps a crossed-out paper keeps a weapon. A crossed-out paper in a box is a thing you can produce in a year and say, *Look, I had thought of it too.* It is the cheapest kind of being right.

And because it was not mine any more. It was in York now, in a registry, in a clerk's hand, with my name at the foot in a stranger's writing, and there was no version of it left for me to keep.

I burned it and watched the ash and then opened the window and let the cold in.

And then I went down and opened the till under the weights and took out the corner of paper with four words on it, and I did not burn that one.

I had found out who put it back. It was Mrs. Sowerby, and she told me on the twelfth without being asked twice, and she was not ashamed of putting it back; she was ashamed of having read it, and she had given me the better of the two stories, which is what people do when they would rather be a person who burned a thing than a person who read it and kept it.

*put your hand flat*

I put it back in the till under the weights. It is there now. One day I am going to have to say those four words out loud to somebody in a room, and when I do I would rather have the paper in my hand.

***

Ellerbeck came on the nineteenth in the afternoon.

He was a big man in a good brown coat with flour on his cuffs, and he took his hat off in the shop and stood turning it round in his hands, and he had come himself and not sent a man, which I have learned to notice.

"Mrs. Rooke."

"Mr. Ellerbeck."

"You will know what I am here about."

"I know what you are a candidate for."

"That is the word," he said. "I have been a candidate for three months and I have never once used the word and now you have said it out loud in a shop and I find that I am glad." He turned the hat. "I am fifty-six years of age and I buried my wife in March, and I have four children, and the youngest is eleven. I want a mother for them and I want a woman who can hold an office and I have been to Mr. Bedloe twice about the settlement."

"And?"

"And I am going to withdraw."

I put down what I had in my hand.

"Say why."

"Because I heard a thing in the market on Tuesday," he said. "I heard that the letter-writer's husband had petitioned the consistory, and that the bishop's officer would be coming, and that there would be a visitation in the spring." He looked up. "And when I heard it I did not think, Thank God, somebody is going to get to the bottom of it. I thought, I hope it is over before the wedding."

He said it plainly and he did not excuse himself.

"That is what I thought, Mrs. Rooke, and I have been thinking about it since Tuesday, and the thing I have come to is that a man who thinks that is not the man to marry a woman with a lawsuit on her." He put the hat on. "I am not a bad man. I am a miller and I have four children and I want a quiet house. That is all of it, and I would rather say it to your face than have it said of me in the market."

I stood with my hands on the counter.

"Mr. Ellerbeck," I said, "there is a girl in this town who has been priced and weighed and spoken for since she was ten years old, and you are the first man who has come into this shop and said a true thing about himself."

"That is a very low bar."

"It is the only bar there is in Barrowfield," I said, "and you have cleared it, and I am sorry you are going, and I would say the same to your face in front of the whole vestry."

He put his hat on and went to the door and stopped there.

"For what it is worth," he said, "I hope she gets it. The beam. I have bought coal off that wharf for twenty years and I have known the weight was wrong since 1824 and I have never said it, and I have a mill and a good name and I could have said it." He opened the door. "Tell her that. It is worth nothing but tell her that, because when the visitation comes there will be forty men in this town saying they always knew, and I want it on record that one of them said it before."

***

On the twentieth Tarrant sent for me.

He was worse. He was in the chair by the window and he had a man with him who was not a servant and who went out when I came in, and there was a writing desk by the chair with paper on it and three letters written and not sealed.

"Mrs. Rooke."

"You have been writing letters."

"I have been writing to my brother in Thorneley about a lease, and to the bank at Hull about the mortgage on this house, and to the bishop's registrar about the almshouse, because you may be sure he will write to me the moment your visitation is granted." He looked at me. "You have seen my nephew."

"I have."

"What did you make of him?"

"That he mended the pump and that he will not say so to anybody but a stranger."

Tarrant laughed, and it turned into a cough, and when it was over he wiped his mouth with a handkerchief.

"He will do," he said. "That is what I have decided and I have decided it in the last ten days and it has taken me ten days to say it. If she will have him, he will do."

"Will he let her keep the beam?"

Tarrant looked at me for a long moment.

"He said he would," he said. "He came in here on Tuesday and told me he had said a thing to you in the shop and could not get back from it, and he asked me whether I thought the law would let her. And I told him the law would let her, because the office is hers and no marriage puts it in his hands, and he sat down in that chair and looked at the fire for a while."

"And?"

"And then he said, 'Then I have been a fool about the wrong thing for six years.'" Tarrant put the handkerchief away. "I do not know what he meant. He is twenty-four and he talks like a man of thirty-five and every so often he says a thing that shows he is twenty-four."

I had nothing in my hands and did not know where to put them.

"Mr. Tarrant," I said, "there is a thing I have been meaning to ask you for a month and I have not asked it because I did not know how to put it, and now you are ill and I am going to ask it badly."

"Ask it badly."

"Were you in that room on the fourteenth of October 1821?"

He did not trouble to ask me what I meant.

"I was in the counting house," he said. "I was nineteen. My father had the door open and I heard him say, 'Put your hand to it, Kell,' and I heard the pen, and I did not go in, and I have never told anybody that in eleven years."

"Who else was there?"

"My father. Mr. Kell. Mr. Bedloe with the second skin." He looked at me. "And Mr. Whitworth. The elder. He had come up from Hull the day before and he was in the passage when I went down, and he said, 'It is a fine thing to watch a man be persuaded,' and I have been forty-nine years getting that sentence out of my head and I have not done it."

***

On the twenty-first, in the afternoon, I wrote to her.

I had the answer in my day book and had had it for sixteen days and had given her the one thing she asked for and nothing else.

I wrote:

*Miss Kell,*

*I have your answer. It is written out and sealed and it is in my box, and I will not send it to you and I will not tell you at your own table.*

*Come and ask me for it. Not to oblige me. Because a thing you have asked for is a thing you own, and a thing you have been given is a thing you owe.*

*I will be in the shop every morning and every afternoon until the fourteenth and after that I do not know.*

*M. Rooke.*

***

She came on the twenty-second, in the last of the light, when the shop was shut and I was locking the door.

She stood in the street with her hand on the rail and her breath coming out white.

"Give it me," she said.

"Say the question first."

She stood there.

"Is it that he was not himself?" she said. "At the end. In that room. Is it that he was not in his right mind?"

"That is not it."

"Is it that my father was made to?"

"That is not it either."

"Then say the question and let me answer it," she said, and her voice was not steady, and she had come out without her gloves.

"Say what you want to know," I said. "That is the question. Not the one you think you should ask, and not the one a court would ask. The one you want."

Judith Kell stood in the snow in the street outside a grocer's shop with her hands in her shawl.

"I want to know," she said, "whether my father was a good man."

I stood with the key in my hand.

"Yes," I said. "That is the question. And it is not a question a paper can answer, and it is not a question I can answer, and it is the reason you have been asking every other question for eleven years."

She stood very still.

"Then what is in the box?"

"The answer to the other question. The one you wrote out at four in the morning and put your hand over." I unlocked the door and pushed it open and the bell rang. "Come in and shut the door."

She came in and shut the door.

"Before I read it," I said, "there is a thing I have to ask you, and it is not the question and it does not need an answer tonight."

"Ask."

"In your father's bureau, when you were ten, you found a paper with three words at the end of it that you could read, and the hand was not your father's and it was not your mother's. You have had it eleven years. You wrote to me about it in May and you did not sign the letter."

She did not move.

"Who told you?"

"Mr. Bedloe's book told me. He enters a thing twice and the second one goes to the family." I put my hand flat on the counter. "The three words are *not yet asked*. He wrote them on the twenty-third of December 1821, four days after the fire, because your father came to him and he asked whether anybody had spoken for you, and your father said, not yet."

She stood in the middle of the shop with her shawl still on.

"Then the line in our book," she said, "is a man saying that nobody had asked me."

"It is a man saying that nobody had asked you, and it was true then, and it has been true for eleven years, and that is what you have been reading at night since you were ten."

She put her hand out and found the counter.

"Read the answer," she said. "Read it now."

I did not take her into the back room. I took her to the counter, and put the candle on it, and got out the day book and opened it at the page, and turned it round so that she could see it, and then I stood back.

"Read it," I said. "Aloud. It is not long."

She stood at the counter with her hands flat on the wood and read it, and it took her four minutes, and I watched her face and did not say anything.

Then she came to the bottom and stopped.

"Read the last three lines again," I said.

"I cannot."

"Then I will." I took the book and read them out, and my voice was steady and I had practised it. "The hand that was writing stopped. A second hand came down on it from the right and lay over it. The hand did not come off. It stopped for two breaths and then it finished the name."

She stood with her hands on her own side of the counter.

"There were three men in that room," she said.

"There were three men in that room." I turned the page. "Your father, who signed. A man with a ring, who put his hand on your father's hand and did not force him and did not guide him and left a mark on his skin. And a third man, standing back by the fire, breathing slow, who moved his feet once and who did not speak."

"Who was the third?"

"I do not know. Mr. Bedloe says he stood at the end of the table with the seal, and a witness stands at a table and a man who stands back by the fire is not a witness."

"And the man with the ring?"

"Mr. Tarrant's father," I said. "The ring is on his son's hand now and has been since 1825, and his son was in the counting house at nineteen and heard his father say, 'Put your hand to it, Kell,' and did not go in."

She stood there.

"That is all of it," she said.

"That is all of it. Your father signed with his own hand and he did it with a hand on him, and he could have stopped, and he did stop, for two breaths, and then he went on." I closed the book. "And the man who put his hand on him is dead, and the man who stood by the fire I cannot name, and the man who witnessed it is fifty-one and has been drawing papers in this town for thirty-one years and told me all of it himself, and none of the three of them is going to be punished by anything except the inside of his own head."

Judith stood at the counter for a while.

"Then what was the point," she said, "of the whole of it?"

I did not answer, because there is no answer to that which is not a comfort.

"That is not a question for me," I said. "That is a question you will be asking at forty and again at fifty, and I could give you a sentence now that would hold you for a month and would be a lie."

She picked up her shawl.

"Mrs. Rooke," she said, "you have given me exactly what I asked for."

"Yes."

"Do you know how rare that is?"

"No."

"Neither did I," she said, "until this evening."

She got to the door and stopped.

"On the fourteenth of January," she said. "The audit. It is in the market hall and every office-holder in this parish stands up and gives his account and the town can hear it."

"It is."

"Then I am going to stand up," she said, "and I am going to give my account, and when it is done I am going to say four words, and I have had eleven years to choose the four words and I have chosen them."

"Say them to me."

"No," she said. "You will hear them with everybody else."

She went out into the snow, and the bell over the door went on ringing.

## Twenty-Two · Three Days

On the twenty-third she was not at the wharf.

I knew before seven because I had got into the habit of going down at seven to see the first carts and she was always there before me, standing at the corner of the warehouse with her hands in her shawl, counting baskets without knowing she was doing it.

The old man on the stool said, "Not today."

"Did she send word?"

"She does not send word. She comes or she does not come."

I went up to Kell's at nine.

The servant with the pinned sleeves opened the door and her face did the thing a servant's face does when the household has decided something and the door has not been told.

"Miss Kell is not well."

"Is she in the house?"

"She is in her room."

"Then tell her I am here and that I will wait in the passage and that I have brought nothing with me and want nothing from her."

I waited in the passage for an hour and ten minutes.

At the end of it the servant came down and said, "She says will you come up."

I went up.

Judith was in her room with the curtains half drawn and the fire low, sitting in a chair by the window with a blanket over her knees and her hair down, and she had not slept.

"I have done nothing for two days," she said.

"I know."

"I have not eaten and I have not washed my hair and I have not opened the shutters and Mrs. Sowerby has been to the door twice and I have not gone down." She looked at me. "Is this what it is like?"

"What?"

"Knowing."

I sat down on the end of the bed.

"Yes," I said. "For a while."

"How long is a while?"

"I do not know. It is different in different people and it is longer than anybody tells you and shorter than it feels." I looked at the fire. "When my mother died I did four months of work in six weeks and could not tell anybody what I had done."

She sat with her hands in the blanket.

"Everybody in this town thinks I am ill."

"Then let them."

"They will send things."

"Then let them send things."

She looked at me.

"You are not going to be kind to me," she said.

"No."

"Good," she said, and put her head back against the chair, and closed her eyes, and I sat there for another half hour and then went down and told the servant to make some broth and to put a little salt in it, because a person who has not eaten for two days cannot taste anything and salt is the only thing that gets through.

***

Rooke came at four in the afternoon.

I was in the shop with the door half shut and the snow coming down in the street and the light going, and I heard a cart stop at the top of the market and a man's voice asking the way to the grocer's, and then a while, and then the bell over the door went.

He stood in the doorway with snow on his shoulders and his hat in his hand and his coat buttoned wrong, and he had come a hundred and eighteen miles on a carrier and then walked up a hill in the snow with a bag.

"Mrs. Rooke," he said.

"Mr. Rooke."

"You are early," I said. "You said the afternoon."

"The roads held," he said.

And then we stood there, four feet apart, with the door open and the snow coming in and the whole of it between us: the citation, the line at the foot of the petition, the twelve days in a cold room, the smell of a fire, the sound of a bell.

"I am still angry with you," I said.

"I know."

"I am going to be angry with you for a while and it is not going to be convenient and I am not going to pretend it is over because you have come a hundred and eighteen miles."

"I know that too."

"Then come in and shut the door, because there is a draught and the flour is in the front."

He came in and shut the door and put his bag down and stood in the middle of a grocer's shop with the snow melting off him, and Mrs. Sowerby came out of the back room and looked at him and looked at me and said, "You will be Mr. Rooke," and he said, "I will," and she said, "Then you will want something hot and you will have it in the back room and not in my shop."

***

We sat in the back room with the door open and a pot of tea and neither of us said anything for a while.

He had aged. That was the first thing. Two months is not a long time and he had aged, and I sat across a table from him and looked at the lines round his eyes and understood that a man who writes four-line letters about a roof and does not say the other thing does not sleep well either.

"The beam," he said at last.

"The beam."

"Tell me the whole of it, from the beginning, and do not leave out the parts you think I will not like, because I will find them out and it will be worse."

So I told him.

It took an hour and a quarter and two pots and I told him the beam house and the notches and the loft and the account book and the case of brass and the two women in the almshouse and the nine numbers and the seven blanks and the £3,047 that nobody would ever get back.

He listened without saying anything and once he got up and stood at the window with his back to me, which I have learned is what he does when he does not want his face looked at.

At the end he said, "And the covenant."

"Your wife read it," I said, "and your wife got a hand on a hand and a ring and two breaths, and your wife has been bleeding out of her nose and lying on a bed for a day, and you may ask me what you like about it except whether I am all right, because I am not and I am not going to discuss it."

He turned round.

"I was not going to ask that."

"What were you going to ask?"

"Whether you want me to leave."

I sat with my hands round the cup.

"No," I said. "That is the only thing in it I would swear to."

I got up to put the pot on the hob and he got up at the same moment and there was a yard of floor between us and neither of us stepped out of it.

"Rooke."

"Go on."

"Come here."

He came. I put both hands flat on his chest and held him off, which was not what he expected, and I mean to be honest about why I did it: I wanted to feel him be stoppable.

"You may kiss me," I said, "and then I am going to be angry with you again, and you are not to take it as a thing that has been settled."

"I would not take it as anything," he said. "I have been in a cart for two days and I have thought about very little else, and I am not going to stand in a back room and pretend otherwise."

Then he put a hand on my jaw and kissed me, and it was not the way he had kissed me in Collow. In Collow it had been careful, in a room with the curtains shut, a man who had got what he had come for and meant to be decent about it afterwards.

This was not careful. It was badly aimed and it knocked my cap crooked and it put my back against the dresser, and I had a hand in his coat and I did not take it out, and when it ended he kept his forehead against mine and neither of us said anything for a while.

"That is all," I said at last.

"That is all," he said.

"Tonight I am going to sit in the chair."

"I know," he said. "I made the chair up before I came down."

***

That night Mrs. Sowerby put him in the room over the shop, because there is one room over the shop and no other room in the house, and she said so to my face and looked at me while she said it, and I said, "He is my husband," and she said, "I am aware of that, Mrs. Rooke, and I am also aware that a woman who has been shouting at a man for an hour does not want him in her bed on the first night."

We went up at ten.

He stood in the middle of the room with his bag and looked at the bed and then at the floor and then at the chair, and I watched him do the arithmetic.

"There is a chair," he said.

"There is a chair and there is a bed and there is a floor, and it is December and the floor is stone under the boards."

"Then I will take the chair."

"You will take the bed and I will take the chair."

"That is not a discussion I am going to win," he said, "and I have learned since October that a discussion I am not going to win is a discussion I should not begin."

We lay in the dark, he in the bed and I in the chair with a blanket, and it was ridiculous, and neither of us laughed.

"Mercy."

"Say it."

"The line at the foot of the petition." His voice was flat in the dark. "I wrote it out and I read it and I did not think. That is the fact. I have been an auditor for nine years and I have read ten thousand documents and I have never once read the words at the foot of a document as if they were a thing a person had to live in."

"No," I said. "You have not."

"And you have."

"Since I was seventeen."

The fire in the grate settled.

"I am not going to say I am sorry tonight," he said. "It would be a sentence and you would have to do something with it and it is four in the morning and you are in a chair."

"It is not four."

"It is going to be," he said, "because neither of us is asleep."

***

The twenty-fourth was Christmas Eve and the town was full of it, and I sold four pounds of currants and a bottle of brandy and twelve candles before noon.

Tarrant's clerk came at eleven with a note.

*Mrs. Rooke,*

*The marriage is fixed for the fifteenth of January, the day after the audit. Mr. Bedloe has the engrossment and will have it ready on the second. The boy knows and is content. I am not well and I have been told to say so by a woman who is not my wife, and I am telling you because you will find it out.*

*N. Tarrant.*

I read it twice and took it to Judith.

She was up. She had washed her hair and put it up and was sitting in the room with the blue cloth with the fourteen books in front of her and a pen in her hand, and she looked at me and said, "The fifteenth."

"You knew."

"His clerk came at eight." She put the pen down. "The fifteenth of January. He has moved it up a month and he has given a reason that is not the reason."

"What is the reason?"

"Because he is dying," Judith said. "He has been dying since the dinner and he wants it done while he can still sign his name, and it is the only thing in eleven years that he has ever wanted for himself." She picked up the pen again. "And because on the eighth of January the consistory sits, and whatever happens in that room he wants the settlement signed before it."

***

That night I could not sleep and I heard him turn over in the bed four times, which is what a man does when he is lying still on purpose and cannot keep it up.

"Rooke."

"Go on."

"Are you awake?"

"No," he said, "I have been asleep for an hour and I am answering you in my sleep."

I lay in the chair with the blanket up to my chin.

"There is a question," I said, "and I have been carrying it since October and I have not asked it of anybody, and I am going to ask it of you because you are the only person in England who will not be kind to me about it."

"Ask it."

"What do I want?"

The bed creaked.

"That is not a question," he said. "That is three questions and a trap."

"Then answer the trap."

"I am going to answer it badly and you are not going to like it." He was quiet for a moment. "You want to be asked."

I lay in the dark.

"Say the rest."

"You have spent two months in a town where every man who is not paying you is using you, and you have done the best work of your life, and you have said to me in four letters that you are well and that the room is cold, and you have not once written down what you wanted, and I have read every one of them looking for it." The bed creaked again. "That is what I think. You want somebody to ask you what you want, and you are never going to be able to answer, because the last person who asked you was your mother and you were eleven."

I lay very still.

"Go to sleep," I said.

"Mercy."

"Go to sleep."

He did not say anything else, and after a while his breathing went even, and I lay in a chair in a grocer's back room in a coal town on Christmas Eve and looked at the ceiling and thought: he is wrong. He is wrong and it is not that simple and I could tell him why in four sentences.

I did not tell him in four sentences. I lay there until the clock went two and then three, and I did not sleep, and in the morning I got up and made the tea and did not say anything about it, and neither did he.

***

Whitworth's man came at two.

He was a Hull man in a black coat and he had a letter and he would not give it to me, he would only give it to Miss Kell, and he stood in the shop and said, "I am instructed to wait."

"Then wait," I said, and took the letter up myself.

Judith read it standing at the window.

It was two pages. I did not see it. What I saw was her face, and her face did a thing at the second page that I had not seen it do before, which was nothing at all.

She put it down.

"He offers to withdraw the covenant," she said.

I sat down.

"Say that again."

"He offers to release the covenant, on terms." She turned round. "The terms are that the thirteen offices pass to his nominee and the beam is surrendered to him outright, not rested, not shut, surrendered, with a deed, and he will pay six thousand two hundred for it, and the covenant goes into the fire in front of witnesses."

I looked at her.

"That is the whole of the wharf," I said.

"That is the whole of the wharf and six thousand two hundred pounds and no lawsuit and no visitation and no scandal, and it is in writing and his man is downstairs waiting for an answer."

"And if you say no?"

"Then the covenant stands and he keeps the toll and the coal goes on being weighed at a hundred and twelve and nobody in this town ever finds out." She picked the letter up again. "He has given me until the second of January."

"Why the second?"

"Because that is the day the settlement is engrossed," Judith said, "and he knows it."

I sat quite still.

"Judith," I said, "there is a thing I have to say and I am going to say it once and then I am going to hold my tongue. It is not my wharf and it is not my three thousand eight hundred pounds and it is not my father in that room."

"Tell me."

"If you take his terms you will be safe and rich and you will be the woman who sold the beam for six thousand pounds, and there will be forty men on that wharf who know it, and you will see them every day for the rest of your life."

She stood at the window with the letter in her hand.

"And if I do not?"

"Then you will be the woman who stood up in the market hall on the fourteenth of January, and whatever happens after that, nobody in this town will ever be able to say you did not."

She looked out at the snow.

"Tell his man to come back on the second," she said, "and tell him that Miss Kell will answer him in writing on that day and not before."

***

On the twenty-fifth, at eight in the evening, she was at the beam house.

I know because I went down at nine to see the lock, having got into the way of it, and there was a light in the shed and the door was open a foot and the chain was on the hook.

She was inside with a lantern on the sill and the tarpaulin off the beam and her hands on the long arm, and she was not doing anything. She was standing there with her hands on the iron.

"Judith."

"Come in and shut the door, it is freezing."

I came in and shut the door.

"I have been here since six," she said. "I have not decided anything. I want that understood. I came down here to look at it and I have been looking at it for three hours and I have decided nothing."

"All right."

"But I have decided one thing." She turned round with the lantern behind her. "On the fourteenth of January, before the audit, I am going to have that beam taken down out of the loft and hung. And I am going to do it whether I have taken his terms or not, and whether I am married or not, and whether the covenant stands or falls."

I stood in the door.

"Why before the audit?"

"Because an audit is a man reading out figures," she said, "and I am not going to argue with a man reading out figures. I am going to hang a beam and put a hundredweight on it and let the town watch the scale."

## Twenty-Three · The Settlement

The engrossment was on the table at Bedloe's office on the second of January at ten in the morning and it was six skins.

I had never seen six skins of anything. I had seen a covenant of two and a mortgage of one and a deed of four, and I had thought four was a great deal of paper. Six skins of parchment laid out end to end on a table would go from the window to the door.

They were laid out, not stacked. Bedloe had had it done before we came in, because a man who has drawn four hundred settlements knows that the thing which convinces a woman is not the words, it is the length.

Tarrant was there. He had been brought in a chair by two men and he sat at the head of the table with a rug over his knees and his hands on the arms, and he had the ring on and he had had his coat brushed.

His nephew sat at the far end. Judith sat at the middle. I sat behind her and to the left, which is where a woman with a book is put, and Rooke stood at the window with his hat in his hand and did not sit down at all.

"I will read it," Bedloe said.

"You will not read the whole of it," Tarrant said. "She will be here till dinner."

"I will read the parts that are not in the ordinary form." Bedloe put on his spectacles, which I had never seen him do. "The ordinary parts are the ordinary parts and everybody in this room has seen them before except Mrs. Rooke, who will want to read them herself afterwards, and I will leave the skins with her for a day."

Then he read.

***

I am going to put down what was in it, because a person who has not sat in a room while six skins are read does not know what it is like, and because the whole of the rest of this book happens because of one clause on the fifth skin.

The first skin was the parties. Judith Kell, spinster, of Barrowfield, of the first part. Nicholas Tarrant the younger, of Barrowfield, attorney's clerk, of the second part. Nicholas Tarrant the elder, of Barrowfield, esquire, of the third part. And two trustees, of the fourth part, and the trustees were Bedloe himself and a man from the bank at Hull, and I did not know then why a trustee from a bank mattered and I found out in November of the following year.

The second skin was the recital. It recited the fourteen offices, and it recited the will of Amos Kell of 1820, and it recited the guardianship and the decree in Chancery of 1822, and it recited the covenant of the fourteenth of October 1821 in eleven lines, and the eleven lines were accurate.

The third skin was the property. Thirteen offices, by name, with their fees, and the total three hundred and twelve pounds four shillings, and the habendum and the tenendum and the words that put them into the hands of the trustees.

The fourth skin was the jointure. Four hundred pounds a year charged on the toll at the turn, and a house, and the furniture, and a power for the wife to dispose of her personal estate by will notwithstanding coverture, which Bedloe read out slowly and twice, and which is a thing that four women in a hundred had in 1832 and which I have since learned was put in at his own insistence and not at anybody's request.

The fifth skin was the beam.

"*And whereas the said office of the beam is of the nineteenth class and cannot be held by deputy, let, or devised, and is vested in the said Judith Kell in her own person, it is hereby declared and agreed that the said office shall be rested, and that the said Judith Kell shall not work the said beam, and that the said Nicholas Tarrant the younger shall not work the said beam, and that the said office shall be held without exercise in manner following, that is to say:*"

Bedloe stopped.

"Read the rest of it," Judith said.

"Miss Kell."

"Read the rest of it."

He read the rest of it, and the rest of it was four lines, and the four lines said that the office should be held without exercise and that the covenant of the fourteenth of October 1821 should be performed and observed by the holder.

I sat behind her with my hand flat on my knee.

"That is the covenant," I said.

The room went quiet, which is a thing that happens when a woman with a book speaks out of turn.

"Say that again, Mrs. Rooke," Bedloe said.

"That clause is not a settlement of the beam. It is the covenant, put into a marriage settlement, and made the husband's promise as well as hers." I stood up. "You have not rested the beam. You have put the covenant into a deed that two people sign and a trustee from a bank in Hull holds, and from the fifteenth of January there will be three parties bound by it instead of one."

Bedloe put his spectacles down.

"Yes," he said.

"Then it is not a settlement," I said. "It is an enforcement."

"It is both," Bedloe said, "and it is both because I drew it and I drew it as I was instructed, and I have been waiting for somebody in this room to read the fifth skin aloud and hear it, and I have been waiting since November."

Tarrant the elder moved in his chair.

"It is a provision for her protection," he said.

"It is a provision against her," I said. "It protects her from her own office."

"It protects her from a lawsuit she cannot afford," Tarrant said, "and from a man in Hull who would take that wharf off her in three years, and from the forty men on that wharf who would be out of work while the case was tried. You may call it what you like in your book, Mrs. Rooke. I have called it a provision for her protection for eleven years and I have believed it, and I am too ill this morning to be told I have been lying to myself."

***

Judith had not said anything.

She was sitting with her hands in her lap and her eyes on the fifth skin, and she had not moved since Bedloe began to read the clause, and I have seen a person go still in that particular way twice in my life and both times it was a person deciding something.

"Miss Kell," Bedloe said. "You may refuse to sign. It is not too late and it will not be too late until the fifteenth. If you refuse, there is no marriage and no settlement and the offices stay as they are, and I am obliged to tell you that if you refuse, your guardian's accounts will have to be passed in Chancery, which will cost money that is not in the estate."

"I am not going to refuse," Judith said.

The nephew looked up.

"I am going to sign it," she said, "and I am going to sign it on the fifteenth, and I am going to sign it in this office with all of you in the room, and then I am going to do the thing that this clause does not prevent me from doing, and I want everybody in this room to hear me say it so that nobody can be surprised on the fourteenth."

"Miss Kell," Tarrant said.

"You have not let me finish." She stood up. "The clause says I shall not work the beam. It does not say the beam shall not be hung. It does not say the office shall not be exercised by a man in my employment acting under my order and in my presence. It says I shall not work it, and I will not work it. I will stand in that shed with my hands at my sides and I will direct the man who does."

Bedloe looked at the fifth skin.

"That is a very fine distinction," he said.

"It is the distinction the covenant makes," Judith said. "The covenant says the beam shall not be used for the weighing of coal. It does not say a beam may not be hung and proved. A beam that is hung and proved and not used for trade is a beam that has been tested, and a holder who tests her beam once in eleven years is doing her duty under the office, and there is not a court in England that would say otherwise, because if there were, no beam in the kingdom could ever be proved and every weight in the country would be a guess."

The room was quiet.

Tarrant the younger was looking at her with an expression I could not read.

"Where did you get that?" he said.

"From the covenant," Judith said. "I have read it four hundred times, Mr. Tarrant. You have read it once."

***

Then Rooke spoke from the window.

He had not said a word for an hour and a quarter and everybody in the room had forgotten he was there, which is a thing he does and which I have stopped trying to explain to people.

"Mr. Bedloe," he said. "The fifth skin."

"Mr. Rooke."

"Read the recital of the covenant again. The eleven lines."

Bedloe looked at him over his spectacles.

"Mr. Rooke, we have had the recital."

"Read it again."

Bedloe read it again.

"Now the four lines at the end of the fifth skin," Rooke said, "the ones that say the covenant shall be performed and observed by the holder."

Bedloe read them.

"Where is Whitworth's name in this deed?"

The room was quiet.

"Nowhere," Bedloe said.

"Nowhere," Rooke agreed. "There is a recital of a covenant and there is an obligation to perform it, and the covenantee, the man in whose favour it was made, is not a party to this deed and has not consented and his name does not appear. That is not a settlement of the covenant. That is a promise between two people to do a thing for the benefit of a third who is not in the room, and a third who is not in the room cannot enforce it, and a third who cannot enforce it is a third who has been given nothing." He came away from the window. "And there is a second thing. A covenant to do a thing which is contrary to the duty of an office may be good between the parties and void as against the office, and a court will not grant an injunction to restrain the holder of a public office from doing her duty at the suit of a man who is not a party to the deed she signed."

Nobody said anything for a while.

Two things had been done in that room and I have had to keep them apart ever since, and I mean to put the difference down here because a great many people in Barrowfield never managed it. Miss Kell had found a way to hang a beam and prove it on the fourteenth without breaking one word of the fifth skin. Mr. Rooke had said, in the same hour, that the fifth skin was not worth the parchment it was written on. The first was for the fourteenth of January. The second was for every January after it. The first she did with her own hands at her sides in front of a street full of people. The second nobody did at all; it simply stopped being true, the way a thing stops being true when a man says it out loud in a room with a clerk in it.

"Mr. Rooke," Bedloe said slowly, "are you telling this room that the fifth skin is worth nothing?"

"I am telling this room that the fifth skin is worth exactly what it was worth before it was written, which is a promise by a woman to a man who is not in the room, and that if the man in the room in Hull wants to enforce it he will have to bring his own action on his own covenant, and that his own covenant is a covenant to shut an office, and that a covenant to shut a public office for ninety-nine years is a thing I would very much like to see argued in front of a judge."

Tarrant the elder sat forward in his chair.

"Then why," he said, "has it been in every draft since November?"

"Because it has never been argued," Rooke said, "and because a clause that has never been argued is a clause that frightens people, and because it has frightened three generations of this family and it frightened me for a week in October until I read the recital properly." He looked at the old man. "Mr. Tarrant, I am sorry. I know what it cost you to have that clause put in. I think you did it because you believed it would keep her out of a court, and I think you were wrong, and I think you have been wrong about it since 1821, and I have not the least doubt that you meant it kindly."

Tarrant sat with his hands on the arms of the chair.

"It has kept her out of a court for eleven years," he said.

"It has kept her out of a court because she did not go into one," Rooke said. "Those are not the same thing."

***

The nephew came out after us into the row.

"Mrs. Rooke."

"Mr. Tarrant."

"May I walk down with you."

We walked down.

He had his hands behind his back and he did not say anything until we were past the saddler's.

"I did not know about the fifth skin," he said.

"You signed nothing."

"I read the draft in November. I read the parts about the property and the jointure and I did not read the fifth skin, because I have been in that office seven years and I have read a thousand skins and I have got into the way of reading the parts that are my business." He stopped in the street. "She read it. She has been in that house with fourteen books since she was ten and she read the whole of it and found the one clause that mattered in a morning."

"Yes."

"That is the thing I cannot get past," he said. "I am not a fool and I have worked hard and I know the fourteen offices better than she does and I did not read the fifth skin."

I stood in the cold with my hands in my muff.

"Mr. Tarrant," I said, "may I ask you a question."

"Ask it."

"Do you want to marry her?"

He looked down the street.

"I have been asked that four times now," he said, "and by four people who all thought they were the first. My uncle asked me. Mr. Bedloe asked me. She asked me herself in October, in that room, with the door open, and I gave her an answer about the wharf and she looked at me the way she looks at a man she has weighed." He turned. "And now you."

"And what is the answer?"

"The answer is that I want to be the man who does not become my uncle," he said. "That is not the same as wanting her, and I know it is not, and I have got to the age where I would rather have an honest reason than a flattering one."

I looked at him.

"Then stand next to her on the fourteenth," I said.

He looked at me.

"Why?"

"Because on the fourteenth of January she is going to do a thing in a public hall that will cost her something, and there will be forty men in that room who have known her since she was ten, and not one of them will be standing next to her." I put my hands deeper into the muff. "I have been married nine months, and the only thing I have learned is that it is not a feeling. It is standing next to a person when it costs you something. Rooke has done it twice and I did not ask him either time."

He stood in the street for a moment.

Then he said, "I will be there," and took his hat off, and went back up the row.

***

***

Whitworth's man came at three and Judith gave him the answer in the shop with the door open.

It was one page and she had written it herself that morning and she would not let me see it until it was sealed.

"Read it to me," I said, when he had gone.

She read it.

*Sir,*

*I am obliged by your offer and I decline it.*

*The thirteen offices are not mine to surrender. They are held of the manor and they are charged with duties to this parish, and I have no more right to sell the town's weights than I have to sell the town's water.*

*The beam I will not surrender, and I will tell you why, and it is not a matter of sentiment. A beam surrendered is a beam in the hands of a man who has an interest in it reading heavy. A beam rested is a beam in the hands of a woman who has an interest in it reading true. There is no third arrangement, and the difference between the two is the whole of what has happened in this town since 1821.*

*I am to be married on the fifteenth of January. After that date all letters to me should be addressed to Mrs. Tarrant and sent to this house, and I will answer them.*

*I am, sir, your obedient servant,*

*Judith Kell.*

I sat down on the bench by the window.

"You have written to a man in Hull," I said, "in the plainest English I have ever seen from anybody in this town, that he is a thief in four sentences and a lawyer's word."

"I have written that a man who holds a beam has an interest," Judith said. "It is not the same thing."

"It is exactly the same thing and you know it."

She folded her copy and put it in the press.

"Yes," she said. "It is."

## Twenty-Four · The Twelve Days

He stayed twelve days and I have put down what happened in them in the order it happened, because I have thought since that the twelve days were the whole of my marriage in a small room and I did not know it at the time.

The twenty-sixth. He came down to the shop at six and asked what he could do and I gave him the ledger and he had it in order by nine and found four shillings of error in it that had been there since October, and Mrs. Sowerby said, "Well," in a tone that had a great deal in it.

He was bad at the counter. That is worth putting down. A man who has spent nine years in an office with a clerk and a stool does not know how to hand a woman a pound of sugar and take her penny, and he was too tall for the shop and he kept knocking the bacon, and by noon he had retreated to the ledger and stayed there.

The twenty-seventh. He went down to the wharf by himself.

He was there four hours. He came back with his boots black and his hands black and a drawing on the back of a bill of lading, and he put it on the table and said, "It is not the poise."

"Say that again."

"It is not the poise and it is not the pivot and it is not the plate. I have been over the whole of it with a straight edge and a pair of calipers that I borrowed from the smith, and the arms are true and the pivot is true and the poise is a piece of cast iron with a hole bored in it and the hole is bored to a weight and the weight is right." He put his finger on the drawing. "It is the fulcrum."

"Say it in English."

"The fulcrum is the point the whole thing turns on. On a true beam the fulcrum is set so that the two arms are in a fixed proportion, and the proportion is stamped on the beam, and the scale is cut to the proportion." He turned the drawing round. "On that beam the fulcrum has been moved. Not much. An eighth of an inch. And an eighth of an inch at the fulcrum will put a seventh part on every reading at the far end, and it will do it evenly, on every weight, so that a man testing it with a fifty-six pound weight at the short end will find it true and a man weighing a hundredweight of coal will find it heavy."

I sat down at the table.

"An eighth of an inch."

"An eighth of an inch, and the bracket that holds the fulcrum has been off and put back, and the bolts are new, and the new bolts are in the old holes with a sliver of iron packed in to take up the slack." He looked at me. "That is not a thing a man does with a file in a shed in a night. That is a thing a man does in a workshop with a drill and a tap, and it takes a day, and it wants the beam taken down and put on a bench."

I sat with the drawing.

"Then it was done before it was hung," I said.

"It was done before it was hung," he said. "In 1809, in a workshop, in Rotherham or Sheffield or Leeds, by a man who was paid for it, and then it was brought up here in a cart and hung in that shed with a brass plate on it and a scale cut to the wrong proportion, and the scale was cut to the wrong proportion on purpose, and the man who cut it knew what he was doing."

The twenty-eighth. He went to the almshouse and sat with Daniel Hogg for an afternoon.

I do not know what they said to each other. Neither of them told me. What I know is that he came back at five with his hands clean and his face different, and that at supper he said, "That man has been sitting in a room for eleven years being grateful to the man who ruined him," and then did not say anything else for an hour.

That evening Judith came to the shop with a book of the toll accounts under her arm, and I made the introductions, and the two of them stood on either side of the counter and looked at each other.

"Mrs. Rooke has written to me about you," Judith said.

"Mrs. Rooke writes to me about you," Rooke said. "I have had thirty-one letters since October and you are in twenty-eight of them."

"Which three are the others?"

"The ones about the roof."

Judith laughed. It was the first time I had heard her laugh since the reading, and it was not a big laugh, but it was hers.

Then she put the book on the counter.

"Mr. Rooke," she said, "you have been an auditor. I want to ask you a thing and I want the answer you would give a man, not the answer you would give a woman."

"Ask it."

"On the fourteenth, in the market hall, they will read out the accounts of the fourteen offices, and I will stand up and give mine. I have nothing to hide in mine. The toll is paid, the crane is let, the market is farmed, the pump is mended, the almshouse has two in it and no accounts at all." She put her hand flat on the book. "What do I say about the beam?"

Rooke thought about it for a while.

"Nothing," he said.

She looked at him.

"Nothing?"

"You have no accounts for the beam because the beam has not been worked, and an office that has not been worked has no account to give, and the moment you stand up and offer an explanation you have made the beam a subject." He came round the counter. "You are not there to accuse anybody. You are there to give your account and to do one thing. What is the one thing?"

"To hang the beam."

"To hang the beam," he said. "Then do that and let the iron talk, and when they ask you why, say the four words you have been saving, and sit down."

Judith stood with her hand on the book.

"Mrs. Rooke said you would say that."

"Mrs. Rooke has been in this county two months and knows more about a public room than I do after nine years of them," Rooke said. "There is one thing I will add. Take a witness who is not your friend."

"Who?"

"Mr. Bedloe," Rooke said. "He has drawn every paper in this town for thirty-one years and he is the only man in Barrowfield whose word would be taken against Tarrant's, and he is ashamed of something, and a man who is ashamed will stand up." He went back to the table. "Do not ask him tonight. Ask him on the twelfth, when he has had time to be afraid and got over it."

Judith looked at him for a moment.

"Thank you," she said.

"Miss Kell."

"I am to be Mrs. Tarrant on the fifteenth."

"Then I will say it now," Rooke said. "If you are marrying him because the settlement is drawn, do not. If you are marrying him because you want him in the room, do. I have been at this table four days and I cannot tell which of those you are doing."

Judith stood with the book in her arms.

"You are the second person in this town to ask me that," she said.

"Who was the first?"

"Mrs. Rooke. In October. In that room, with the door open." She went to the door. "You are both of you very tiring and I have not decided yet whether I am grateful."

The twenty-ninth. Tarrant the elder had a second seizure and was not expected to live the night.

We were sent for at nine in the evening. The house on the market cross was full of women and a surgeon and the nephew, and the parson came at ten, and the old man was in his bed with his eyes shut and his breath going like a clock with something wrong in it.

He was not dying. He came out of it at two in the morning and asked for tea and abused the surgeon, and I went home at three with Rooke and we walked up the empty street in the cold and neither of us said anything.

At the door he said, "He will be at that audit if he has to be carried."

"Yes."

"He has done it wrong for eleven years and done it properly, every year, in a good hand," Rooke said. "Next month he will do it right, and he will do it badly, because he has never done it before."

The thirtieth. The night I am going to put down.

We had the room and the chair and the bed and the arrangement, and at eleven I got up out of the chair and went and stood by the bed.

He was awake. He had been awake for a while; I could tell by his breathing.

"Rooke."

"Go on."

"I am tired," I said, "and I have been tired since October, and I do not want to be in a chair tonight."

He moved over.

I lay down on the bed with my back to him and he put his arm along the bed and did not touch me, and we lay there in the dark for a while, and then I turned round.

"Mercy."

"Say it."

"This is the part where I am supposed to ask you a question," he said, "and I am not going to ask it, because I have been in this town six days and I know what the question is and I know you will not answer it and I know that if I ask it you will do the thing you do, which is to give me a version."

I lay in the dark with my forehead against his shoulder.

"What version?"

"The one where you are well and the room is cold and the beam is interesting." His hand was on the bed and not on me. "You have given me that version in six letters and you gave it to me in the shop on the first night and you gave it to me on Christmas Eve, and I have taken it every time, and I am not going to take it tonight."

"Then what do you want?"

"I want to be in a room with you where you are not being useful."

I lay there for a while.

"I do not know how to do that," I said.

"I know," he said. "That is why I am not going to ask."

And then he did the thing that I have not forgiven him for and am not going to forgive him for, which is that he put his hand flat on my back, the way he did at Thorneley, and left it there, and did not move it, and after a while he took it away and turned over and went to sleep, actually went to sleep, breathing even, in a bed with his wife in it.

I lay awake until four.

I want to be exact about what I felt, because it would be easy to write it as a warm thing and it was not a warm thing.

I felt dismissed.

That is the truth. He had said a true thing about me in the dark and then he had put his hand on my back and then he had gone to sleep, and what I heard in the dark was a man closing a book he had decided not to read that night.

There is a second thing under that one and I mean to write it down, because it is the worse of the two.

I was relieved.

If he had asked me the question there in the dark I should have had to answer it, and there was nothing in me at eleven o'clock on the thirtieth of December that was ready to be looked at. A woman who is asked a question has to be a person. A woman who is being useful does not. I had been a woman being useful since I was eleven years old and it had never once failed me; it had brought me a hundred and eleven miles and a day book and a ledger full of other people's weights, and there was no room in any of it for a man to lie beside me and want the woman and not the version.

So I lay in the dark and was dismissed and was relieved, and was angry with him for giving me the thing I had asked for without asking.

At four I got up and went down and made up the fire and sat in the shop with the ledger, and I did the thing I always do, which is to work.

The thirty-first. The town was up till midnight and there was a bell and a bonfire in the market and I sold a great deal of everything and Mrs. Sowerby got drunk on gin and honey and told me the whole of her husband's life in nine minutes and cried and went to bed.

Rooke stood in the door of the shop and watched the bonfire with his hands in his pockets.

"The year I have had," he said.

"Say it."

"January I was a man with a house and a paper and a name in a county." He was looking at the fire. "December I am a man with a house and a wife in a coal town and no office and a suit of clothes that is going at the elbows, and I have never been happier in my life and I cannot say so to my wife because she is in a coal town being useful to a girl with a lawsuit."

I stood beside him in the door.

"You could say it," I said.

"I could," he said. "I am not going to, tonight, because you would have to answer it and you have nothing left to answer with."

I put my hand through his arm, which is a thing I had not done in Barrowfield in daylight, and he looked down at it and then put his own hand over it and left it there, and we stood in the door of a grocer's shop and watched a bonfire in a market square with our hands like that until Mrs. Sowerby came out, said something coarse about the pair of us, and went in again for her shawl.

The second of January. The settlement. It is in another place and I have put it there.

The third. He went at eight in the morning.

The carrier came to the market at half past seven and he stood in the shop with his bag and his coat and his hat and the whole of it was arranged and there was nothing to arrange.

"Six weeks," he said. "I will be back on the fourteenth if I can and the fifteenth if I cannot."

"You will be back on the fourteenth."

"I will be back on the fourteenth."

He stood at the door with his hand on it.

"Mercy."

"Say it."

"There is a thing in this town that you have not told me," he said, "and I have known it since Christmas and I am not going to ask, and I want you to know that I know, because if I go away and say nothing at all you will spend six weeks thinking I did not notice."

I stood behind the counter.

"Say what it is," I said.

"No," he said. "You have had two months of people deciding what you may be told and what you may not. I am not going to be the third."

He opened the door and the cold came in.

"I am not asking," he said, "because I have decided that a woman who has been told what to do by every man in this county since she was seventeen is entitled to one thing that is hers and not mine, and if it is a thing you cannot say, then I am not going to make you say it."

"And if I never say it?"

"Then I will never know it," he said, "and I will have a wife with a room in her that I am not allowed into, and I will take it, because the alternative is a wife with no room at all."

Then he went out and got on the carrier, and the carrier went up the street and turned at the market cross, and I stood in the door of a grocer's shop with my hand on the frame until the cart was out of sight.

I want to put down here, at the end of the twelve days, that he was right and that I hated him for being right, and that I stood in that door and thought: he has given me a room of my own and I do not want a room of my own, I want to be asked, and I have spent two months telling myself I did not want to be asked because a woman who wants to be asked is a woman with nothing to do.

Then I went in and sold four pounds of flour and a quarter of tea and got out the day book, because there were nine days to the fourteenth and there was a beam in a loft.

## Twenty-Five · The Letter She Did Not Send

On the fourth of January I sat down to write a petition to the consistory court in my own name and it took me three days.

I want to put down how it went, because I have written four hundred letters for other people and I have never once found it hard, and this was the hardest thing I have done with a pen.

The fourth. I wrote eleven lines asking for a visitation of the parish of Barrowfield, and I set out the beam and the fulcrum and the eighth of an inch and the twenty-two years during which the coal of that town had been weighed on a scale that was cut to a false proportion, and I did it in the plain words, and I did not put in one adjective.

Then I read it and I saw that it was a good petition and that it was exactly the petition a woman would write who had been in that town two months and had measured a beam with a pair of calipers.

I crossed it out.

The fifth. I wrote it again and this time I put in the numbers: five thousand two hundred and twenty-five tons over eleven years, and three thousand and forty-seven pounds, and the fourteen pounds on every hundredweight, and the ninety-eight.

I read it and I saw that it was a better petition and that a bishop's officer reading it would sit up in his chair.

And then I saw the other thing, which was that every word of it was mine, and that none of it was hers, and that if I sent it I would be sending Judith Kell's case to York in my handwriting with my name at the foot of it, and that she had never asked me to.

I crossed it out.

The sixth. I did not write anything. I went down to the wharf and stood at the corner of the warehouse and counted baskets for an hour.

Twenty-nine baskets. One of them was written as ten and was twelve.

The seventh. I wrote it a third time and this time I wrote it as a letter to the bishop and not as a petition, and I told him the truth, which was that I was a woman who had been in a town for two months and had found a thing, and that the thing belonged to a girl of twenty-one who was to be married on the fifteenth, and that I did not know whether the girl wanted it told.

I read that one and it was honest and it was useless, because a bishop's officer cannot act on a letter from a woman who does not know whether the person concerned wants it told.

I crossed it out and I sat with my head in my hands and understood, at last, what I had been doing.

***

I had been doing what he did.

That is the whole of it. I sat in a room over a grocer's shop with three crossed-out petitions in front of me and understood that every one of them was a hand laid on the back of somebody else's hand. They were better than his. Mine had the calipers and the numbers and the loft and the two women in the almshouse and I had done the work myself and it was good work.

And none of it was mine to send.

I had come a hundred and eleven miles to answer a question for a woman who had asked me. She had asked me in May and she had asked me again in October at the beam house with her hand on the door, and I had answered her, and the answering was the whole of my business in that town. Everything after that, the beam and the fulcrum and the three thousand eight hundred pounds, was a thing I had found on the way, and finding a thing is not the same as being asked to do something about it.

And the difference between me and the man who had signed my name at the foot of a petition was that he had not seen it, and I had.

***

The eighth. The consistory sat at York and I did not go.

Bedloe went. He had business there anyway and he went, and he came back on the ninth in the evening and came to the shop with his hat in his hand and would not sit down.

"The visitation is granted," he said.

I sat down on the bench.

"Say it again."

"The officer read the petition and asked three questions and granted a visitation of the parish of Barrowfield, to be held in March, and the commission will be issued in February." He turned his hat round. "It was not opposed. The parson had written to say he had nothing to hide, which is the worst thing a man can write to a consistory court, and the registrar read it out and the officer said, 'Then he will not mind showing us the books.'"

"And Judith?"

"Miss Kell is not a party and does not have to be. She will be a witness if she chooses." Bedloe looked at me. "Mrs. Rooke, there is a thing I have to tell you and it is not a pleasant thing. Your husband's petition has done what no petition of yours could have done, and it has done it because it was signed by a man and a man's signature is a thing a court will act on, and there is no way round that and I am not going to pretend there is."

"I know."

"I thought you did." He put his hat on. "There is a second thing. The officer asked after the petitioner and I said she was in Barrowfield and he said, 'Then she may appear in March if she wishes,' and I said I would tell her."

"Thank you."

"Mrs. Rooke." He stopped at the door. "There is a third thing and I will say it badly. I have been the man who draws the papers in this town for thirty-one years and I have never in that time stood up in a public room and said a thing against a client. On the fourteenth I am going to be in the market hall because Miss Kell has asked me to be there, and I am going to be asked a question, and I am going to answer it, and after that day I will not draw another paper in Barrowfield."

I stood up.

"Mr. Bedloe."

"Do not," he said. "I have had a week to get used to it and I have got used to it, and if you are kind to me about it I will not be able to do it."

***

Judith came at eight that night and I told her about the visitation in the back room with the door shut.

She sat with her hands in her lap and did not say anything for a while.

"March," she said.

"March. The commission issues in February and the officer comes in March, and he will sit in the vestry with the parson and the churchwardens and he will ask for the accounts of the almshouse, and there are none."

"And the beam?"

"The beam is not a charity and the visitation does not touch it. But a visitation has a clerk with a book and any man with a grievance and a shilling can stand up in that room and say a thing under oath."

She sat with that.

"Then it will come out in March," she said.

"It may."

"And by March I will have been married two months and the settlement will be engrossed and the fifth skin will be signed."

"Yes."

She looked at me.

"You have given me a thing I did not ask for," she said, "and it is a good thing and it may save this town, and I cannot use it."

I sat down opposite her.

"Say why."

"Because a visitation in March is a visitation after the wedding, and the wedding is the thing I have decided to do. I am not going to walk into that church with a bishop's officer behind me." She said it evenly. "Mrs. Rooke, I have had two months of people doing things for me. My father did a thing for me in 1821 and it killed him. Mr. Tarrant has done things for me for eleven years and it has made him ill. Your husband has done a thing for me from a hundred and eleven miles away and I have never met him. Now you have done a thing for me and I did not ask for it and it is the best of the four."

"You did ask me," I said. "In May."

"I asked you a question," she said. "I did not ask you to save the town."

I sat with that, because it was true.

"Then what do you want me to do?" I said.

"Nothing," Judith said. "That is what I have come to tell you. On the fourteenth I am going to do the thing I decided to do in that shed on Christmas night, and I am going to do it whether the visitation comes or not, and I am not going to be saved by a paper from York and I am not going to be saved by a man from Collow and I am not going to be saved by you." She stood up. "I am going to do it myself. If it goes wrong I will be the woman who tried it, and that will be the whole of me in this town for the rest of my life, and I have thought about that, and I am going to do it anyway."

***

The eleventh, the twelfth and the thirteenth were the longest three days I have spent in my life.

There was no letter.

I want to put down what those three days were like, because in the writing of a life a person is tempted to leave out the waiting, and the waiting is most of it.

I did eleven things in three days and I could give the list.

I cleaned the shop shelves and took the tobacco out and wiped the tins and put it back. I wrote to Mrs. Trewin about the roof and told her to send the mason's bill to me and not to Rooke. I walked down to the wharf twice and stood at the corner of the warehouse and did not go into the shed. I counted the day book twice, the whole of it since October, and found nothing wrong with it, and counted it a third time to be sure that I had found nothing wrong with it. I mended the hem of my second-best petticoat. I read the whole of the quarter's *York Courant*, including the corn prices and the deaths. I wrote out the fourteen offices and their fees on a clean sheet and checked them against the folio, and they were right, and I had known that they were right before I began.

On the night of the thirteenth I sat in the back room with the lamp and understood that I had spent three days being useful in an empty shop.

The post came at half past ten. I was at the door of the shop at twenty past on all three days, and on the eleventh the postmaster came up the street with two letters for the parson and one for the saddler and nothing for me, and on the twelfth there was a packet for Bedloe and a bill for Mrs. Sowerby and nothing for me, and on the thirteenth there was nothing at all because the mail had been held at Thorneley by snow.

I did the shop. I sold flour and tea and candles and a great deal of salt. I wrote two letters for other people, one to a son in Manchester and one to a daughter in Leeds, and I wrote them well.

On the twelfth night I got out the box and read his letters. All of them, from the first one in November to the last, and there were nine, and I read them in order on the bed with the candle and then I put them back and lay in the dark and thought: he has not written because he is coming.

On the thirteenth night I thought: he has not written because he is not coming.

I did not sleep either night.

***

The fourteenth of January was a Monday and it was cold and clear and there was no snow.

I was up at five. I had laid out my good gown the night before and I put it on in the dark and did my hair by the glass with the candle, and then I went down and lit the stove and made the tea and sat in the back room with the day book and read the whole of it, from the first page.

Then I put it in my pocket and put my coat on and went out.

The market hall was at the top of the street with the corn market under it and the upper floor on stone columns and a stair at each end, and by seven there were men on the stair.

By half past seven there were forty people in that hall and by eight there were a hundred and twenty.

***

On the tenth I sat down and wrote to Rooke and it was four lines.

*I have been three days writing a petition and I have burned it.*

*I have understood something and it is not comfortable and I will not explain it in a letter. It is this: I have been doing what you did and I did not see it until it was in my own hand.*

*I have nothing to send you but that, and it is not a kindness and it is not an apology, it is the only true thing I have written to you since October.*

*Come on the fourteenth. I am asking.*

Then I stopped and looked at the last line for a long time.

I had never asked him for anything. That is a thing I have had to face in the writing of this, and it is true. In nine months of marriage I had asked him for a floor to be mended and a letter to be posted and a sum to be checked, and I had never once asked him for a thing that he could refuse.

*I am asking.*

I put the pen down and got up and walked round the room and sat down again.

There is a thing nobody tells you about asking. It is not the asking that is hard. It is the four seconds after the pen comes off the paper, when the thing is on the page and you have not yet sealed it, and you understand that a person who asks can be refused and a person who does not ask cannot.

I sealed it.

***

I took it to the post office myself at four in the afternoon and the postmaster weighed it and said, "One penny," and I paid him and he looked at the superscription and said, "Collow."

"Collow."

"That is a long way."

"It is a hundred and eleven miles," I said.

"It is more than that by road."

"It is a hundred and eighteen by road, and he knows it, and he will be at that market hall on the fourteenth if the roads hold."

The postmaster looked at me over his spectacles and then he put the letter in the bag.

I went out into the street and stood on the step.

It was a Thursday and the market was thin and there was a woman selling turnips and a man with a cart of firewood and the light was going at half past four and the whole of the town was the colour of a wet slate.

I stood on that step and counted to forty.

I do not know why forty. It is not a number I have ever used for anything. I stood there with my hands in my muff and counted to forty and at thirty-eight a man came round the corner of the row and my heart went, and it was the saddler, and at forty I stopped counting and went in.

There was no sense in it. A letter that is in the postmaster's bag is in the bag, and I knew that as well as I know anything. But a woman who has just asked for something will stand on a step for four minutes looking up the street for the answer to come round the corner of the row, and I have done it since, and I expect I shall do it again.

Then I sat down in the back room with the day book and wrote, at the top of a clean page:

*He will not answer before the eleventh. The post from Collow takes four days if the roads are good. I have asked and I must wait like everybody else.*

And under it, because I am what I am:

*I have asked. It is done. Whatever happens on the fourteenth, I have asked him for something and I have not taken it back.*

## Twenty-Six · The Fourteenth of January

The market hall is a long room over the corn market with a ceiling of beams and four windows and a floor of boards that go down in the middle, and on the fourteenth of January it was full.

I have been in courtrooms and I have been in a bishop's consistory and I have stood in a room in Collow while eleven men decided whether a dead woman had been married, and I have never been in a room like that one.

It was not a court. That is the thing to understand. There was no judge and no bench and no dock. There was a long table at the end with the parish books on it and a man behind the table with a pen, and there were a hundred and twenty people standing on the boards with their hats in their hands, and there were the fourteen office-holders, and the whole of it was a town doing its accounts in public because it had always done its accounts in public and because a town that does its accounts in public is a town where nobody can put a hand in the till.

They had done it in that room since 1760. There was a board on the wall with the years painted on it and the names of the stewards, and the last name on it was Tarrant.

***

I got in at a quarter to eight and stood at the back on the left, by the window, and I was not the only woman there. There were twenty women: the shopkeepers' wives, the parson's wife, Mrs. Ellerbeck, two of the Rennards, a woman from the almshouse who had been brought and did not know what year it was.

Judith was at the front on the right, alone, with the fourteen books of the offices stacked on the floor beside her chair in two piles of seven.

Bedloe was at the table. He had a seat because he was the clerk of the manor and the steward's attorney and he had been at that table for thirty-one years.

The nephew was at the table too, in his uncle's place, with his uncle's folio in front of him and his uncle's pen, and he looked about nineteen.

Whitworth's man stood at the far end by the door with his hat in his hand and his coat buttoned and did not sit down.

And at the door, on the left, with his back against the frame and his hat under his arm, there was a man in a coat that was going at the elbows.

I saw him at three minutes to eight.

He looked up at the same moment I looked across, and he did not smile and he did not move and he did not come over, and I stood at the back of a market hall with a hundred and twenty people in it and my hands went cold and then hot and then steady.

***

The audit began at eight.

It began with the constable reading out the names of the fourteen offices and the fourteen holders, and each holder stood up and gave his account, and the clerk wrote it in the book, and the steward asked one question or none.

The market. Forty-one pounds, farmed to Mr. Whitworth's agent for thirty-eight and ten. Question: why is the farm ten shillings down? Answer: the stall rents are down, there are four stalls empty since September.

The crane. Twenty-two pounds. No question.

The bridge. Nineteen pounds. Question: is the bridge repaired? Answer: it is not. Question: why not? Answer: there is no money in the office for two hundred pounds of stone.

The pump. Fourteen pounds. The nephew stood up for this one because the office is in his uncle's gift and the holder is seventy-one and could not come. Eleven pounds four spent on the repair. Question: by whom? Answer: by me. Question: out of which fund? Answer: out of my own pocket and there is no fund.
