There was a noise in the room at that, not a big one, the kind of noise a hundred and twenty people make when a thing has been said that they will talk about in the street.

The shambles. The coal back. The measure. The ferry. The lamps. The scavenger. The bell. The almshouse. The toll at the turn.

The measure took four minutes. It is eight pounds a year and the holder is Judith Kell and she stood up and said, "The office of the measure is eight pounds and the standard weights are in my house in a case and I have taken the eight pounds every Michaelmas for eleven years and I have not once done the thing the eight pounds is for."

The room went quiet.

"Miss Kell," the nephew said, "that is not a question the audit asks."

"No," Judith said. "It is a statement I am making to the audit, and the audit may write it down."

The clerk wrote it down.

The almshouse was one pound six and there were two in it and there were no accounts. The clerk wrote that down too, and I saw Bedloe look up at the ceiling and then down at his hands.

The toll at the turn was seventy-five pounds and it was farmed and it was paid and there was no question.

***

Then the audit was done, and the clerk blotted the book, and the nephew closed the folio, and every man in that room began to put his hat on.

"Mr. Tarrant."

Judith was standing.

The nephew looked up.

"The audit is closed, Miss Kell."

"The audit of the fourteen offices is closed," Judith said, "and I have given my account of thirteen of them, and I have not given my account of the fourteenth, and I am the holder of the fourteenth, and I am going to give it now."

Nobody put a hat on.

"The office of the beam is nine pounds twelve a year," Judith said, "and it has not been worked since the nineteenth of December 1821, and it has no account because an office that has not been worked has no account. That is my account, and I would like to sit down."

She did not sit down.

"But there is a thing that this town has been doing for eleven years which is not in any book, and it is this. Every ton of coal that has gone over that wharf since 1821 has been weighed on a beam in the yard, and that beam does not read true, and it has not read true since the day it was hung, and I have had it measured with a straight edge and a pair of calipers and the fulcrum has been moved an eighth of an inch and the scale has been cut to the wrong proportion, and every hundredweight of coal that this town has bought since 1821 has been fourteen pounds short."

The room made a noise.

"Miss Kell," the nephew said, and he had gone white. "This is not the place."

"It is the only place," Judith said. "It is the place where this town does its accounts. I have been told for eleven years that the accounts are the business of the men who keep them. I am the holder of an office and I am giving my account and I am telling this town that the beam in the yard is false."

"On whose measurement?"

"On mine, and on Mrs. Rooke's, and on the measurement of a brass case of standard weights with the county stamp on them that has been in my house since 1711 and that I have not opened since I was ten years old."

She turned and looked at the nephew.

"And I am going to prove it," she said. "Now. In this room. Or in the yard, if the hall is too small."

"You cannot," the nephew said. "The beam in the yard is the only beam that can be hung."

"There is another one."

The room went absolutely quiet.

"There is a beam in the loft over the beam house," Judith said, "and it was made in Rotherham in 1711 and it has a brass plate on the arm and a scale cut in it that is true, and it was taken down in 1809 when the new one came and it was carried up a ladder and laid on two trestles and it has been up there for twenty-three years."

"That is a rumour," the nephew said.

"That is a beam," Judith said, "and I have been up the ladder and put my hands on it."

***

Then the man from Hull spoke.

He had been standing at the far end by the door for the whole of the audit with his hat in his hand and had said nothing, and when he spoke he did not raise his voice and the room had to go quiet to hear him, which is a trick and I have seen it done by better men than him.

"Miss Kell." He came forward two steps. "I am Mr. Whitworth's agent and I am here on his behalf and I have no vote in this room and no office and no standing except as a man with a principal. I want to say three things and I want them minuted, because I am going to be asked about this morning in Hull on Wednesday."

"Say them," the nephew said.

"The first is that what has been said in this room in the last ten minutes is spoken in a public place of a named firm and a named gentleman, and my principal will take advice on it."

Bedloe did not look up from the book.

"It is minuted," he said.

"The second is that the covenant of the fourteenth of October 1821 is a deed under hand and seal, and the holder of the office of the beam is bound by it, and no order of a steward of a manor can set aside a covenant."

"The covenant is with your principal," Bedloe said, "and your principal is not a party to the settlement, and if he wishes the covenant performed he may bring his action. It is minuted."

"The third," the man from Hull said, "is that this town takes four thousand tons of coal a year through that wharf and three thousand of them are Whitworth coal, and if the beam of this town is to be the subject of a public quarrel, then my principal will consider whether his coal is better weighed at Goole."

There was a silence in the room of a particular kind.

Then somebody at the back said, "Let it go to Goole."

It was not a shout. It was a man's voice saying a thing in a room, and it was Daniel Hogg's brother-in-law, a man I had never spoken to, and after he said it two other men said it, and then the room was saying it, not loudly, not in a chant, just forty men saying a plain sentence to a man in a black coat, and the man in the black coat stood there with his hat in his hand and understood that he had made an error.

"I will report what has been said," he said.

"You will report it," Bedloe said, without looking up. "It is all minuted."

***

Then Tarrant the elder came in.

He came in on two men and a stick, and he was in his coat and his hat and he had had his face shaved, and the whole room turned round and the whole room went quiet in a different way, because a hundred and twenty people in a market hall will make a noise at a scandal and no noise at all at a dying man.

He got as far as the table and stood there with his hand on it.

"Miss Kell," he said.

"Mr. Tarrant."

"You are going to hang that beam."

"I am going to prove it," Judith said. "And then I am going to hang it, because a beam that has been proved and left in a loft is a beam that will be doubted in a month."

Tarrant stood at the table with his hand on it.

"If you hang that beam," he said, "you will be in a court within the year. Whitworth will sue on the covenant and he will sue you and he will sue your husband and he will sue the trustees, and it will cost four hundred pounds and it will take three years and it will be tried in a county you have never been to."

"Then it will be tried," Judith said.

"And in the meantime the coal will go to Hull."

"Then the coal will go to Hull," Judith said, "and the men who load it will know what a hundredweight is, and in three years or in ten years there will be a beam in that shed that reads true, and this town will not have spent another eleven years paying fourteen pounds in the hundred to a man in Hull for the privilege of being cheated."

Tarrant stood with his hand on the table.

"I am bound to say this," he said, "and I am going to say it in this room because I am not going to be able to say it anywhere else, and I want it minuted and I want it minuted in my own words and not in a lawyer's."

"Mr. Tarrant," Bedloe said.

"Write it down."

Bedloe dipped the pen.

"I have known that beam was wrong since 1824," Tarrant said. "I did not know the measure of it and I did not know the way of it, and I knew the coal was going over a beam that was not true, and I told myself for eight years that a beam is a difficult thing and a man cannot be sure, and I was sure in 1824 and I have been sure every day since." He looked at the room. "I have taken my fees out of that wharf for eleven years and every one of them was paid out of coal that was weighed short. I did not set the beam. I did not cut the scale. I have never taken a penny I was not entitled to under an office I hold, and I have taken eleven years of a fee that was paid for by a fraud, and there is no court in England that will call that a crime, and I am standing in front of my own town telling them what it is."

Nobody said anything.

"Write it down," he said again.

Bedloe wrote it down.

Then Tarrant turned and looked at Judith, and there was a moment in that room when a man of sixty-two with a stick and a girl of twenty-one looked at each other across a table and neither of them said anything at all.

Then he did a thing I had not expected.

He laughed.

It was not a good laugh; it was the laugh of a man who has been carrying something for a very long time and has just been told he can put it down. It turned into a cough and the men held him up through it, and when it was over he wiped his mouth and looked at the room.

"Then hang it," he said.

The room made a noise like a wave.

"Mr. Tarrant," the nephew said.

"Be quiet, Nicholas." The old man turned his head. "I have been the steward of this manor for seven years and my father was before me for thirty-four and I am going to give the last order I ever give in this room and I am going to give it in front of everybody." He put his hand flat on the table. "The office of the beam is the holder's. The shed is the holder's. The ladder is the holder's. If the holder of that office wants the beam in that loft taken down and hung and proved, then it will be taken down and hung and proved, and any man who stands in the way of it can explain himself to me."

He turned.

"Mr. Bedloe."

"Mr. Tarrant."

"You will minute it."

"I will minute it," Bedloe said, and he was already writing.

"Mr. Harker." That was the constable. "You will take four men and go down to that wharf and you will bring that beam up out of the loft and you will hang it in the shed, and you will not damage it, and if any man on that wharf gives you trouble you will bring him to me."

The constable put his hat on.

"It will take an hour," he said.

"Then we will wait an hour," Tarrant said, "and this town will wait with us, and I would be obliged if somebody would bring a chair."

***

They brought him a chair and he sat in it at the end of the table with his stick across his knees and his eyes shut, and the room did not empty.

That is the thing I want to put down about that morning. A hundred and twenty people stood in a cold hall for an hour and a quarter and nobody left. The women stayed. The men sent the boys for beer. The parson went and came back. Mrs. Sowerby came in at half past nine with a basket of bread and went round the room with it and would not take a penny.

At ten the constable came back up the stair and put his head in.

"It is down," he said. "It is in the shed and it is whole."

Judith picked up the two piles of books and put them under her arm and went out.

I went after her, and at the top of the stair Rooke was waiting with his hat under his arm, and he did not say anything, he just turned and came down the stair beside us, one on each side, and the three of us went down the street to the wharf through a hundred and twenty people who came out of that hall after us and did not run and did not shout and walked.

## Twenty-Seven · The Beam

There were nine of us in that shed and a hundred and eleven outside.

The shed is twelve feet by twenty and with the beam hung and the pan swinging and the four men from the constable's party in it there was not room to turn. The door was open and the door is four feet wide and it is the only light and the only window, and the constable stood in the doorway with his back to us and his arms out and said, "You will stand back and you will be quiet and if you push I will shut the door and you will hear it after."

Then he turned round and said, "Who is to work it?"

"Mr. Rennard," Judith said.

The old man was sitting on his stool with his blanket round him and he had been brought in by the constable's men and put by the wall.

He looked up.

"Say that again."

"You have sat outside this door for eleven years and taken nine shillings a week to tell people it is shut," Judith said. "You are the only man in this town who has been at that door every day since 1821. You will work the beam."

The old man sat with his hands on his knees.

"I am seventy-one."

"So is the beam. It has been up in that loft for twenty-three years and it has not been asked to do anything and neither have you." Judith stood in front of him. "Mr. Rennard, I am the holder of the office and I am directing you to work the beam in my presence. I am not going to touch it. My hands will be at my sides. You will do the whole of it and you will do it slowly and you will say out loud what you are doing, and there are a hundred and ten people outside that door who will hear you."

The old man looked at her for a long time.

Then he stood up and put the blanket on the stool.

"Where is the true weight?" he said.

A folded paper came through the doorway and landed at my feet. I knew the hand that wrote it. Rooke had not left the threshold, but his arm had, and the paper carried his figures in the square hand he kept for accounts.

I gave it to Judith. She read the last line to the door.

"At a hundredweight," she said, "the yard beam will read a hundred and fourteen and this one will read a hundred. He has set it down from one pound to a hundred, and it is the same in each. He did it last night."

Rooke did not answer. He had given her the one thing he could from where he stood, and he had not come in.

***

Bedloe had the case.

He brought it in himself and put it on the stone sill and opened it, and the nine pieces of brass were in their sockets with the county stamp on them and the green in the hollows, and he stood back.

"The fifty-six," Judith said.

He gave her the fifty-six and she did not take it. She nodded at the old man, and he took it with both hands, and it was heavy and he had to set it down on the pan and he did it slowly.

The first time he moved the poise his hand went from him. Twenty-three years it had been still, and the beam tipped and would not settle, and for one breath it looked as if the true weight might not read true either. He drew his hand back, set the poise again, and this time the beam came up level and held.

"Fifty-six," he said.

"Say what that means," Judith said.

"It means the beam reads true at fifty-six pound."

"Now the twenty-eight, and then the fourteen, and then the seven, and I want every one of them said out loud."

It took a quarter of an hour. He did them in order, the four and the two and the one and the eight ounces and the four-ounce, and every one of them came up level at the mark, and he said each one out loud, and outside the door a hundred and eleven people were so quiet that I could hear the water going in the lock.

At the end he put the four-ounce in its socket and stood with his hands on the sill.

"It is true," he said. "It is true at every weight in the case and it is a better beam than the one in the yard and it has been up in that loft for twenty-three years."

"Now the coal," Judith said.

***

The constable had brought a sack up from the wharf.

It was one of the sacks that had come off a boat that morning and it was not a sack that anybody had chosen; the constable had gone down to the crane with four men and had taken the next sack that came off the boat, in front of everybody, and had chalked a cross on it and had carried it up himself.

The old man hung it on the pan.

The beam went down and the poise went out and the beam came up level and he read the mark.

"Ninety-nine and three quarters," he said, "and a quarter ounce over, which is nothing, and I will call it a hundred."

"Write it down," Judith said.

Bedloe wrote it down.

"That sack was weighed at the crane this morning," Judith said, "and it went over the yard beam at a quarter past seven, and there are forty men on that wharf who saw the mark. Will one of them come in and say what it read?"

There was a movement at the door and the constable looked round and then stepped aside, and the man who came in was the man with the board, the one who had written eleven for twelve and who had a wife who was ill and who had been writing eleven since Judith was ten.

He came in with his cap in his hands.

"Tell them what you saw," Judith said.

"An hundred and fourteen," he said.

"Say it again, and say it loud enough for the door."

"An hundred and fourteen pound." His voice cracked. "It read an hundred and fourteen and I wrote an hundred and fourteen in the book and the coal went into the sack and the sack went out of the yard."

"And what does the true beam say?"

"An hundred."

"Then weigh it again," Judith said, "and weigh it on the yard beam, and bring the yard beam in here and put the same sack on it, and do it in front of the door."

***

That took another quarter of an hour and it was the longest quarter of an hour of my life.

They could not bring the yard beam in; it is bolted to a frame in the ground and the frame is bolted to the yard. So they carried the sack out and hung it on the yard beam and the constable stood by the mark and the crowd went round in a half-circle, and the man with the board put the poise on and the beam came up level and read an hundred and fourteen, and a man in the crowd said, "That is a hundred and fourteen," and another man said, "It is a hundred," and the constable said, "It is a hundred and fourteen on this beam and it was a hundred on the other and I have seen both and so have you."

Then they carried the sack back in and hung it on the true beam and it read a hundred, and they carried it out again and it read a hundred and fourteen, and they did it a third time because the constable said, "Once more, and then nobody can say it was the sack," and the third time it was a hundred and a hundred and fourteen.

At the end of it the old man stood by the beam with his hand on the arm.

"Eleven years," he said, and his voice was not loud. "I have sat outside this door for eleven years and I have taken nine shillings a week and I have said *shut* to every man, woman and child that came past, and the whole of it was to keep a hundredweight at a hundred and fourteen."

He turned round and looked at the door.

"My brother is in the churchyard," he said. "Peter Thwaite. He was a keelman and he was thirty-eight and he was in the north end when it went up. And I have been sitting on a stool outside this shed for eleven years being paid to say the beam was shut, and the beam was not shut, it was in the loft, and I could have carried it down myself any day of those eleven years if anybody had asked me."

Nobody said anything.

"Mr. Rennard," Judith said.

"I have not finished."

"Go on."

"Before I go, I have a thing to say, and I am going to say it in front of the door." The old man turned and faced the doorway. "I have known the yard beam read heavy since 1825. I knew it because I have weighed coal all my life and I could feel it in my hands. And I have never said it. Not once. Not to my brother's widow and not to the parson and not to the man who pays me." He put his hand flat on the iron arm of the true beam. "I am seventy-one and I have not got long and I am not going to stand in front of God with that on me, so I am saying it now, and any man in this town who wants to spit at me can do it in the street and I will not say a word."

Then he took his hand off the arm and said, "The beam is proved. It reads true at every weight in the case and it reads a hundred on a sack that reads a hundred and fourteen on the other. That is what I have to say."

***

Judith had not moved from the middle of the floor.

She had stood there for the whole of it with her hands at her sides, exactly as she had said she would, and she had not touched the iron once.

Now she turned round and looked at the door, and the light came in past the heads of the crowd and lay on the floor in a long shape.

"The office of the beam is mine," she said. "It is nine pounds twelve a year and it cannot be held by deputy and it cannot be sold and it cannot be let and it has been shut for eleven years by a covenant my father signed, and I have not opened it. I want that understood by everybody in this street. The beam is hung and the beam is proved and the beam has weighed one sack of coal to establish what the yard beam has been doing, and it has not weighed a single pound for trade and I am not going to work it today or tomorrow or this year."

She looked at the doorway.

"But the office is not the beam. The office is the duty to keep a true weight in this town, and I have not kept it, and neither did my father, and neither did the man who holds the market and the crane and the shambles and the toll. We have all of us taken a fee for eleven years and the fee was paid out of a false hundredweight, and there is not one of us in this shed who did not know it, and I have been the worst of the lot because I have known it since I was ten and I said nothing because I was a child and then because I was a girl and then because I was afraid."

She took a breath.

"So this is my account, and it is the account of the fourteenth office, and it is the only four words I am going to say in this town about the last eleven years."

She said them.

"The beam is out."

***

There was a woman at the front of the crowd with a child on her hip.

I had written her a receipt in October for a hundredweight of coal. Her name was Ellen Sunderland and she lived at number nine and she had a penny and she had spent it on a piece of paper that said she had bought a sack.

She was standing four feet from the door and she had heard the whole of it, and when Judith said the four words she did not cheer and she did not cry. She shifted the child onto her other hip and said, in a voice that carried because the street had gone quiet:

"Fourteen pound in the hundred."

"That is right," the constable said.

"On every sack."

"On every sack."

She stood there with the child and looked at the yard beam standing in the yard with the sack on it and the poise at a hundred and fourteen.

"Then I have been buying ninety-eight pound of coal for sevenpence for four years," she said, "and I have four children and one of them is in the churchyard, and I have been going without my own dinner in January to buy it."

Nobody said anything.

"I want it written down," she said. "Not the money. I know I will never see the money. I want it written down that I stood in this street and I heard it."

"It is written down," Bedloe said from the door, and he held up the book, and he had written it.

***

Daniel Hogg was at the back of the crowd.

He had come up from the almshouse on his own, in the cold, with his hands in his coat, and he stood at the corner of the warehouse where he had stood on the night of the nineteenth of December 1821 and watched the north end go up.

I went to him when the crowd began to move.

"Mr. Hogg."

"Mrs. Rooke."

"Did you hear it?"

"I heard the whole of it. I was at the back and the door was open and there is nothing wrong with my ears." He looked at the shed. "It read a hundred and it read a hundred and fourteen and I have known that since 1825."

"Then why did you not say it?"

He turned his head and looked at me with the flat patience of a man who has been asked a stupid question by somebody he likes.

"Because a man with no hands cannot hold a beam," he said. "And because when I said a thing in 1821 about a man in a good coat, Mr. Tarrant came the next morning and put a story in front of nine of us and paid for this room, and I have been in that room eleven years and it is warm and there is a pump in the yard."

I stood with him.

"And now?"

"Now a girl has done it," he said. "A girl of ten that I saw in the yard that night in her nightgown. She has done it in a market hall in front of the whole town and she has not asked me for anything and she has not needed me to say a word." He put his hands in his pockets. "You will want to put this in your book, Mrs. Rooke. I have been waiting eleven years for a man to come and stand up, and it was a girl, and she did not need me at all, and I find that I am glad and I find that I am ashamed, and both of them are in me at once and I have not sorted them out."

"Mr. Hogg."

"Go on, then."

"What was in the north end that night?"

He looked at the wharf.

"Coal," he said. "Six ton and a half of wet coal and a tarpaulin on it and a night man at his sister's at Thorneley." He turned. "And a man in a good blue coat with his boots on, who came down at eleven at night to take the tarpaulin off, and I have known that since the morning Mr. Tarrant told nine of us that Mr. Kell was in his bed."

I stood very still.

"You knew he was there."

"I knew he was there," Daniel Hogg said. "I saw him come down. I saw the coat and the shoulder and the side of the face and I knew the coat, and when Mr. Tarrant said he was in his bed I said nothing, because I had no hands and I had four children and no trade, and because a man who contradicts the steward of a manor in a yard with the ashes still hot is a man who is looking for a room in another county."

"And the story you have told for eleven years?"

"I have told it because it was the only story in this town that had a place in it for me." He looked at the shed. "And now the girl has said the four words, and I am seventy-one and I have not got long, and I have decided this morning, standing at the back of that crowd, that I am going to say the other thing before I die, and I am going to say it in the vestry in March when the bishop's officer comes, and I am going to say it under oath, and then they can do what they like with an old man with no hands."

***

Nobody cheered.

I want to put that down because a person who has not stood in a crowd at a thing like that expects a noise, and there was no noise.

What happened was that a hundred and eleven people stood in a street in January and looked at a shed, and then one woman at the back began to cry, and it was Mrs. Sowerby, and she was ashamed of it and put her apron over her face, and the man next to her put his hand on her shoulder, and then the crowd began to talk, quietly, in the way a crowd talks when it has just been told a thing it has known for eleven years.

I stood in the door of that shed with my hands in my muff.

Rooke was on the other side of the doorway, outside, with his hat under his arm, and he had not come in. He had stood out there for the whole of it, in the cold, and he had not come in and he had not said a word.

He looked at me across the door.

I did not go to him. I want to put that down too. I stood in the door of a shed in a coal town with my husband four feet away on the other side of the threshold and I did not go to him, because I had been two months in that town and I had just watched a girl of twenty-one do the thing I had come a hundred and eleven miles to do, and I needed a minute to be a person in my own right before I was anybody's wife.

He understood it. He did not move.

***

Bedloe came out last with the books under his arm.

He stopped in the door and put his hat on and looked at the crowd and then at Judith.

"Miss Kell."

"Mr. Bedloe."

"That was well done," he said, "and I am going to tell you what it will cost, because somebody must and it may as well be me. Mr. Whitworth will bring his action on the covenant before the spring. He will not win it, in my opinion, but he will bring it, and it will cost you three hundred pounds and three years. And the coal will go to Goole for a while."

"Then it will come back."

"It will come back," Bedloe said, "when Goole weighs short and Barrowfield does not, and that will be a year and a half, and in the meantime this town will be poorer than it has been since 1821 and there will be men in this street who will say it was your doing."

"I know."

"Do you." He looked at her. "Then you are a better man than I am, and I have been in this trade thirty-one years."

He turned to go and then stopped.

"There is one thing more and then I will leave you to your town." He looked at the yard beam, which was still standing in the yard with the sack hanging on it and the poise at a hundred and fourteen. "That beam was hung in 1809. I was twenty-eight years old. I witnessed the indenture of gift and I saw it hung and I said, 'That is a fine piece of iron,' and I have walked past it every day for twenty-three years."

"Mr. Bedloe."

"I am not going to make a speech," he said. "I am going to say one sentence and then I am going to go home and be ill for a week." He put his hat straight, and then he laughed, one breath of it, without any pleasure in it at all. "My scale was wrong."

Then he looked past Judith at Tarrant, who was sitting in his chair in the corner of the shed with his stick across his knees, and the two old men looked at each other for a while.

"Nicholas," Bedloe said.

"Bedloe."

"You knew in 1824."

"I knew in 1824," Tarrant said.

"And you let me go on witnessing your deeds."

"I let you go on witnessing my deeds," Tarrant said, "and you let me go on being the man who was too useful to be asked, and we have both of us been very comfortable, and I am not going to spend the last month of my life being lied to by the only man in this county who has known me since I was a boy."

Bedloe stood in the door.

"Then I will see you on Sunday," he said.

"You will see me on Sunday," Tarrant said.

## Twenty-Eight · The Money

The wedding was on the fifteenth and on the fifteenth she did not come out of the house.

I went up at eight and the servant with the pinned sleeves said, "She is in the parlour," and I went in and Judith was sitting at the blue cloth in a plain gown with the fourteen books in front of her and no flowers and no veil.

"The fifteenth," she said.

"The fifteenth."

"There are people in the church."

"There are about sixty people in the church and the parson has been there since seven and Mr. Tarrant has been carried there and is sitting in the front pew in his chair."

She looked at the books.

"Mrs. Rooke, what do I do?"

That is the only time in the whole of it that she asked me to decide for her, and I have thought about my answer a great deal since.

"You do what you said you would do," I said. "You told me in October that on the twenty-first of November you would be a woman who had broken her father's word in a public place and the coal would go to Hull. It did not happen that way. You broke nothing and the coal is going to Goole anyway for a year and a half and that is not the same thing." I sat down opposite her. "You told me that a wharfinger who cannot keep a promise is a wharfinger whose weights nobody will trust. Yesterday you proved the weights. Today is a different question and it is not a question of trust, it is a question of what you want."

She sat with her hands flat on the table.

"I do not want to be married," she said.

"Then do not."

"That is not the whole of it." She looked up. "I do not want to be married and I have a settlement that is engrossed and a man who has been decent to me and a guardian who is dying and sixty people in a church, and if I do not go, this town will say that Judith Kell proved her beam and then destroyed her guardian's last arrangement in the same week."

"Yes," I said. "They will say that."

"And you are not going to tell me it does not matter."

"No."

"Because it does matter."

"It matters," I said, "and not as much as the other thing, and you are twenty-one years old and you are going to have to learn the difference between a thing that matters and a thing that decides."

She sat there for a while.

Then she got up and went to the door and called the servant and said, "Tell Mr. Tarrant's man that Miss Kell will be at the church at eleven and that she is not going to be late and that she will not require a carriage."

***

They were married at eleven and it took twenty minutes and I was in the third pew on the left with Rooke beside me.

I will not describe it. Weddings are the same in every county and the only thing anybody remembers afterwards is who cried and what the weather did, and the weather was cold and nobody cried except Mrs. Sowerby, who cried at the beam and at the wedding and would have cried at a dog show that week.

What I remember is the nephew's face when he turned round at the end.

He looked like a man who had come to a thing he had decided to do and had found it done, and there is no expression in the world quite like it, and I have seen it since on other men and I always think of that morning.

And I remember Tarrant in his chair in the front pew with his hands on his stick and his eyes shut, and I do not know whether he was praying or sleeping or whether a man in his condition simply has to shut his eyes sometimes.

But I do remember what he did at the end.

When the parson had finished and the clerk had written it in the register and the nephew had kissed her and the sixty people in that church had begun to move, Tarrant put his stick on the floor and got up.

It took him a long time. The nephew went to him and he put his hand on the nephew's arm and got up, and he stood in the front pew in his coat with his hat in his hand and he waited until the church was quiet again.

"No speech," he said. "I have made one this month and it took eleven years to get ready."

Somebody laughed.

"I have been the steward of this manor for seven years and my father was before me for thirty-four and this morning I have given the last of it to my nephew, and he is a better man than I was at his age and I want that said in this church." He looked at Judith. "Miss Kell. Mrs. Tarrant. I have known you since you were ten years old and I have done you a great deal of harm and a certain amount of good, and I have never once been able to tell which was which, and I am not going to be able to tell on this side of the grave." He put his hat on. "The ring is my father's and I have worn it since 1825 and I have taken it off this morning and given it to my nephew and told him what it means. That is all. You may go and eat."

Then he sat down again, and the church let out its breath, and everybody went out into the cold.

***

The accounts were settled on the eighteenth and it took three days and it cost me more than I have ever been paid for anything.

It was done in Bedloe's office with the door shut and the six of us: Bedloe, the nephew, Judith, Tarrant in his chair, Rooke, and me.

Bedloe had it all worked out on a sheet in his own hand.

"Four hundred and eighty pounds," he said. "That is the sum of the excess fees taken by the fourteen offices of this parish in eleven years, calculated at fourteen parts in a hundred of the annual value of the thirteen saleable offices, and it is the figure a court would arrive at and it is the figure I have arrived at and I have done it three times."

"Four hundred and eighty," Judith said.

"Four hundred and eighty pounds fifteen shillings and tenpence, if you want it exact, and I would rather have it exact."

"Who is it owed to?"

"To the persons who paid it," Bedloe said. "Which is every man, woman and child in this parish who has bought coal since 1821, and there are about four thousand of them and most of them cannot be identified and none of them can prove what they bought." He put the sheet down. "So it will not be paid to them. It will be paid into a fund and administered by the vestry for the relief of the poor of this parish, and there will be a book, and I have drawn the scheme and it is on the second sheet."

"And who pays it?"

"The holders of the thirteen offices, in proportion to their fees."

There was a silence in that room.

"Then I pay most of it," Judith said.

"You pay three hundred and twelve on the four hundred and eighty," Bedloe said, "and I have thrown the fifteen and tenpence away to make the composition come out even, because a vestry will vote for a round sum and will argue about a halfpenny until Easter. And your husband pays nothing at all, because the offices were yours for the whole of the eleven years and he did not hold them. And I have drawn it as a composition at thirteen shillings in the pound, and the vestry will take it, because the alternative is to sue a girl of twenty-one for a hundred and sixty-eight pounds and spend two hundred getting it."

"I will pay it," Tarrant said.

Everybody turned.

"I am answerable for it," Tarrant said. "I took my fees out of that wharf for eleven years and I knew in 1824. I have nine hundred pounds in the funds and a house and no children but one nephew, and I will pay the whole of it, and it will be done with, and the fund can be opened at Easter."

"No," Judith said.

"Miss Kell."

"No," Judith said again, and she said it quietly and it stopped him. "Mr. Tarrant, you paid nine hundred pounds in 1821 to clear a mortgage and you have not been repaid and there is no paper on it, and if you pay this now there will be no paper on that either, and in twenty years there will be nobody in this town who remembers that you paid twice for the same thing and everybody will remember that you were the man who knew in 1824." She put her hand flat on the table. "I will pay it. It is my office and my eleven years and I was ten when it started and I am twenty-one now and I have been the holder of those offices since I was ten years old in everything except the signature."

"You were a child."

"I was a child for three years," Judith said, "and then I was a girl who kept the accounts of fourteen offices and signed nothing, and I have been that for eight years, and I am not going to be told now that I was a child the whole time because it is convenient for the men who were not."

Tarrant sat with his hands on his stick.

"You are very like your father," he said.

"I know," Judith said. "I have been told it all my life by people who meant it as a kindness and I have never once heard it as one, and I am going to hear it as one today because you are dying and because he is dead and because there is nobody left in this county who remembers him signing a covenant in a room with a fire in it except you and Mr. Bedloe."

Nobody said anything for a while.

"Three hundred pounds," the nephew said. "Where will you get three hundred pounds?"

"I will mortgage the toll at the turn."

The room went quiet in a different way.

"The toll is seventy-five pounds a year," Bedloe said slowly, "and it is the best of the fourteen, and if you mortgage it for three hundred and twelve pounds at five per cent for ten years you will pay forty pounds a year out of it for ten years and you will have thirty-five pounds a year to live on, and the thirteen offices between them will bring you in nothing else but the fees you cannot remit."

"I know."

"And your husband has just married you."

"My husband has just married me," Judith said, "and he has a clerk's salary and a good coat and a mother in Thorneley, and he knew what he was marrying and he is sitting in that chair and he has not said a word for ten minutes, and I am going to ask him now, in front of everybody, whether he will take me on those terms."

The nephew sat looking at the table.

Then he said, "I will take you on those terms."

"Say why."

"Because you proved a beam in front of a hundred and ten people," he said, "and I have been in this town all my life and I have never once done a thing in front of a hundred and ten people, and I have decided that I would rather be the man who married the woman who did than the man who did not."

Judith looked at him for a long moment.

"All right," she said.

***

Bedloe closed his office on the eighteenth of January and he did it in the afternoon and there was nobody there but me and the clerk.

He had the shutters open and no fire, and the desk was clear, and there were four boxes on the floor with tape on them.

"Hull," he said. "My sister's husband has a practice in Hull and he has been asking me for nine years and I have been saying no for nine years and I have run out of reasons."

"Will you draw papers in Hull?"

"I will draw papers in Hull," he said, "and I will be one of nine attorneys in a town of thirty thousand and nobody will ever ask me to witness a covenant again, and that is the whole of what I want."

The clerk took two boxes down.

"Mr. Bedloe."

"Proceed."

"There is a book."

He stood with his hat in his hand.

"Yes," he said. "There is a book."

He went to the cupboard and took out the quarto in reversed calf and put it on the desk.

"Three hundred and sixty-six entries and seven blanks," he said. "It has been in that cupboard for fourteen years and I have looked at it perhaps twenty times and I have never been able to make a thing of it, and I am fifty-one and I am going to a town where nobody knows what it is and it would sit in a box in my sister's attic until I died and then it would be sold with the furniture."

He pushed it across the desk.

"Take it."

I looked at it.

"Mr. Bedloe, I cannot take this."

"You can and you will," he said, "and I will tell you why, and then I will ask you for something. Farrow began it in 1806 in a room in the Shire Hall and he brought it here in 1809 and he went on with it until 1818, and I have added to it twice, and the last thing he wrote in it was four words at the bottom of the last page, and neither he nor I ever found out what it was for." He put his hand flat on the cover. "You are the only person who has ever looked at those numbers and understood that they are in order. You did it in an afternoon. I have had it fourteen years and I have never known what to do with it."

"And what do you want?"

"I want you to write to me in Hull," he said, "once a year, and tell me what you have found, and when you have found the whole of it I want you to write and tell me that too, and I will be sixty or seventy and it will be a letter I will be glad of."

I put my hand on the cover.

"I will write to you," I said.

"Then take it and go," Bedloe said, "because I have four boxes and a sister in Hull and a clerk who has been with me eleven years and is going to cry in a minute, and I would rather he did it after I have gone."

***

I walked home with the book under my arm and it weighed about four pounds.

The town was quiet. The market was done. There was a man sweeping the steps of the assembly rooms and two boys with a handcart and the light was going at half past four.

***

Tarrant sent for me on the twenty-fourth of January and I went, and this time there was no clerk in the passage and no man in the room, and the chair had been moved to the window and he was in it with a rug and a table beside him with one sheet of paper on it.

"Sit down," he said. "I have not got long and I am going to say a thing and then give you a paper and then you will go."

"Say it."

"I have been thinking about the third man," he said.

I sat very still.

"The man standing back by the fire," Tarrant said. "You told my nephew's wife that there were three men in that room and one of them was standing back by the fire breathing slow, and that you could not name him, and that a witness stands at a table."

"Yes."

"It was Whitworth," Tarrant said. "The elder. He came up from Hull on the thirteenth of October 1821 and he was in my father's counting house for two days and he was in that room, and he did not sign and he did not witness and he stood back by the fire with his hands behind him and watched my father put his hand on Amos Kell's hand and he did not say a word." He looked out of the window. "And when it was done he came out into the passage where I was standing and he said, 'It is a fine thing to watch a man be persuaded,' and he put his hat on and went down the stair."

I found that I had my hands in my lap and could not remember putting them there.

"Why are you telling me this?"

"Because I am going to die in a fortnight," Tarrant said, "and because it is the only thing I have left that is worth anything, and because I have carried it since 1824 and I am tired."

He picked up the sheet of paper.

"This is a statement," he said. "It is dated, it is signed, and it is witnessed by my nephew and by the parson, and it says what I have just told you and it says what I said in the market hall about 1824 and it says that my father put his hand on Amos Kell's hand, and it is not worth anything in a court because a statement by a dead man is not evidence, and it is worth everything to a girl who is going to be told for the rest of her life that her father was a willing party."

He held it out.

"Give it to her," he said, "and do not give it to her until I am in the ground."

I took it.

"Mr. Tarrant."

"I am listening."

"There is a book," I said. "It has three hundred and sixty-six entries and seven blanks and it was begun in 1806 by a man named Farrow, and the covenant of 1821 is in it at number 204, and there are nine numbers written in the margin of nine returns in a Shire Hall, and the highest is 366 and the lowest is 73."

Tarrant looked at me.

"And?"

"And I do not know what it is," I said, "and you have been the steward of this manor for seven years and your father was before you for thirty-four, and I am going to ask you a question and I want the truth and not the comfortable thing."

"Ask it."

"Did your father ever speak of a book?"

He sat with his hands on the arms of the chair.

"Once," he said. "In 1823. He had had a letter and he read it and put it in the fire and he sat for a while and then he said, 'There is a book somewhere with all of us in it,' and I said, what book, and he said, 'Never mind,' and he never spoke of it again."

***

Tarrant died on the second of February, at his house on the market cross, in his bed, with his nephew in the room, and he was buried on the sixth in the same row as the six from 1821 and there was no verse on his stone either.

## Twenty-Nine · The Steps

They buried him on the sixth of February at eleven in the morning and it was colder than any day that winter.

The whole town came. That is not a figure of speech. There were four hundred people in a churchyard that holds two hundred and they stood on the slope and in the path and among the stones, and the ones at the back could not hear the parson and did not need to.

I stood on the left, four rows back, with Rooke beside me and Mrs. Sowerby on the other side of me, and Judith stood at the front in black with her husband's arm and a bonnet she had borrowed from Mrs. Ellerbeck because she had not had time to have one made.

Bedloe was there.

He had come up from Hull on the mail and he stood at the very back under the yew tree with his hat in his hands and he did not go up to the grave and he did not come to the house afterwards, and when the parson had finished he turned and walked down the path and out of the gate, and the two men who had drawn the papers of that town for thirty-one years did not speak to each other at the end, because one of them was in the ground.

I watched him go. He was fifty-one and he looked sixty and he had a bag with him and he went down the hill towards the market with his coat collar up, and I have never seen him since and I have his book.

The men from the wharf stood together on the right, forty of them, in their good coats, and the man with the board was among them and had his cap off, and Daniel Hogg stood at the front of them with his hands in his pockets because he had no hat and could not take one off.

The old man from the stool was not there. He had said he would not come and he did not come and I understood it and so did everybody else.

The parson read the lesson and said the thing about a man's days being threescore and ten, and he did not say anything about 1824, and he did not say anything about the market hall, and when it was over he said, "We commit his body to the ground," and the men from the wharf shifted their feet.

That was the whole of the funeral. There was no eulogy and no bell beyond the one they ring for everybody, and if a stranger had walked in he would not have known that the man in the coffin had been the steward of that manor for seven years and had stood up in a market hall in January and told a hundred and twenty people that he had known a beam was false since 1824.

I have thought since that this is what most funerals are. The town knows and the parson does not say.

***

There was sherry at the house afterwards and a great deal of standing about.

I did not stay long. I had a thing in my pocket that I had been carrying since the twenty-fourth of January and I had promised a dying man that I would not give it up until he was in the ground, and he was in the ground, and I stood in a room full of people in black with my hand on my pocket and waited for the room to thin.

It thinned at two.

Judith was in the passage seeing out the last of the vestry when I came up to her.

"Mrs. Tarrant."

"Mrs. Rooke." She had her hands full of gloves and a plate. "You are going."

"I am going on Thursday," I said, "and before I go there is a thing I have to give you and I would rather give it to you in a room and not in a passage."

She looked at me.

"The parlour," she said. "It is empty."

***

It was the room with the blue cloth.

She put the plate down on the sideboard and shut the door and stood with her back to it.

"Tell me."

I took the sheet out of my pocket and put it on the table.

"That is a statement," I said. "It is dated the twenty-fourth of January, it is signed by Mr. Tarrant the elder and witnessed by his nephew and by the parson, and he gave it to me and told me not to give it to you until he was in the ground. I have not read it. I have carried it in my pocket for thirteen days and I have not opened it and you may believe that or not."

She looked at the sheet on the blue cloth.

"What does it say?"

"He told me what it says," I said. "I am going to tell you before you read it, because a page read alone in a room is carried alone, and you have carried enough alone."

"Go on."

"It says there were three men in that room on the fourteenth of October 1821. Your father, who signed. Mr. Tarrant's father, who put his hand on your father's hand. And a third man who stood back by the fire and did not sign and did not witness, and the third man was Whitworth the elder, who had come up from Hull the day before, and when it was over he came out into the passage and said to the nineteen-year-old boy who was standing there, 'It is a fine thing to watch a man be persuaded.'"

Judith stood with her back to the door.

"Whitworth," she said.

"Whitworth the elder. He has been dead eleven years and his son has the wharf and the covenant and the trade and he will bring his action in the spring, and there is nobody left to be angry with except a dead man in Hull."

She came to the table and picked the sheet up and read it.

It took her four minutes. I stood by the window and looked out at the churchyard and did not watch her face.

At the end she put it down.

"Two breaths," she said.

"Two breaths."

"He stopped for two breaths and there was a man in the room who had come up from Hull to watch him stop." She put her hand flat on the sheet. "Two breaths, then. That is what happened to my father. He was a good man and he was frightened and he was persuaded by a man with a hand and watched by a man with a hat, and he signed, and then he went down to the north end three months later to take a tarpaulin off a heap of wet coal and it killed him."

"Yes."

"And there is nobody to hang for it."

"There is nobody to hang for it."

She stood at the table for a while.

"Mrs. Rooke," she said, "I want to tell you a thing and I want to tell it to you once. In October I asked you to find out whether my father signed it himself, and you have found out, and the answer is worse than either of the answers I had made up in my head, and I have spent a week being angry about it and I am not going to spend eleven years." She looked up. "I am going to put it in that drawer with the fourteen books and I am going to take it out on the fourteenth of January every year and look at it for as long as it takes to read one page, and then I am going to put it back and go and do the accounts. That is the whole of what I am going to do with it. It is not forgiveness and it is not forgetting and I do not recommend it to anybody, and it is what I have got."

I stood by the window.

"That will do," I said.

"It will have to."

***

She came out to the steps with me.

The churchyard was empty by then and the light was going and there was frost coming down on the stones, and the six from 1821 were four rows down on the left and the new one was at the top and there was a man still filling it in with a shovel, working slowly, the way a man works when he is the last of a party.

Judith stood at the top of the steps with her arms folded.

"Thursday," she said.

"Thursday. The carrier at seven and the mail at Thorneley and Collow on Friday night if the roads hold."

"And then?"

"And then I am a woman in a house in Nether Collow with a table and a cloth on it and a husband who signs his letters with an initial." I put my gloves on. "And you are a married woman with thirteen offices and a mortgage on the toll and a lawsuit coming in the spring and a visitation in March and a husband who mends pumps."

She almost smiled.

"Mrs. Rooke," she said. "I am going to ask you a question and I want you to think about it before you answer, because it is the only thing I have to give you and I have been saving it for a week."

"Ask it."

"What do you want?"

I stood at the top of the steps of a parish church in a coal town in February with my gloves half on.

"I do not know," I said.

"That is not an answer."

"It is the only one I have."

Judith looked at me for a while.

"Then I will tell you what I have noticed," she said, "and you may take it or leave it. In two months you have found a beam, a loft, a case of brass, a book in a Shire Hall and a line in a lawyer's book that nobody in this town had ever read. Every one of those you did for somebody else. You have not once done a thing because you wanted it." She pulled her shawl round her. "Next week you will go home and be useful in that house and nobody will ask you what you want, and you will think that is the same as being good."

I stood with my gloves in my hand.

"That is a hard thing to say at a funeral."

"It is the hardest thing I have got," Judith said, "and I have been saving it since Christmas, and if I do not say it now I will never say it, because next week you will be a hundred and eleven miles away and I will be here with a mortgage and I will have decided that you were a woman who came and did a great deal and went away."

She came down one step and put her hand out and I took it.

"Thank you," she said. "For the question and for the answer and for not being kind to me."

"Mrs. Tarrant."

"Then tell me."

"Your father stopped for two breaths," I said. "That is the thing to keep. Not the hand and not the hat and not the man from Hull. He stopped for two breaths and he thought about it and then he did the thing he thought was the best thing in a bad room, and there is not one man in this churchyard who has done better with two breaths."

She let go of my hand.

"Go home," she said. "You have a husband and a table and a window that does not shut."

***

I went down the hill and I did not go straight home.

I went to the almshouse first because I had a thing to leave and it was Wednesday and I was going on Thursday and I am not a woman who leaves a town without paying her accounts.

Daniel Hogg was in the doorway of the second room with his blanket.

"Mrs. Rooke."

"Mr. Hogg."

"You are going."

"Tomorrow at seven."

He looked at the pump.

"I have been in this room eleven years," he said, "and a woman came in October and sat on that bench and asked me what I saw, and nobody had asked me what I saw, and I have been thinking about it every day since and I have not got to the end of it."

"Mr. Hogg, there is a thing I want to leave with you." I got out of my pocket a folded paper. "It is a statement of what you told me in October. I wrote it out on the twenty-eighth of December and I read it back to you and you said it was right and you put your mark on it, and it is in your words and not mine, and there is a copy of it in the vestry and a copy with Mr. Bedloe in Hull."

He took it.

"And what am I to do with it?"

"Nothing, unless the bishop's officer comes in March and asks. Then you give it to him and you say what you told me and you say it under oath." I put my hands in my muff. "You are seventy-one and you told me you had not got long and that you were going to say the other thing before you die. That is the other thing, written down, so that when you say it there will be a paper behind you."

He held the paper in his two bad hands.

"I cannot read it," he said. "I have not been able to read for nine years. I can make my letters and I cannot read a paper."

"Then I will tell you what it says and you will remember it, because it is your own words and not mine." I stood in the yard with the pump dripping. "It says: I, Daniel Hogg, of the almshouse in Barrowfield, was on the wharf on the night of the nineteenth of December 1821 and saw a man in a blue coat at the north end door. It says the night man was not there and the coal was wet and the tarpaulin was on it, and it says you told the night man on the Tuesday and he did not see to it. And at the end it says: and I have said nothing for eleven years, and that is the whole of what I have to say about it."

He stood in the door with the paper.

"That is it," he said. "That is what I saw."

"Then it is done," I said, "and I am going tomorrow, and I am not going to say thank you, because you did not do it for me."

He looked up.

"No," he said. "I did it for the girl in the nightgown."

***

Mrs. Sowerby was in the back room with the accounts when I came in and she had a pot on and two cups out and she had known what time I would be back, which is the whole of what a woman is who keeps a shop.

"Sit down," she said.

I sat down.

"Four pounds eleven," she said. "That is what I put in the till for two years and it is in the book at the back in my own hand and I have been thinking about it since October."

"Mrs. Sowerby."

"Let me say it." She sat down opposite me. "When you came in October I had been paying out of that till for two years and the only part of it I had ever let myself add up was four pounds eleven, and I had been telling myself it was my own wickedness, and I had got so far down that I thought a woman who sells short weight is a wicked woman and there was nothing else to think. And you came in and weighed a sack and said, it is not you, it is a beam." She turned her cup a quarter turn. "I have not been able to explain to anybody what that did. There is no word for it in the language I have."

"There is a word," I said. "It is the same word as being told you are not mad."

"Yes," she said. "That is the word."

We drank the tea.

"Mrs. Rooke," she said. "What will you do now?"

"Go home and be a wife."

"And after?"

I looked at the fire.

"I have a book," I said. "It is three hundred and sixty-six entries long and seven of them are blank, and it was begun in 1806 by a man in a Shire Hall who was frightened of something, and there are nine offices in England marked in the margin of a return and one of them is here." I turned my cup. "I am going to find out what it is."

"And that will take how long?"

"I do not know," I said. "It may take years. It may take the rest of my life and come to nothing and be a book in a box in an attic."

Mrs. Sowerby looked at me over her cup.

"Then you had better take a good pen," she said.

***

That night I went and stood on the step outside the shop.

The market was empty and the light was going and there was frost coming down on the stones and the churchyard was up the hill and out of sight and the wharf was down the hill and out of sight, and there was a dog and a boy and a woman going by with a lantern and then there was nobody at all.

"What do you want?" I said, out loud, to nobody, in a street, in the cold.

And then I said, "Edward."

I said it once, quietly, the way a person says a word in a language they have not spoken since they were a child, and it came out right, and it was the first time in eleven months that I had said it, and there was nobody to hear it.

Then I cleared my throat and went in, because it was February and it was cold and I had a bag to pack.

## Thirty · Nether Collow

The carrier left Barrowfield at seven on the seventh of February with a crate of geese and a woman going to Selby and me.

Mrs. Sowerby came down to the market to see me off, which is a thing I had not expected and did not want and was glad of.

"You will write," she said.

"Once a month."

"Once a month is a thing people say." She put a parcel in my hands. "That is bread and cheese and a piece of the cake and you will eat it on the road and not save it, because a woman who saves cake for a man who is not there eats it in March and it is stale and then she is sad about cake."

"Mrs. Sowerby."

"Out with it."

"The four pounds eleven," I said. "The book at the back with the difference in your own hand. Do not stop keeping it."

She looked at me.

"Why?"

"Because in ten years somebody will write the history of the weights of this town, and it will say that the beam was false from 1809 to 1833 and that it was found out by a girl with a pair of calipers, and there will be no mention in it of a woman who paid the difference out of her own till for two years so that the poor of this street should not go short while the thing was still going on." I got up into the cart. "Keep the book. Put the date on every page. A book with dates in it is a book that a hundred years cannot argue with."

She stood in the market with her arms folded.

"You are a hard woman, Mrs. Rooke," she said, "and I am going to miss you every day."

***

The road back was the road out and it was worse going than it had been in October because it was February and the ruts were frozen and the light was short.

Two nights. Thorneley the first night and the same inn and a different room, because the room we had had in October had a man in it and a fire and a woman who had said a thing out loud in the dark.

I sat in the room they gave me and ate the cake and did not save any of it.

The second day it snowed from eleven and the coach was slow and we came into Nether Collow at half past six in the evening with the snow coming down in the light of the inn windows and the church tower black against it.

I got down in the yard with my bag and the book wrapped in a piece of sacking and stood in the snow for a moment.

Nobody came out. It is not that kind of inn. A woman getting down from a carrier at half past six with one bag is not an event, and I have been that woman in a great many yards and I have always liked it.

Then I walked up the lane.

***

The house is at the end of the lane with the pump on the corner and a wall and a gate and a path and a door with a step, and there was a light in the kitchen and a light in the office and no light upstairs.

I put my bag down at the gate and stood in the lane for a moment with the snow on my shoulders.

Sixteen weeks. That is all it had been. Sixteen weeks and a hundred and eighteen miles and it was the same gate and the same path and the same step with the worn place in the middle, and I stood there with my hand on the gate and understood that I had come back to a house that I had left as one woman and was entering as another, and that the house did not know and would not be told, and that this is the whole of what coming home is.

I went up the path and opened the door.

Mrs. Trewin was in the passage with a candle.

"Mrs. Rooke."

"Mrs. Trewin."

"You are early. You wrote the ninth."

"The roads held," I said, and then I stood in the passage and laughed, and I could not stop, and it was not a good laugh and it was not a bad one, and Mrs. Trewin stood there with the candle and did not know what to do with her face.

Then she said, "There is a fire in the office and there is soup and I will bring it," and went away, which is the correct thing and which I have thanked her for since.

I went up with my bag and washed at the basin and put on a dry shift and stood in the bedroom with the candle.

The book was wrapped in a piece of sacking. I unwrapped it and put it on the chest of drawers and did not put it on the shelf, and I saw myself do it and have thought about it since. A woman who puts a book on a shelf has decided where it lives. I put it where I would see it when I woke up.

Then I did a thing I had done for other people in four counties and had never once done for myself.

I got out a sheet of paper and headed it with my own name and set down what I was worth.

*The toll at the turn, seventy-five pounds.*
*Mortgage, three hundred and twelve at five per cent, ten years, forty pounds a year.*
*Left, thirty-five pounds a year.*

I sat on the edge of the bed with the pen in my hand and looked at it for a while.

Thirty-five pounds a year, a house I did not own, and a husband whose practice was smaller than the one that had been taken off him in the summer. In Barrowfield I had handled eleven years of another woman's money and found three thousand pounds in it, and here I was at the end of a lane in February with the snow coming down, and my whole estate would go on one side of a sheet of paper with the other side left empty.

So I turned the sheet over and made a second column, and I did not put money in it, and I have never shown it to anybody until now.

*The beam, out. The visitation, granted, March. A girl of twenty-one who will open a drawer on the fourteenth of January every year of her life. A lawyer in Hull who asks for one letter a year. A shop in Barrowfield that keeps its own book with the dates in it. A man downstairs who has asked me for nothing except a promise I have not made yet.*

I folded it and put it in the front of the quarto, where it still is.

Then I went down.

***

He was in the office with the lamp and the table and the cloth on the table and a paper in front of him and he had not heard the door.

I stood in the doorway.

He looked up.

"Mercy."

"Edward," I said.

I want to put down what happened in the next four seconds, because I have thought about them a great deal.

He did not move. That is the first thing. He sat with his hand on the paper and looked at me and did not move at all, and his face did a thing I have only seen once, which was a man's face when a thing he had decided not to hope for arrives in the doorway with snow on its shoulders.

Then he said, "Say it again."

"Edward."

He got up and came round the table and stopped a foot away and put his hands on my arms and held them.

"Eleven months," he said.

"Eleven months."

"I have been in this house for sixteen weeks with a cloth on that table at four o'clock and I have not once known what to write to you that was not about a roof."

"I know," I said. "You signed yourself with an initial."

"I did."

"You have signed yourself with an initial in nine letters and you have never once written your own name to me."

He stood there with his hands on my arms.

"It is on the deeds," he said. "It is on a thousand deeds and it is on the door of an office I no longer have, and it is a name that has been written by a clerk under a paper for nineteen years, and I did not think it was a thing a woman would want at the bottom of a letter."

"It is the only thing I have ever wanted at the bottom of a letter," I said, "and I did not know it until I said it out loud in a street in Barrowfield to nobody at all."

Then he kissed me.

***

I mean to write down what happened after that and I mean to write it plainly, because everything else in this book has been written plainly and it would be a poor thing to go coy at the end.

We went upstairs and I do not remember the stair.

Nobody tells you this about a marriage: a bed is not a place where a thing begins. It is a place where a thing that has already begun is allowed to go on. In October at Thorneley I had begun it because I was going to do a thing the next day that I could not do with a husband in the room. In Barrowfield there had been a chair and a bed and twelve days and a man who put his hand on my back and went to sleep.

And here, in a house at the end of a lane with the snow coming down outside and the fire downstairs and the soup going cold on the table, there was nothing to begin and nothing to prove and nothing to be got out of the way of.

He undressed me and I let him, and then I undressed him because I wanted to see him, and he stood there in the middle of the room and let me look, which is a harder thing for a man than it sounds.

"You are thinner," I said.

"Eleven weeks of a cloth on a table at four o'clock."

"You are thinner and your hands are cold and you have been sleeping badly."

"I have."

"Come here."

There is a thing I remember about his hands. They are not a labourer's hands and they are not a gentleman's hands; they are the hands of a man who has spent nineteen years holding a pen and turning the pages of other people's books, and there is a callus on the middle finger of the right hand and nowhere else, and when he put his hands on me I could feel exactly where it was.

He went slowly. He has always gone slowly, and in October I had thought it was because he is a careful man, and I have since decided that it is because he is a man who does not believe that a thing is real until it has been looked at.

I put my hand flat on his chest, the way I have gone through the world since I was nine years old, one hand on a thing and then the thing tells me what has been done to it. Under my palm he was warm and he was going like a hammer, and I took my hand away. I could have read him the way I read a ledger — one entry at a time, and known every column of him. I did not. A living man is still being written, and to read him would have been to close the book before the ink was dry, and I would rather have had him in the room than in the record.

"Mercy," he said, and it was a question.

"I am here," I said. "I am not going anywhere."

He put his hands on either side of my face and looked at me the way he looks at a column of figures before he sets his name under it, and then he kissed me, not the way he had kissed me downstairs, but the way a man kisses a thing he means to keep.

I have spent my life being looked at by men who wanted something from me — a signature, a statement, a confession — and I have spent my life keeping my face still so they could not read it. He did not want my face still. He wanted it the other way, and it was, and I could not have stopped it if I had tried.

"Tell me what you want," he said, against my mouth.

"Now?"

"Now."

"I want your hands on me," I said, "and I do not want you to stop, and I want to be the one who says when."

"Then say when."

He went to his knees beside the bed and put his mouth where my hand had been, on the side of my throat, and I stopped being able to hold myself upright and he caught me and laid me back, which is a thing he has done before and will do again and I have decided to let him.

There is a thing about being undressed by a man who does it like a man counting — one entry at a time, and nothing skipped, and nothing taken that was not offered. He did not hurry it. He put his mouth on the inside of my wrist where the pulse is, and then higher, and I said his name, and he said "I have it," which is a foolish thing to say and which I have not stopped thinking about.

The bed has a loose board under the foot and it knocked. The snow had stopped. Downstairs the fire went down to embers and the colour in the room went from gold to grey to the colour of a thing remembered, and I could see him only by the shape of him against the window, and that was enough.

I got my hands on his back and then could not keep them anywhere, and he was breathing against my ear and said nothing at all, and I understood that he was working, in the way he works, and that the work was me. He came over me with his weight on his elbows, which is a courtesy of his that I have never once mentioned to him, and he waited, because he is a man who does not believe a thing is real until it has been looked at, and he was looking.

I put my heel in the back of his knee and told him to move, and he moved, and the loose board knocked again, and I laughed because I could not help it, and he stopped and looked at me with a question in his face, and I said "do not you dare stop," and he did not.

There is no accounting for what came after. I have kept an account of every pound and every day in this book and I can tell you the price of a ton of wet coal in any year since 1821, and I could not have told you how long that took, and at the end I got my hand on the back of his neck and held it there, and he came down onto his forearms and looked at me, and there was nothing in his face that I have seen on any other man, and I do not know what it was called but it was mine, and it is still mine.

"Say when," he said, and his voice was not steady, and I said "not yet" and then "now," and then something that was not a word in any language, and he kept his word about that too.

Afterwards we lay in the dark and did not say anything for a while.

"Mercy."

"Go on."

"There is a question," he said, "and I am going to ask it now, and I have not asked it for sixteen weeks on purpose."

"Ask it."

"What do you want?"

I lay in the dark against his shoulder and did not answer at once, and the snow came on the window, and the clock downstairs went nine, and I understood that a woman can be asked a question by two people in one fortnight and that the second time is the one that counts.

I thought about saying the true things that are easy to say. I want to sleep for a week. I want the window at the top of the stair mended. I want to be a woman with a book and a table and a husband who signs his own name.

All of those were true and none of them was the answer.

"I want to find out what that book is," I said.

"The Farrow book."

"The register. Three hundred and sixty-six entries and seven blanks and nine numbers in a Shire Hall and a line in a lawyer's book in Barrowfield that says *and one not entered.*" I turned my head. "I do not want to be the woman who found it and handed it to a man in a wig. I want to be the one who finds out."

He did not say anything for a moment.

"And what do you want to be when you have found out?"

"Old," I said. "Doing something. Not a woman with a lawsuit."

He laughed, once, in the dark.

"Then that is what we will do," he said. "We will find out. And it will cost money and it will take years and you will be a hundred and eighteen miles from home for months at a time and I will be here with a cloth on the table and I am not going to complain about it, and I want that understood now, tonight, before we start, because I am not going to be a man who agrees in February and complains in June."

"Say why."

"Because I have been in a room in Barrowfield for twelve days," he said, "and I watched a girl of twenty-one stand up in a market hall and tell a hundred and twenty people a true thing that nobody wanted told, and I have been an auditor for nine years and I have never once done that, and I am not going to spend the rest of my life being the husband of a woman who does."

***

The third letter came on the eleventh of February.

It came up from the inn with the rest and Mrs. Trewin brought it in with the bread and said, "This one has no county on it."

It was addressed to me in a hand I did not know.

*Mrs. Rooke,*
*Nether Collow,*
*by Thorneley.*

That is all. No county. No direction beyond the village.

I turned it over. The seal was a wafer and there was nothing on it and the paper was good and the postmark was not a postmark I recognised, and when I asked the postmaster at Collow about it in March he said it had come up with the mail from the south and there was nothing on it to say where it had started.

I opened it at the table with Rooke in the room.

It was one page and it was not signed.

*The register you have was begun in 1806 and it is one of four. The other three were kept by men who are dead and their books are not where they left them.*

*A blank in a register is not a thing taken out. It is a place kept.*

*Two hundred and four is not an office. It is a house. The office is what is written against the number.*

*You have read a coat and a cuff and a pen and a covenant. You have not yet read a place. When you do, do not do it alone, and do not do it in the winter, and do not do it in a house where somebody is expected.*

*You will know when. The book will tell you.*

I read it four times and then I put it down and looked at Rooke.

"There is no signature," he said.

"There is no signature."

He picked it up and read it and put it down.

"Four," he said. "One of four. And ours is one."

"Yes."

"And whoever wrote this knows you have it."

I sat with my hands flat on the cloth.

"Yes," I said. "And he knows I read a coat in Collow and a pen in Barrowfield. He knows what I can do. He knew it before I did."

The fire moved in the grate.

"Mercy," Rooke said. "What is the third book?"

I looked at the page.

"I do not know," I said. "But it says a blank is a place kept, and there are seven blanks in that register, and I have been calling them erasures for six weeks."

I got up and went to the shelf and took down the quarto in the reversed calf and put it on the table and opened it at the front and read the title out loud, the way I do, because a thing said out loud is a thing you cannot pretend you have not read. *Offices of the Nineteenth Class,* and then the words I had carried about with me since November without knowing what they were for: *held in the person, not capable of deputation.*

Then I turned to the first blank.

"Seven places," I said, "kept in a book by a man who was frightened, and a letter with no county on it, and a man who knows what I did in a room in Collow before I had decided to do it."

I put my hand flat on the page.

And then I took it off, because I had a husband at the table and soup going cold and a whole life in front of me, and a woman who reads a book at a table in February with the snow coming down is a woman who has decided to start.

"We will begin in the spring," I said.

Rooke closed the book.

"In the spring," he said. "Not in the winter, and not alone."

"Not alone," I said, and it was the first promise I had ever made to a living person that I intended to keep.

---

## After the End · A Note for the Reader

Thank you for reading. A few questions, none of which require you to
remember a single figure:

1. Where did you stop reading, even for a moment? What was happening on
   that page?
2. The rules of the reading are given in full the first time and never
   explained twice. Did you always know what she was doing, and what it
   cost her? Was there a place you had to go back?
3. Chapters four, twelve and thirteen are ledgers and registers. Did the
   paper hold you, or did you begin to skip? Be honest with me here; this
   is the thing I am least sure of.
4. How did you find the distance between them - the nine months of
   letters, the twelve days at Christmas? Too slow, too fast, or right?
5. Which character would you follow into a second book, and which would
   you be glad to leave behind?
6. If you described this book to a friend in one sentence, what would the
   sentence be?
7. Did the people sound like different people? Judith, Sowerby, Tarrant
   and Rooke each answer a question their own way; did any of them blur
   into another, or into the narrator?

Reply in any form - a list, a rant, notes read off the back of an
envelope. All of it helps.
